#7050. Mark Rober's Class CrunchLabs Courses Now Available for Free on YouTube

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

Designed in partnership with the National Science Teaching Association, the standards-aligned "Carnival of Collisions: Contact Forces" and "Mission MagLev: Electric & Magentic Forces" courses offer free video lessons, guided activities, and supplemental teaching resources for grades 6-8.

Class CrunchLabs was launched last year to offer free hands-on science curriculum resources including teaching guides, classroom slides, student readings and handouts, assessments, and instructional videos featuring Rober alongside "Science Bob" Pflugfelder, celebrity guests, and student co-hosts. The initiative was backed by a $7.2 million grant to create the CrunchLabs.org Foundation, a nonprofit "dedicated to revolutionizing science education in a way that promotes critical thinking and inspires lasting curiosity and confidence in STEM," according to a news announcement at the time.

The organization plans to roll out additional courses on YouTube over time, with the ultimate goal of posting more than 1,000 science videos in 34 languages.

Check out the Class CrunchLabs YouTube channel here.

#7047. This Colorful California Mountain Home Gives Classic Ski House Style a Fresh New Look

Using the surrounding landscape as palette inspiration, designer Alden Fletcher brought 70s retro flair to this family retreat.

When most people think of a classic ski chalet, they picture log-lined walls, dark corners, a raging hearth, plenty of plaid, and maybe even a taxidermied deer or two. But a certain kind of magic strikes when high-altitude architecture hits the right notes, and this Truckee, California, retreat is a masterclass in making the mountains sing.

Built almost 20 years ago, this family holiday home boasted all the 90s mountain classics: an abundance of knotty pine, traditional slate floors, and a minimalist beige palette. Undoubtedly stylish for the time, the retreat now felt out of step with the Bay Area family looking to make it into a personalized winter escape. The owners knew updates were imminent (necessary, even), but they feared losing the home’s classic California soul. Fighting modernism’s white-box aesthetic, they called in Alden Fletcher of the eponymous San Francisco-based design firm to meld the home’s retro architecture with a fresh, forest-inspired brief.

Noting the owner’s concerns and dedication to sustainability, the project’s goal was not to remodel for grandeur, but to restore the home’s charm. Fletcher’s task was to design around storied memories, the family’s obvious love for winter sports, and their passion for entertaining—all while combining the crisp aesthetic of the alpine wilderness with cozy textiles and natural details to create the ultimate mountain sanctuary. By pulling saturated hues from the surrounding landscape and sourcing perky patterns inspired by the local culture, the redesign breathes new life into the slope-side abode without sacrificing sentiment.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Alden Fletcher Interiors Stylist: Beth Protass Location: Truckee, CAThe Space: A five-bedroom, four-bathroom home across 3,000 square feet

The home's hallways set the tone for the entire design scheme. On the first floor, just beyond the main door, wall-mounted ski runners double as decor. Upstairs, Fletcher redefined the boundaries of the primary suite to carve out a nook for a vignette displaying the home’s most bespoke decor piece: a vintage cherry-red chairlift. “The home’s location is unique,” adds Fletcher. “It sits on a ski slope, and you can view chairlifts out the back windows!”

A true gathering space, the living room was designed around the original stone-faced fireplace, though Fletcher did replace the former pine mantel with white oak, connecting the floor details with the exposed oak beams running along the ceiling.

Swiveling burnt orange armchairs accompany an inky indigo sectional. Both gather around a button-tufted ottoman, which doubles as a coffee perch or game table. Shaded sconces flank the fireplace to give the room a glow, even when the hearth is unlit, while a pair of vintage ski shoes hang on the wall, crowning the design.

In the playroom, the deep green hue of the wallpaper paired with the natural texture of knotty pine borrows inspiration from the forest-like landscape just beyond the window. Vertical lines carried through both the wallpaper and the wainscoting create the illusion of height, while playful ski motifs bring the local sport’s culture into the play space.

“The clients are sustainably minded and didn't want to get rid of what was working,” says Fletcher. With conservation in mind, she opted to keep the original perimeter cabinets, refreshing them with paint, new hardware, and Virginia Black Satin Granite countertops, a sturdier alternative to show-stopping soapstone.

To optimize the space, she nixed the two existing islands, reimagining the room to fit a larger iteration that boasts a sink and easily seats five. The design team then added upper cabinets, transforming the space into a storage haven. Later, she placed a library ladder and golden hardware for easy access and a touch of whimsy. Then, she turned her attention to making sure the lighting was up to par. Fletcher firmly believes a beautiful space should be equally as functional. To ensure no detail fell short, she installed discreet recessed lights alongside under-cabinet lighting, and two large glass pendants to adorn the space. All dimmable for ambiance—of course. “There’s nothing worse than a home kitchen that is just flooded with bright light everywhere. A kitchen should still feel cozy and warm, and good lighting is the key to accomplishing that,” says Fletcher.

Fletcher compensated for this former powder room’s lack of windows with vibrant wallpaper to energize the space and make the most of interior lighting. Borrowing space from an adjacent closet, she converted this former two-piece bath into a full retreat with a shower, vanity, and storage, finishing the space with gold hardware for an additional touch of luxury.

Perhaps the most customized room in the home, the mudroom boasts bold color and smart storage solutions for a space as functional as it is chic. The custom cabinet is an ode to smart design, featuring bench seating, pegs, an overhead shelf, and under-seat shoe storage—but the real showstopper is its bold red hue.

“My favorite element of the built-in is the color. The brick color has just the right amount of earthiness,” says Fletcher.

The family loves to entertain, often hosting large groups of friends and family. To accommodate their social lifestyle, Fletcher designed a hospitality hub. Removing a bathroom door, she altered the room’s layout, allowing space for this guest suite to come to life.

With the new dimensions secured, she covered the walls in printed wallpaper inspired by the great local outdoors. The bold pattern set the tone for the rest of the design as she pulled colors, patterns, and themes to complement it.

For the primary bedroom, the theme was warmth: a warm bed, warm home, warm soul, all channeled through muted terracotta walls. But before it could truly transform, the team had to iron out a few oddball details.

“This room used to have a few odd details, including transom windows to the adjoining bathroom and an exterior door to a deck. We removed those elements, which allowed for a better furniture layout and a cozier feeling,” says Fletcher.

The room features a traditional four-poster bed in a nearly black finish, which carries over to the lighting details and furniture hardware, bringing out the warmth and depth of the surrounding rich red hues.

For the primary bath, the team capitalized on the luxury of the sloped-side views, framing a classic 400-pound slipper tub with a nearly floor-to-ceiling window featuring vintage pine trim. Truly the focal point of the design, Fletcher finished the window treatment with woven shades to tie in the natural wood details of the home and stand up to moisture. Natural grain runs through the room’s doors and vanity, topped with soft white Calacatta marble, channeling the classic ski chalet aesthetic. Gold fixtures extend from the vanity to the lighting, towel rack, and tub, for a touch of glamour.

Boasting the project’s biggest splurge, a custom bunk-trundle bed, the kid’s room combines playful hues with forest-inspired details and maximum sleepover potential. “We wanted to maximize sleeping spots in this home,” Fletcher explains. “The Casa Kids bunk bed in the kid's room was a splurge, but it can sleep 5!”

Alden Fletcher first fell in love with design while working as an art teacher and refurbishing furniture in her downtime. In 2020, she grew that passion into a full-service design firm focused on bringing functionality to storied designs and creating ultra-personalized interiors.

#7044. OpenAI Getting Rid of Custom GPTs and Moving to Plugin Architecture

OpenAI has announced it will retire Custom GPTs and move the specialized workflows built around them to a new plugin architecture spanning ChatGPT and its Codex coding agent.

Custom GPT retirement is currently scheduled for Dec. 11, 2026, although the company says timing and migration availability can vary by account and workspace.

For affected Enterprise workspaces, OpenAI's current migration schedule targets Oct. 26 for ending creation of new Custom GPTs. Qualifying Enterprise customers with approved deferrals can continue using them until Feb. 11, 2027.

OpenAI has changed its UI to make GPTs less prominent, and when users choose to try creating a new Custom GPT, they are now prompted with an option to create a plugin instead.

Rather than maintaining separate customized versions of ChatGPT, OpenAI is shifting customization toward plugins that can package reusable instructions, reference material, and connections to outside services.

Under OpenAI's plugin architecture, a plugin can include one or more skills containing instructions and resources for repeatable workflows, a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server exposing tools and external systems, or both. ChatGPT and Codex share a universal plugin directory, although some capabilities can remain specific to a particular environment.

For organizations, that makes the change more than a simple renaming exercise. A company could package recurring workflows into skills while using MCP servers or connected apps to give those workflows access to enterprise systems and data.

OpenAI says creators will be able to migrate an existing GPT through the My GPTs interface. The GPT's instructions become a skill in the replacement plugin, while knowledge files are copied into plugin reference files and connected apps can be carried over.

Not everything transfers automatically. Existing GPT conversations do not migrate, the selected model does not carry over, and Custom Actions may have to be rebuilt using an available app or a custom MCP server.

Sharing also changes. Migrated plugins start private, existing GPT users do not automatically receive access to the replacement, and prior sharing settings do not carry over.

OpenAI is also cautioning organizations against assuming that migration will preserve behavior exactly. "A migrated plugin may respond differently," the company says, recommending that users retest familiar prompts, reference-material use, required output formats, and integrations before relying on the replacement.

That warning has contributed to pushback from some GPT builders, who argue that plugins do not fully reproduce the dedicated and predictable environment of a Custom GPT. Other complaints have focused on sharing, migration complexity, and differences in how migrated workflows use instructions and reference material.

Despite those concerns, OpenAI is clearly consolidating around plugins as its common mechanism for extending its AI products. Its developer documentation now describes plugins as packages users can "discover, install, share, and publish" across ChatGPT and Codex, replacing the separate-custom-assistant model with reusable AI capabilities that can be combined with enterprise tools and services.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

#7040. AI Ambitions Are Running Into a Network Infrastructure Trust Problem

Enterprise ambitions around AI are running into a fundamental problem: Orgnanizations aren't always confident in the networks those projects depend on, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

The research found that concerns about network providers have caused organizations to rethink strategic initiatives over the past two years. Among respondents whose initiatives were affected, AI and data-driven projects were the most cited, at 49%. That put AI ahead of security and compliance programs at 44%, digital transformation at 42%, and cloud migration at 38%.

The findings come from Arelion's report, "The Trust Illusion: Why AI and Digital Transformation Need More Than a Reliable Network," based on a survey of 518 senior enterprise network decision-makers in the U.S., U.K., Germany, and France conducted by Savanta.

All respondents worked for organizations with more than 2,000 employees and influenced network strategy across data centers, cloud, and connectivity. Of those surveyed, 66% had final sign-off responsibility. The results suggest that as organizations pursue increasingly data-intensive AI projects, confidence in the network infrastructure supporting them could become another deployment obstacle.

On the surface, Arelion found little evidence of a trust crisis. A total of 93% of respondents said they largely or completely trust their current network provider, while 83% said that trust has strengthened over time.

The picture changes when respondents are asked about a serious network incident. Only 15% said they have complete confidence in their provider's ability to quickly detect and address such a problem. Another 42% said their provider seriously lets them down anywhere from a few times a year to monthly or more frequently.

Those concerns have consequences beyond the network itself. Responding organizations have delayed, scaled back, or stopped strategic initiatives because of provider trust concerns, while others have added resiliency or risk-mitigation measures.

AI and data projects were the most affected among respondents reporting an impact, as organizations try to move AI from experimentation into larger production deployments.

A serious provider failure could also have substantial consequences. Forty percent of respondents said such an event would cause major operational disruption, while 18% anticipated significant financial loss or severe financial or reputational damage.

Another tension runs through Arelion's findings. Enterprises need networks they can trust to support AI projects, but many of the same decision-makers are concerned about AI's growing role inside those networks. A total of 54% of respondents identified AI and automation in network management and security as the factors most likely to negatively affect network trust over the next three years. That ranked ahead of data sovereignty expectations and geopolitical instability, both at 39%, and regulatory requirements and quantum computing threats to current encryption standards, both at 38%.

At the same time, respondents aren't rejecting AI as a network technology. Proactive AI-driven fault prediction was among the capabilities they said would matter most to future trust, alongside stronger built-in security, greater accountability, and responsive support. That leaves enterprises in an unusual position: AI is increasing their dependence on digital infrastructure while also raising questions about how that infrastructure itself is managed.

Arelion has an interest in emphasizing the importance of network-provider trust, and the survey reflects the views specifically of network decision-makers at large enterprises. Still, the findings point to an infrastructure consideration that can get lost amid the rush to adopt new AI models and agents. AI projects ultimately depend on the networks, cloud environments, data centers, and connectivity beneath them. If organizations lack confidence in that foundation, access to better AI models alone won't remove the obstacle.

"Trust is influencing which strategic initiatives move forward, how organizations manage risk, and which providers they choose to work with," said Mattias Fridström, Vice President at Arelion. "As businesses become increasingly reliant on digital infrastructure, enterprises are looking for network providers that can demonstrate transparency, accountability, and operational excellence, not just availability."

The full report is available for download here at the Arelion site (registration required)

#7036. Research: Lack of Specialized Infrastructure Is a Top AI Adoption Challenge

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research Digital Realty. Forty percent of survey respondents cited a lack of specialized infrastructure required by AI as a main challenge to adopting a formal AI strategy, up from 9% in the company's 2024 research.

The Global Data Insights Survey 2026 points to a widening mix of infrastructure models behind enterprise AI. Half of respondents host AI models in private clouds, 86% are pursuing or exploring sovereign AI, 92% tie data-location strategy to their AI plans, and 88% follow a distributed-data approach.

The research is based on an online survey of 2,131 IT decision-makers across 19 countries, and 11 industries, conducted from Nov. 23, 2025, through Feb. 4, 2026.

Organizations also appear to be shifting from AI planning toward execution. The share actively executing AI to achieve operational efficiency or reduce costs rose from 27% in 2024 to 37% this year. Only 3% reported no measurable ROI to date, while 63% expect returns within the next six months to two years.

Specialized infrastructure led the survey's list of AI adoption challenges at 40%, followed by regulatory and personal-data concerns at 35%. Data availability and quality, storage capacity, interconnection, compute resources, and staffing also ranked as significant constraints.

Those requirements are also shaping workload placement. Integration with existing IT or data infrastructure ranked first at 43%, while regulatory compliance and performance were each cited by 37%. Data proximity, sovereignty, sustainability, cost, and intellectual property protection also factored into hosting decisions.

Private cloud features prominently across the report's AI hosting categories, but enterprises are clearly using a mix of private cloud, public cloud, on-premises infrastructure, and colocation. Private cloud accounted for 50% of custom-built AI model hosting and 47% for open source or fine-tuned large language models (LLMs).

Sovereignty is another part of that placement decision. Thirty-two percent consider sovereign AI — in which data and AI models remain within a single national or regulatory jurisdiction — a requirement, while another 54% are exploring it. The findings point toward hybrid environments in which infrastructure location, governance and data control increasingly overlap.

#7030. OpenAI Intros Dots, AI Agents that Continue Working Between User Conversations

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

OpenAI is also previewing specialist Dots for organizations, with separate identities and credentials for defined responsibilities. Those enterprise deployments are beginning with focused pilots. The company is working with Microsoft on integration with Agent 365's governance and security controls.

The broader ambition seems to be to make delegation last beyond a chat session. That change could reduce the need to repeatedly explain a project. It also makes supervision a continuing obligation: An instruction that was appropriate yesterday may no longer fit today's circumstances.

Users currently cannot inspect or directly edit individual Dot memories. Deleting a Dot deletes its own context, while files and conversations it created remain separately stored. Plugin permissions are shared across Dots, ChatGPT, ChatGPT Work, and Codex.

For an organization, that creates two related governance questions: what an agent can retrieve now, and what it already knows. Revoking a connection addresses the first without necessarily resolving the second.

OpenAI's safety account distinguishes authorized tasks that continue in the background from unsolicited "proactive research." The latter uses tools restricted to reading permitted sources. Those research tasks cannot directly send messages, change connected application content, or control a browser or desktop. Any subsequent action must pass the usual rules and checks.

A separate system, Auto-review, checks certain planned actions against instructions and safety requirements. OpenAI says the agent cannot modify the controls enforcing those checks.

"Dots can still make mistakes," the company cautions.

OpenAI's Dots system-card appendix acknowledges that persistence and multi-agent systems are not new. Its evaluations examine how continuing work affects authorization boundaries.

In one test involving successive related tasks, moderate scope violations increased from 8.6% to 19.7% when the number of intervening tasks doubled from five to 10. Examples included carrying information between unrelated tasks or editing a shared document beyond the intended scope. The company reported no severe breaches or exfiltration in that evaluation.

These are controlled test results, not measured failure rates in customer deployments. They nevertheless identify a problem that a successful demonstration cannot settle: whether an agent keeps the right boundaries as its workload accumulates.

According to OpenAI's DevDay recap, Dots are available to Pro and Business Premium users in eligible markets. Enterprise, Edu, and Healthcare access is an administrator-enabled beta, off by default.

The operational question is how organizations will keep delegated authority current. A continuing assignment needs a human owner who can revise its scope and decide when to stop it. Otherwise, the convenience of leaving work with an agent can outlast the reason it was authorized.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

#7028. 10 Stylish Sofa Covers to Totally Refresh Your Living Room Before the Holidays

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Hide stains, protect upholstery, and add instant polish.

Life at home gets messy with its regular residents, and it gets even crazier when guests come over. Hosting season demands the most of our couches, as they become extra seats for family dinner or makeshift beds for overnight stays. It’s not always feasible to swap out your sofa just because the upholstery has seen better days. That’s where the mighty sofa cover comes in.

Whether you opt for a tightly-fitted silhouette or a looser, blanket-style slipcover, a high-quality sofa cover can totally refresh your space. Just think about it: a dated, yellowing couch suddenly looks fresh and brand new. You can even go bold and experiment with patterned options, without the commitment of buying a whole new piece. Pro tip: patterns are also a great way to hide stains.

There are hundreds of sofa cover options available, so below, our shopping team did the hard part for you. We hunted down nine options for different types of couches, including an editor-tested and approved option. Plus, we chatted with interior designer Tiffany Leigh for tips on styling sofa covers, keeping them clean, and more.

The fabric is extra soft, so you feel like you're sitting on a high-end sofa at a fraction of the price. The stretchy jacquard fabric is leakproof and fits snug to your cushions and frame.

Want to add trendy bouclé upholstery to your home but are hesitant to commit to it 24/7? This slipcover offers the best of both worlds. The fitted fabric makes it appear as though you bought the sofa in this style, but you can easily swap it out when you want a different look or feel. It's all in one piece for your sofa's frame, so you can still add the throw pillows of your choice.

Material: Polyester-spandex | Colors: One | Care: Machine washable

This couch cover is basically your favorite waffle blanket, but it covers the entire sofa. The linen is stonewashed, so it's already soft from the first time you sit on it. We love the neutral and earthy tones it's available in, especially this cinnamon color. If you're unsure whether you'll love it, take advantage of the 30-day trial period.

Material: Cotton-linen blend | Colors: Two | Care: Machine washable

The stretchy spandex of this sofa cover makes it a great choice for an inexpensive, colorful refresh. The textured jacquard fabric comes in seven sizes and a wide range of 30 colors, so you're sure to find something that suits your sofa and style. It's also machine washable, so feel free to enjoy a few wine nights on your sofa, especially if you opt for one of the darker colors.

Material: Polyester-spandex | Colors: 30 | Care: Machine washable

For a much more casual look (and application process), this geometrical sofa cover fits like a big textured blanket, so it'll avoid pooling on one end of the sofa. Plus, it can be used for several purposes: it's a great picnic blanket whenever you decide to change up the look of your sofa. It's bordered with adorable tassels, and the chenille fabric is also durable enough to withstand your furry friends.

Material: Chenille | Colors: 48 | Care: Machine washable

If the idea of investing a small fortune in a white couch sounds like a risky idea, opt for a white sofa slipcover instead. This blanket-like cover is oversized for a luxurious, draping look, and it's available in two light linen shades. It will give your living room that coveted clean, modern feel without triggering your anxiety. The best part? This one is machine washable, so anytime you notice a mark or stain, simply pop it in the wash, and it will be good as new.

Material: Chenille and linen | Colors: Two | Care: Machine washable

If you want something that'll truly protect your sofa from the wrath of your pets, Pottery Barn's pet furniture throw is a no-brainer. Available in three sizes, this lucky find can also be customized in a variety of fabrics, including tweed, bouclé, and chenille, among others. Of course, they're all performance-grade.

Material: Basketweave, Linen, Boucle, Velvet, and more | Colors: 95 | Care: Depends on the fabric material you chose

The size of your sofa cover will depend not only on the size of the sofa or couch you want to protect, but also on the properties of the fabric itself. Leigh says that with a stretchy fabric, you may want a cover with measurements slightly smaller than the dimensions of your sofa for a tighter, more fitted look. For sofa covers with corner ties that allow you to tighten or tie bows, the fabric may have a bit less give, so you'll want to allow for a little extra room in the cover size.

Some styles lend themselves to being covered more effectively than others. Leigh says that sofas with thinner or rolled arms tend to look nicer with a slipcover than those without. You can also choose between having a skirt on your sofa cover or showing off the legs of your furniture, depending on your aesthetic preference. “I like when they go right to the floor because a skirt cover is a much more traditional, covered look,” Leigh says. “If you have a little bit more of a modern style, having a slipcover that stops at the legs can be a fresher, kind of more updated take.”

Natural fabrics, such as chenille or linen, are typically a great choice when selecting a sofa cover fabric. For family rooms or homes with pets, indoor/outdoor fabrics like Crypton that have been treated to repel dirt and debris are safer bets for those wanting something stain-resistant. The stretchiness of the fabric also plays a role in selecting a sofa cover. "If you wanted something a little bit more durable, canvas or cotton would be a really great material on there," Leigh says.

It's always a good idea to pay attention to the care instructions for whatever cover you buy, as fabric care really depends on the individual material. For example, a heavier cotton material can likely withstand more washing. “I would say only wash when you really need to, just like with a really nice sweater,” Leigh says. “Don't wash it every time you wear it because washing breaks down fibers. So, it could reduce the longevity of your sofa cover. Most are designed to be removed and washed. So if and when you feel like 'oh, you know what, it's looking a little bit dingy, it's time,' that's the time to do it.” Additionally, it's essential to consider the size of your cover, as larger ones may necessitate the use of a commercial washing machine.

Additionally, the cover may wrinkle over time, especially with linen, which softens with each wash. “Linen does get a little bit wrinkly, but I think that's kind of the charm of a slipcovered sofa, having that kind of rumpled, you know, 'someone just took a nap here' look,” Leigh says.

“An oatmeal linen slipcover is that very quintessential, relaxed coastal style that you see a lot of slipcovers used in, and I think it's a classic choice that you'll never go wrong with,” Leigh says. Don't run away from patterns either, though—Leigh is seeing a rise in patterned upholstery in the design industry, and it's a chance to push the envelope and have fun with your design choices. “A cover can be a great way to, you know, try out a pattern without necessarily committing to it forever, because if in five years you say, ‘okay, this pattern was fun, but I'm ready for something different,’ you can just change the slipcover as opposed to having to get an all-new sofa,” Leigh says.

At House Beautiful, we are experts in reviewing and buying anything for the home, from mattresses for back pain to reclining sofas. For this story in particular, we asked our staff members to share their favorite sofa cover (if they own one) and spent hours combing the internet, reading hundreds of reviews about the best ones currently available on the market. While we haven't personally tested every product on this list, we've thoroughly outlined the pros and cons of each sofa cover to provide you with all the necessary details before you make a purchase.

Since 2019, they have designed approachable and livable interiors that feel welcoming and elevated, focusing on quality craftsmanship, emotional connection, and natural elements.

#7026. Survey Identifies Data Governance Enforcement Gap for Agentic AI Tools

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time. Perhaps more worrying is that only 19% said their organization could detect in real time when an agent last accessed data outside its intended scope.

That disconnect is at the center of Delinea's fall 2026 Identity Security Report, "The AI Enforcement Gap," which suggests companies have made quick progress establishing AI governance but have not necessarily built the technical controls needed to enforce it.

The findings come from a pair of surveys covering 2,254 IT and security leaders and 2,250 non-IT employees at organizations with at least 500 employees across the U.S, U.K, Germany, Australia, Singapore, UAE, France, and India.

All the organizations were using or piloting AI, while the employees surveyed used at least one AI tool for work.

The problem becomes more consequential as companies move from AI tools that primarily respond to users toward agents capable of taking actions on their behalf.

Eighty-seven percent of IT and security respondents said their organization had experienced or suspected of an incident during the past year in which an AI tool or agent accessed sensitive information beyond what was required for its task.

Delinea's research also suggests companies are giving agents access that can persist longer than necessary. Only 58% of IT and security leaders said they have mechanisms that automatically revoke or expire AI access when a session ends, and those controls do not necessarily cover every agent or tool. Forty-two percent said many agent credentials remain active until an audit.

Agents also are not always receiving their own narrowly defined permissions. Half of IT leaders said their organizations use the employee's existing permissions as a boundary for what an agent can access. That can effectively hand an agent years of accumulated access belonging to the person who launched it.

That is particularly significant because an AI agent does not necessarily behave like a traditional service account. A service account typically performs a predefined process, while an agent can choose different tools, actions and sequences at runtime depending on the situation.

"Service accounts run fixed scripts," said Mike Albrecht, associate director in the Risk Advisory Practice at Cross Country Consulting. "An AI agent decides what actions it's going to take at runtime."

The enforcement problem is not limited to autonomous agents.

The researchers found that 76% of employees surveyed had bypassed formal approval at some point to use AI tools with company data, applications or systems. And 48% said they always or extensively use AI tools without going through formal approval.

Meanwhile, 60% said they had felt pressure to use AI with sensitive or confidential information even when they were not sure doing so was permitted.

Executives were particularly likely to circumvent the process. Eighty-one percent of C-level respondents said they always or regularly bypass AI access approval, compared with 33% of intermediate-level employees.

The result is a widening gap between written AI governance and what companies can see and control. Delinea found that only 41% of IT leaders said all AI tools and agents accessing company data are actively monitored. When an AI tool or agent went outside its intended scope, 55% said it took at least a day to detect the incident, while 30% said detection took four days or longer.

That suggests the next phase of enterprise AI governance may depend less on writing additional rules and more on whether organizations can enforce existing ones while an agent is working.

"Most organizations cannot confidently answer three questions: What agents are running in our environment? What can they access? What have they actually done?" said Delinea CEO Art Gilliland. "An agent can begin a session with legitimate access and drift into something it was never meant to do."

The full report is available for download here.

#7022. Claude Intros Sonnet 5.5 with Model-Switching Cybersecurity Safeguards

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

According to Anthropic's explanation of model switching, automatic fallback is enabled by default in Claude. Users receive a notice, and the answer is labeled with the model that provided it. The chat then stays on Sonnet 5 unless the user changes the selection.

The company identifies penetration testing and exploit generation among activities that may trigger cybersecurity fallbacks. Source-code vulnerability scanning remains supported. Biology and reasoning-extraction safeguards can block requests outright, rather than route them elsewhere.

The distinction matters operationally. A block stops the request; a fallback allows an attempt to continue with a different model. Neither outcome should be treated as interchangeable with completion by the model the user originally chose.

Anthropic says Sonnet 5.5 generates output more than 30% faster than Sonnet 5 and can reduce cost per task by up to 30%, based on its testing. Token prices remain unchanged.

Those claims describe performance under the company's test conditions. An organization evaluating the service also needs to examine what happens when its own assignments encounter safeguards.

An internal pilot could record how often work is interrupted, whether fallback answers are usable, and when users need help. Such measurements would give buyers a more relevant basis for deployment than assuming every task receives the advertised model's capabilities.

Anthropic has announced plans to expand its Cyber Verification Program with tiered access for Sonnet 5.5, Opus 5.5, and Mythos models. Its current program guidance explicitly excludes the two new 5.5 models and says the expansion is forthcoming.

Organizations should distinguish those planned arrangements from access available today.

The broader enterprise issue is accountability. When an AI service changes models during an assignment, someone still needs to determine whether the resulting work is suitable. A visible handoff helps, but organizations must decide how employees should respond to it.

For more information on Sonnet 5.5, go to the Anthropic site.

#7020. Marshals Is Ruining the Dutton Family Legacy

The Yellowstone spinoff wants to move on from its Kevin Costner-sized shadow, but it can’t.

Ever since John Dutton (Kevin Costner) died, the Yellowstone-verse has sought to do away with everything he stood for. Without the patriarch in charge, it’s dawned on his children, Kayce (Luke Grimes) and Beth (Kelly Reilly), that the man was too violent and too stubborn. So, Kayce sold the ranch and resolved to put his violent life behind in the spinoff, Marshals, while Beth moved to Texas in Dutton Ranch to try ranching her own way.

It’s not really working out. Beth and her husband Rip (Cole Hauser) basically just moved the whole Dutton operation to Texas. And as much as Kayce’s plan to turn his last remaining acres of the Yellowstone Ranch into an equine therapy center would follow through on his plan for peace, he’s too busy continuing his father’s cycle of violence by running into danger as a member of a team of U.S. Marshals every week. Why? Well, because peace for the Duttons just isn’t good TV.

Still, Marshals tries its best to weave the framework into season 2. The latest round of episodes to kick off the second season deals heavily with post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD), and the various ways the Marshals team handles their crazy, violent lives. Mostly, it’s by bottling it up and never speaking about it. After killing a man in the season 1 finale, Kayce’s son Tate (Brecken Merrill) says a line in season 2, episode 3 that could have been plucked directly from a Dutton family quote book: “What happened stays where it is.”

But try as they might, the Dutton children are becoming their father anyway. By now in the task force spinoff, Kayce’s likely killed more men on the ranch than his father ever did. And though he’s not branding people like cattle anymore—which still haunts Kayce’s dreams in season 2, episode 4—even John Dutton gave up that practice after Yellowstone season 3.

Most likely, the Marshals team just wrote themselves into a trap. They might think they’re providing Kayce good backstory by tackling his inner quest for a peaceful life, but when he heads to work to kill criminals every morning, you can’t really believe the guy. Is he trying at all? And since he’s clearly not, maybe we should focus on that instead...?

So, at the end of episode 3, Kayce demands that Tate break his family’s generational cycle and take his ass to therapy. “It’s the last place my father would’ve taken me, and the last place I would’ve gone myself,” he says.

Two episodes have passed since that moment, and we haven’t seen Tate actually go yet. Plus, Kayce hasn’t exactly set a good example. Despite ordering his son to open up, Kayce didn’t give us any hints that he’s decided to see a therapist himself. And that equine center? We’ll be lucky if we ever see it until Marshals is over for good.

#7019. This 100-Year-Old Louisiana Home Got a New Plot of Land and a Colorful New Chapter

The expansive property also includes a pool house and carriage house.

Long before it became the backdrop for family dinners, afternoon swims, and spirited tennis matches, this century-old Colonial in Baton Rouge, Louisiana, had already lived a rather extraordinary life. In the late 1920s, the house was dismantled and relocated from Prairieville to its current address, where its distinctive red clay roof tiles—salvaged from Louisiana State University—have presided over a landscape of majestic live oaks ever since. Decades later, a young girl growing up down the street would admire the house, never imagining that one day she’d raise a family of her own beneath that very roof.

When she and her husband finally purchased the property, they knew that turning a beloved neighborhood landmark into a forever home for themselves and their three daughters would require a little imagination—and a lot of careful planning. Enter interior designer Anne Underwood, who was tasked with bringing the historic residence into the present while preserving the quirks that made it so beloved. Over several years, the project grew from a renovation of the original four-bedroom home into a sprawling family compound, complete with a carriage house, pool house, and landscaped grounds designed to make an ordinary weekend feel like a vacation.

Inside, Underwood embraced the home’s traditional bones without letting them stiffen the home (and the family’s) sunny disposition. Lemon-yellow checkerboard floors mingle with antique furnishings (an element Underwood says is a must for creating “a lived-in, collected-over-time feel that I find crucial to making a true home”), hand-painted finishes, and exuberant textiles. A whimsical chandelier welcomes guests into the living room, while a tucked-away banquette has become an unofficial headquarters for homework, puzzles, and art projects.

Outside, the property’s newest additions—including a pool house and carriage house—offer an equally playful proposition. Conceived as the estate’s crown jewel, the pool house features pale wood walls, a colorful handwoven light fixture, and a monumental wood-burning pizza oven. The homeowners’ relatives jokingly dubbed the expanding property their own private resort, a description Underwood happily embraces.

“So much was poured into this project to make it unique, joyful, and really functional for the clients,” says Underwood. “The warmth and care of each detailed layer creates a sense of ease, which is what facilitates the joyfulness that plays out inside and outside these walls.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Anne UnderwoodArchitect: LRK ArchitectsContractor: Bernhardt Normand ConstructionStyling: Lucy BammanLandscape Architect: CARBOLocation: Baton Rouge, LouisianaThe Space: A four-bedroom, three-and-a-half-bathroom main house, with a carriage house and pool house.

With a simpler architectural structure, the pool house was a place where Underwood leaned into the family’s playful energy. “We selected the wall stain to look like the inside of a shell and wanted the furnishings to feel layered, colorful, vibrant—like a vacation but also very durable for wet swimsuits and sweaty tennis players!” she adds.

The first selection for the pool house was also its most exuberant: a 12-light PET Lamp fixture, with each colorful pendant and cord individually customized and positioned beneath the soaring vaulted ceiling. “Its colorful, playful nature was exactly what we wanted and is very effective for balancing light throughout the space with high vaulted ceilings,” says Underwood. Below, a generous sectional upholstered in indoor-outdoor fabric creates an inviting place to retreat after a swim.

With vibrant Moroccan mosaic flooring, blue zellige shower tile, and a vintage wooden mirror, the pool house bathroom could easily belong to a boutique hotel on some far-flung island. Wood siding and thoughtfully chosen antique details lend the space a relaxed, resort-inspired character that feels right at home beside the pool.

Tradition gets a playful twist in the home’s primary gathering space, where a hand-knotted rug and classic upholstered furnishings mingle with an exuberant chandelier by Stray Dog Designs.

“This house lacks a foyer, so we wanted the living room to be welcoming with plenty of seating and layers of color and accessories,” says Underwood. “While this is the more ‘formal’ living space, we selected a bright, whimsical light fixture to let guests know that these homeowners don’t take themselves (or their interiors) too seriously.”

A passageway between the living room and the home’s more casual quarters, the dining room needed to make an impression without feeling overly precious. Schumacher’s Weeping Pine wallpaper wraps the space in pattern, while pale blue trim, upholstered chairs, and vintage sconces sourced from Round Top lend the formal setting an unexpected sense of ease.

With the adjoining breakfast room to consider, Underwood gave the kitchen a quieter disposition. Existing cabinetry received a custom pale green stain, expertly applied by a local faux-finish artist, while paneled appliances and tonal counter stools create a seamless effect. The yellow-and-white checkerboard flooring continues underfoot, connecting the two spaces.

A powder room is as good a place as any to indulge a dramatic streak. “We wanted a bold escape for guests in the powder bath, and this incredible grasscloth wallpaper was the jumping-off point,” says Underwood. She complemented the Aux Abris pattern’s lush, atmospheric tone with a wood-grained antique chest repurposed as a marble-topped vanity. Vintage Murano glass sconces and a striped inlay mirror add a final dose of eccentricity.

In the office, Underwood leaned into the appeal of a well-appointed study, furnishing this hardworking space almost entirely with vintage and antique finds. Custom cabinetry painted in Benjamin Moore’s Templeton Gray conceals printers, files, and other less-than-picturesque necessities, while mohair-upholstered armchairs make the space equally suited to work and contemplation.

As it turns out, the inspiration for the primary suite was waiting just outside the window. “I started with the idea of bringing materials—like the patinated copper on all exterior lanterns and gutters—from the exterior to the interior,” says Underwood. “While the walls are painted a green/blue color reminiscent of the patina of weathered copper, the accents in the room are warm shades of camel and copper.”

A Gustavian antique secretary and dimensional papier-maché art from Gusto complete the room's quietly collected atmosphere.

The generously proportioned soaking tub in the primary bathroom has already lived several lives, first as a favorite bathing spot for the family’s three young daughters, and now as a welcome retreat for their parents. Underwood carried the bedroom’s blue-green palette into the bath, introducing blush-patterned wallpaper, graphic stone tile, and black accents to balance the room’s softer flourishes.

In daughter Emily’s room, Underwood chose to embrace the sloped ceiling, outfitting it with custom bed canopies that create an enveloping, almost storybook atmosphere. Layers of patterned bedding, including bespoke eiderdowns made in the UK, heighten the cozy effect, while chunky upholstered ottomans offer a welcome counterpoint to the room’s more feminine details.

Making sense of the home’s quirky upstairs architecture required some clever spatial maneuvering. Underwood divided one large bathroom into two separate spaces, each with its own dressing vanity. In daughter Olivia’s bath, Salvesen Graham floral wallpaper covers the sloped walls and ceiling, visually softening the room’s unusual angles. “The pattern play in the tile, wallpaper, and fabrics keeps the energy moving throughout the space,” she adds.

In the carriage house, Underwood continued the layered approach with velvet, chenille, and a generously proportioned sectional, creating a retreat where overnight guests can comfortably settle in for an afternoon—or an entire weekend.

The carriage house kitchen offers guests all the comforts of home, with a nostalgic twist. Cabinetry painted Benjamin Moore’s Mistletoe features vintage-inspired vent detailing, while a retro SMEG refrigerator reinforces the room’s charming, slightly old-fashioned character.

Underwood preserved the home’s original red clay roof tiles while refreshing its facade using Benjamin Moore’s White Dove on the siding and Kennebunkport Green on the shutters. An expanded bluestone sidewalk gives the front door prominence while mature live oaks lend the property a sense of permanence that no renovation could replicate.

Anne Underwood is the founder of Underwood Interiors. Known for her thoughtful approach to creating layered, Anne creates livable spaces that balance timeless tradition with a fresh, playful sensibility. With an appreciation for antiques, vintage furnishings, and artisanal craftsmanship, her interiors feel collected over time rather than decorated all at once. Her work celebrates color, pattern, and architectural character, with an emphasis on thoughtful details and everyday functionality. Whether reimagining a historic residence or designing a new space from the ground up, she believes the most successful interiors reflect the personalities of the people who call them home.

#7017. Convergence of AI, Quantum Risks Is Impacting Cybersecurity

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures. The 2026 Thales Data Threat Report: Quantum & AI Trends, based on responses from 3,120 security and IT management professionals across 20 countries, found that 98% are considering how the two technologies affect each other. Thirty percent reported a significant increase in security budgets specifically for AI.

The research, conducted by 451 Research by S&P Global, examines both the risks and potential benefits of the technologies. Fifty-seven percent believe quantum computing will enhance machine learning, while 54% expect it to enable advanced simulations of complex systems. The security findings span data governance, cloud infrastructure, and preparations for quantum-resistant encryption.

Cloud-based storage, cloud-delivered applications, and cloud management infrastructure were the three most frequently cited asset types targeted by attackers, at 35%, 34%, and 32%, respectively. The report connects that exposure to the large datasets required for AI training and the rapid expansion of AI infrastructure.

Organizations are also looking to cloud providers for protection: 67% reported investing in their cloud provider's AI-specific security tools, compared with 63% using an established security provider. Respondents could identify multiple sources of protection, including startups, large language model providers, and internally developed controls.

Encryption coverage remains incomplete. Only 7% of organizations reported encrypting more than 80% of their sensitive cloud data, while 29% reported encrypting more than 60%. The report's chart puts the average share encrypted at 47% in 2026, down from 51% in 2025.

The findings extend concerns raised in coverage of Thales' 2025 cloud security research, when AI security already ranked second to cloud security in spending priorities and 52% of respondents reported that AI security spending was displacing existing security budgets. The latest report again places AI security second, while its historical comparison shows average sensitive cloud data encryption coverage falling from 51% in 2025 to 47% in 2026.

AI security was the second-highest prioritized security spending category overall, behind cloud security. But protecting data ranked much lower among the measures organizations use to judge AI projects: Reducing or managing risks of data loss or noncompliance placed sixth among seven success criteria, at 35%.

Improving customer experience led those measures at 68%, followed by reducing employee toil through greater task automation at 63%. Meanwhile, poor data quality or initial data governance was the leading inhibitor to AI adoption, cited by 65%; security risks from exposed data followed at 61%. Another 51% cited AI initiatives moving too quickly to be properly secured.

Artificial intelligence is raising the stakes of cyber conflict as attackers use the technology to accelerate reconnaissance, uncover vulnerabilities, and launch attacks faster than many security teams can respond, according to a new report from agentic AI security company Kai.

Cybersecurity companies Rubrik and CrowdStrike have announced an expanded identity-security integration designed to move compromised identity environments from threat detection through clean recovery with less manual intervention.

Microsoft has shut down EvilTokens, a cybercrime service that used AI to help attackers break into e-mail accounts, study their victims, and build convincing financial scams.

#7015. Three Clues That Suggest Callum Turner Could Already Be the Next James Bond

The final screen tests are seemingly finished, but we may already have our answer.

It's been over five years since the release of No Time to Die, Daniel Craig's final outing as James Bond, and the speculation surrounding his successor has hit a fever pitch.

Recent reports suggested that the final screen tests have already happened. It's widely thought that Jack Lowden, Callum Turner, and Josh O'Connor are the three actors vying for the role—and it's anyone's guess who will get their hands on the hallowed tux. Heck, if The Hollywood Reporter’s recent dispatch is to be believed, it might even be Robert Pattinson or Nicholas Hoult.

However! You don't have to be a super spy to see that there are some clues pointing to a specific candidate—namely, one Callum Turner. Below, we've broken down the evidence.

Though he's currently filming the remake of Possession alongside Margaret Qualley and in pre-production for The Comebacker, 2027 isn't looking especially busy for Turner, one of Britain’s most in-demand young actors. We can assume that Bond 26 will go into production next year, so a wide-open schedule would prepare Turner for the intense, high-commitment shooting schedule that Amazon will likely stick to.

Turner recently stepped down as a director of Twentytwo Films Ltd., the production company that he founded last year with his now-wife Dua Lipa. Given that the final screen tests also supposedly took place in the last few days, do we think this could be a result of some complicated contract negotiations? Or maybe he's just freeing himself up even more?

This isn’t hard evidence, exactly, but an argument in Turner's favor. One thing that people noted about Daniel Craig when he was first announced as Bond was that he wasn't exactly a blockbuster actor, and his lineup mostly consisted of strong, independent films, really only including one that signaled he'd make a brilliant Bond: Layer Cake.

Craig then, indisputably, went on to be one of the best-loved 007s to date, and we think that Turner's range over the past ten years makes him a pretty chameleonic, versatile candidate for the role. Across the likes of Green Room, Rose of Nevada, and Rosebush Pruning, we've really seen Turner take on all sorts of mystery, thriller, and action films. With Denis Villeneuve at the helm, there's no way that Bond 26 will be a simple spy story, and Turner's ability to take on all kinds of genre and tone makes him the perfect partner for whatever Villeneuve's vision will be.

#7012. SNL’s You Can See Everything Parody with Druski Resurrects a Classic Eddie Murphy Bit

Back in 1984, Eddie Murphy had fun with John Howard Griffin’s 1961 book Black Like Me with a Saturday Night Live sketch titled White Like Me. In it, Murphy caked on makeup to pass as a white man, flipping his world upside down. It’s still pretty funny to watch after all these years, even if putting on makeup to resemble another race for a joke is walking a tightrope. So it’s damn impressive that Saturday Night Live just got away with it again, with Druski.

On the most recent Saturday Night Live, guest star Druski was at the center of one of the night’s best sketches (and maybe the only one worth watching), which was also the inevitable parody of You Can See Everything, titled Dru Can See Everything.

The premise is simple: What if, before Nathan Fielder, Druski first filmed the final days of freedom for Elizabeth Holmes? Cast members Ben Marshall and an incredible Jane Wickline co-star and elevate the sketch from a mildly funny “haha” to “holy shit they’re actually committed here.” Clocking in just four minutes long, the sketch runs through the highlights of You Can See Everything’s first hour: Elizabeth (Wickline) and her partner Billy (Marshall) living at home, cooking eggs, showing off the controversial Theranos machine, and even Billy’s barenaked shower. But the one change is swapping out Fielder for Druski. And it makes a huge difference.

You can see everything (the sketch) below.

While Fielder’s social awkwardness and determination to ensnare a charlatan without falling victim himself is part of the movie’s drama, SNL knows that Druski would just call out Holmes and Evans’s bullshit straight up. No hesitation. “I just want people to see me as an ordinary person. Can you do that, Druski?” asks Wickline’s Elizabeth Holmes.

Says Druski, “Based on what I’ve seen so far, I’mma go ahead and say no. Y’all got some of the weirdest, freakiest vibe I’ve ever seen.” Grabbing the bowl of eggs, he then turns to the camera: “Whatever they said this bitch did, she done did that sh-t.” (Remember the eggs scene is like, ten minutes into the movie.)

Funny as Druski is, the real MVPs are Wickline and Marshall, even if all their jokes are about the size of Elizabeth and Billy’s saucer-pan eyes. The now spoofed-to-death “I’m always real” scene gets its SNL roast too, with Druski asking literally point-blank, “Are you trying to f-ck me?” Then, when Elizabeth shows off Theranos’s supposedly miraculous machine, Druski doesn’t hold back. “Listen white girl, I’mma say this with all love, right? This the most Temu ass piece of sh-t I done ever seen in my life.” Cut to “Billy” and his weird lizard.

(By the way, we know Saturday Night Live’s production design is top-tier, but it’s frankly amazing they were able to build a virtually precise replica of Holmes’s oceanfront rental and recreate the lighting conditions to make the shots look passably real. Remember that they shoot these things in under a week. I demand a behind-the-scenes video of how they pulled this one off.)

The sketch peaks at the end, when Druski emulates Fielder’s method for staging reenactments by putting on White Chicks-style makeup (to better understand Elizabeth, of course). There’s no getting around it: the man looks freaky. Uncanny. He’s like Fiona from Shrek in a J. Crew sweater. Which makes the joke’s reveal a funny jump scare. Post-midterms, it’s honestly awesome that this kind of woke comedy is “back” (if it actually ever left). I’m not smart enough to suss out if whiteface is actually acceptable. I just know Druski and the SNL team killed it this week.

#7010. 27 Creative Ways Designers Hide TVs for a Stylish, Screen-Free Environment

These genius tricks keep it accessible yet out of sight.

For years, many designers and design-loving homeowners have felt that having the TV on display is a major faux pas. Even though most of us have one and use it daily, there’s something unappealing about having a big black box against the wall and breaking up the interior flow. It’s become even more of an eyesore now that analog spaces are becoming more popular.

While one solution is to invest in a TV that can disguise itself as artwork, those come at a high price; the other option is to learn how to hide the TV in a way that doesn’t feel obvious or tacky, and thankfully, designers have a lot of ideas.

We’ve also included plenty of inspiring photos and expert tips to guide you along the way. If your goal is to reduce the visual presence of technology in your home, you’re in the right place. Keep reading to find inventive ways to make any room feel screen-free—at least when the TV isn’t in use.

Of course, you can hide your television the same way you'd hide anything else you wouldn't want on full display at all times: tuck it into a cabinet. At least, that's what designer Kylie Buhagiar at Melbourne, Australia-based Golden did. "In the main living room, the TV is discreetly integrated into a wall-hung metal joinery box, with hinged doors that elegantly stack back to reveal the screen when needed, maintaining a clean and uncluttered aesthetic," Buhagiar says. "This thoughtful design transforms the joinery into a sculptural piece of furniture, allowing the space to effortlessly transition from a main living room to a calm sitting area, enhancing the home's rich yet comfortable atmosphere."

The whole point of concealing the TV is to hide it, but sometimes, the mechanism used to hide the screen just draws more attention to the area. Gillian Segal, an interior designer based in Vancouver, British Columbia, came up with a clever solution that basically looks like the wall. "This room functioned as both a formal living space and informal family room (complete with movie nights and lots of kids' TV shows), so we wanted a design that felt both elegant and approachable," Segal explains. "We concealed the TV behind a very simple millwork door system that folds open and closed. It was a cost-effective solution that allowed other elements in the room to shine."

It’s no secret that many designers find the Samsung Frame TV to be beautiful, but designer Ariel Okin takes it a step further by adding a literal frame to the Frame. This hidden-TV tactic ensures that it matches other elements in the space, such as the fireplace screen, thus hiding it in plain sight.

If you don’t want to think about your TV when it’s off, you can cleverly camouflage it. In this living room, designer Emily Del Bello painted a shadowy accent wall black and mounted the TV there—you wouldn't even notice it at first glance.

In a room as traditional and cohesive as this, you probably don't want a big black rectangle interrupting your aesthetic, so do what designer Lynne Uhalt did and hide your TV in a closet. This guest room is heavily inspired by European interiors and architecture, but Uhalt still wanted the guests to be able to watch TV and relax in bed. As a design-friendly solution, she put the TV in one of the closets, which still boasts enough room to enough to hold clothes.

Designer John Wooden maximized limited square footage in his clients' cottage by cleverly hiding several items, including placing their TV in a beautiful credenza. One of the simplest methods to hide your TV is by incorporating it into furniture that already complements the space. This wooden mid-century piece fits perfectly into the room, making it an ideal spot for a hidden TV.

In a maximalist, highly colorful space like this, designer Shazalynn Cavin-Winfrey couldn't see how a TV could work in the room, so she decided to hide it in the green cabinet in the back. However, the designer took it a step further and covered the glass with fabric that pulls hues from the rest of the room.

Everick Brown designed a statement wall encased in custom steel with walnut insets to create a “floating credenza” for a hidden TV. A console table behind the sofa helps separate the two living room zones.

Designed to look like abstract artwork, Kristin Fine hid the TV behind modern paneling above the fireplace. It suits the neutral tones nicely and maintains the refined and grown-up atmosphere while also ensuring that it's family-friendly.

This living room sitting area revolves around the corner fireplace. To ensure that the TV didn't take up too much valuable visual space, designer Denise McGaha invested in a Samsung Frame and disguised it as a photograph that complements the blue and grey color scheme.

This outdoor living room, designed by Amanda Lindroth, is the perfect place to entertain and unwind. Look closely at the pagoda-shaped cabinet above the fireplace, and you'll notice that it opens to reveal a hidden TV. It was based on a design from St. Michael's Mount castle in England.

What do you do in a busy kitchen where the children love TV and the mom doesn't—but she does want to keep an eye on said kids? Enter the Samsung Frame, once again making it all work in family homes with competing interests and preferences, like in April Tomlin Interiors designer Sarah Rogers's kitchen here.

A bi-fold panel painting by Stuart Coleman Budd hides a TV, but the goal was to be transparent. “Bronze hinges honestly express that it’s a movable screen—that this is true kinetic art,” says architect Ken Pursley of Pursley Dixon Architecture.

This formal living room, designed by Mark Cunningham, doubles as a more relaxed and casual hangout room. To ensure that it could do both well, Cunningham built a hidden TV into the back wall shelf that pops up when the occupants want to unwind with a good movie.

Don’t hide it, just make it one with your wall. This gallery wall of various-sized framed prints blends in with the Samsung Frame TV, which has a wood frame and a rotating display of art images. “None of my clients' guests realize it's a TV when they first walk into the room,” says Natalie Myer of Veneer Designs.

Custom paneling over a fireplace elevates the television area in this bedroom lounge zone by Studio McGee. Flush with the fireplace facade and further disguised by a custom mirror, the hidden TV definitely will not be ruining the chic decor scheme here.

Related Story: Shea McGee Just Dropped a Cottagecore Home Collab With a Major Fashion Brand

Designer CeCe Barfield Thompson hid a TV behind a retractable antique mirror for maximum glamour. “In an oak-paneled Manhattan library, I designed an antique mirror to hide the wall-mounted television,” Thompson says. “The mirror's lower panels retract like a garage door to reveal an entertainment system behind. This mechanism allowed us to create a room that was incredibly functional, without sacrificing an ounce of beauty!”

Related Story: This Manhattan Apartment Showcases an Abundance of Antiques and Contemporary Art

Here's another example of custom cabinetry with a hidden TV. A sliding door flush with the wood accent wall makes this media room designed by Heidi Caillier extra discreet and pretty. After all, the custom upholstery, drapery, and grasscloth wallpaper are too good to get lost in the entertainment system shuffle.

Designer Nina Farmer took the most elegant approach possible—hiding the TV in a custom mirrored cabinet atop the mantel. “The living room needed a concealed TV due to the formality of the architecture,” she says. “Hand-blown mirror was used on the doors so that the enclosure fit with the original 1850s marble mantel. It has concealed hinges and no pull, so when it’s closed, you would never know what’s behind it.”

If you can’t outright conceal your screen, the key is to avoid the “black hole” effect when it’s off. “I always try to blend them into the architecture of the house,” says designer Eche Martinez. “We recently completed a project in Belvedere, CA, where the homeowners were hesitant about installing a TV in their living room. To solve this, we decided that the best way to divert attention from the TV when it was not in use was to have an oversized, freestanding piece of art right next to it. Clients loved it, and above all, the piece looks great in the room."

#7009. Flashpoint Receives Patent for Ransomware Risk Model, Tying Vulnerabilites to Likelihood of Attack

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

The patent comes at a time when security teams are confronting a growing vulnerability problem: Flashpoint's Global Threat Intelligence Report: 2026 Midyear Edition tracked 21,667 vulnerability disclosures during the first half of 2026, an 8% increase.

Of those, 4,015, or around 19 percent, already had public or functional exploit code. Flashpoint also reported a 45 percent increase in Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS) attacks during the period.

The patent was granted August 11 2026, although the underlying Ransomware Risk model isn't new. It has been available through Flashpoint Vulnerability Intelligence since 2022.

The model addresses a basic vulnerability-management problem: severity and real-world ransomware risk aren't necessarily the same thing, because severity scores do not necessarily indicate which flaws attackers are most likely to target.

The Common Vulnerability Scoring System, or CVSS, measures how severe a vulnerability could be. The Exploit Prediction Scoring System, or EPSS, estimates the probability of exploitation more broadly.

Flashpoint's Ransomware Risk model adds another dimension by comparing newly disclosed vulnerabilities with the characteristics of flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks.

That could change where a vulnerability lands in an organization's patching queue. A lower-severity flaw might normally sit behind numerous critical vulnerabilities. But if its characteristics closely resemble vulnerabilities repeatedly targeted by ransomware operators, security teams may have reason to move it higher.

"A severity score alone can't tell you which vulnerabilities pose the greatest real-world risk of exploitation by ransomware actors," Flashpoint CEO Josh Lefkowitz said. "Our patented Ransomware Risk model applies years of threat intelligence to that problem, giving customers an earlier signal and helping them prioritize based on how attackers actually operate."

The firm's midyear research also illustrates the scale of the prioritization problem. Its vulnerability intelligence covers traditional IT systems as well as cloud, IoT, operational technology, open source software. and third-party libraries.

The company says it also tracks more than 105,000 vulnerabilities that aren't represented in CVE or the National Vulnerability Database, expanding the pool of flaws that security teams may need to evaluate.

The cybersecurity company's patented methodology profiles vulnerabilities using more than 60 technical characteristics, including whether a flaw can be exploited remotely without authentication, whether it affects operational technology systems, and how exploitation could affect data availability.

Those characteristics form a multi-factor fingerprint. The model maps each vulnerability against clusters containing flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks and assigns a Low, Medium, High, or Critical Ransomware Risk rating.

Flashpoint says the rating can be generated when a vulnerability is disclosed, rather than requiring security teams to wait for public evidence of active exploitation. Ratings are then updated as new ransomware activity and threat intelligence emerge.

Within Flashpoint Ignite, Ransomware Risk appears alongside CVSS, EPSS, Social Risk, and exploit maturity. The idea isn't to replace existing scoring systems, but to give administrators additional information when deciding what to remediate first.

Nor does a High or Critical Ransomware Risk rating mean a vulnerability will necessarily be exploited. The model measures how closely its characteristics resemble vulnerabilities historically associated with ransomware attacks.

For security teams facing thousands of potential vulnerabilities, that distinction matters. The flaw carrying the highest severity score isn't necessarily the one that most closely resembles what ransomware operators have historically chosen to attack.

"Despite being the most popular indicator for priority, we know that severity alone doesn't tell you whether a vulnerability will be exploited or used in ransomware," said Ben Haynes, Data Science & Analytics Lead at Flashpoint and co-inventor of the patented methodology.

"A vulnerability can score low on a traditional severity scale and still share the characteristics we repeatedly see in ransomware attacks."

For more information, go to the Flashpoint site.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

District and state policies run the gamut from embracing to banning AI. This raises a key question: When does AI accelerate student learning and when does it interfere?

#7006. Winter’s Most Wanted: Tommy Hilfiger Holiday Picks for Every Occasion

From sharp knits to travel-ready extras, these are the pieces worth putting on your list.

The sweater that looked fine in October suddenly feels too thin, the jacket you reach for every day isn’t warm enough, and a weekend away calls for more than whatever you can squeeze into an overnight bag. Then there’s the rest of the season: office parties, cold walks across town, slow mornings at home, and the occasional trip out of town. Getting dressed for all of it takes a few reliable essentials.

Tommy Hilfiger has been building its take on American style since 1985, when the brand launched with a fresh spin on classic menswear. Its signature mix of preppy staples, sporty details, and relaxed ease has made it a year-round proposition, with pieces that move from everyday dressing to more seasonal needs. For winter, that means familiar wardrobe staples with a little extra warmth, practical layers for unpredictable weather, and accessories suited to life on the go. These six picks cover the season from the first cold snap to the last weekend getaway. Here’s what we’re putting on our fashion gift list.

A cable-knit sweater is a cold-weather staple, and Tommy Hilfiger’s take keeps the look classic. Made from combed cotton with a traditional cable pattern and ribbed trim, it’s an easy layer under a wool coat or a natural choice for a casual holiday gathering. Pair it with dark jeans or chinos for a straightforward winter look.

For trips, a jacket that barely takes up any room in your bag is always useful. Tommy Hilfiger’s lightweight puffer folds into its own interior zip pocket, making it easy to stash for a weekend away or keep on hand when the forecast is unpredictable. It has thermal insulation, a regular fit, and side pockets, so it’s a practical layer for travel and everyday errands alike.

A good winter evening often calls for staying in, and this sleep set makes a comfortable night at home feel a little more put together. This one pairs a simple T-shirt with plaid flannel pants, bringing a classic cold-weather pattern into the mix. It’s an easy gift for someone who appreciates a slow weekend morning as much as a cozy night in.

A warm beanie is one of winter’s most useful accessories, and this one gets an upgrade with a cotton-cashmere blend. The classic knit is an easy finishing touch for everything from a morning commute to a weekend walk. It’s also a practical gift that will see plenty of use once the temperature drops.

A card holder and keychain are the kind of everyday essentials that make an especially easy gift. Tommy Hilfiger’s leather set brings the two together in one package, with a polished finish that feels a step up from the usual stocking stuffer. It’s a useful pick for someone who likes to keep their daily carry simple.

A weekend away calls for a bag that can handle a change of clothes and a few extra essentials without looking like you’re heading to the gym. Tommy’s leather duffel brings a more polished feel to the overnight bag, making it a smart companion for a winter getaway or a quick trip home for the holidays.
