#7005. Designers Reveal the Fastest Way They’ve Updated a Living Room Under $100

A few small swaps can make your space feel brand new.

A living room is arguably the most popular spot of the house, so why not give it the tender loving care it deserves with a design refresh? Problem is, when it comes to most home projects, it’s easy to convince yourself good things only come to those who wait—or anyone willing to fork over a small fortune.

Fortunately, affordability and efficiency are no longer mutually exclusive. According to the pros, it’s possible to elevate a space swiftly and without spending much money. Want proof? Keep scrolling for the clever (and quick) ways designers have upgraded a living room for $100 or less.

Barbra Fordyce of Ginger Lemon Indigo says plants are “hands-down” one of the best low-cost, high-impact ways to spruce up your space. “Bringing something living into your living room instantly changes the mood, quite literally bringing life, nature and an organic beauty into the room,” explains the designer. While fresh foliage can look good anywhere, she loves how plants can add a “verdant richness” to neutral rooms without throwing off the entire color palette.

That said, houseplants aren’t a one-size-fits-all situation, so choose wisely.

“If you have high ceilings, you want the plant to be proportional to those dimensions,” Fordyce says. “If the plant or tree is too small in a large living room, it will feel dwarfed.”

For the perfect finishing touch, use your leftover budget to buy a beautiful planter. “The container should echo the feeling of your room,” she shares. “A basket, ceramic container or even a vintage wooden crate can all make beautiful ‘pots’ for your plants.”

Though there is such a thing as too many throw pillows, the right ones can make a difference. “A pillow and throw refresh can go a long way in bringing new life to your sofa,” explains Natalie Kaelin, an interior designer in Raleigh, North Carolina.

Of course, there’s no need to buy brand-new lumbar and square cushions on a whim: Simply purchase covers for your existing pillows and swap them out seasonally.

Neutral linen layers can conjure coastal grandmother vibes virtually anywhere, while plush, jewel-toned velvets offer an elegant, autumnal look. Come winter, embrace the holiday spirit with flannel fabric or a plaid repeat.

We might spend a lot of time talking about layered lighting or how to pick the perfect fixture, but we cannot stress it enough: Pay attention to your bulbs.

“Daylight LED bulbs are incredibly white, so they put out light akin to doctors’ offices and hospitals,” says Elizabeth Cross-Beard of Palm Henri in Baltimore. “A warmer temperate bulb gives a home a candle-light, cozy feel.” As a general rule of thumb, she recommends buying lightbulbs with a temperature of 2700K and avoiding anything above 4000K.

Cross-Beard says switching your bulbs can effectively “redesign” your space, and it’s shockingly inexpensive. “Depending on how many lights you have, it can cost as little as $50,” she adds.

If you want to upgrade your space without spending a cent, Blair Burton says to start with your furniture.

“Sometimes simply pulling furniture away from the walls and creating a more comfortable conversation area can completely change how a room feels,” the Austin-based designer explains.

After all, just because an armchair has been in the corner of your living room for years doesn’t mean it needs to go there. By doing a room rearrangement, you might end up using it more when it’s in front of a warm, sun-drenched window. Maybe you’ll even realize you need to pick up an ottoman to create the ultimate cozy space.

Another $0 hack? Burton also encourages her clients to “shop their homes.” “It’s practical, of course, but it's also about honoring the things they've collected and loved over the years,” she explains. “Sometimes finding an unexpected new home for a favorite piece is one of the most satisfying discoveries in a project.”

“Adding artwork, whether it's a gallery wall, a single large-scale piece, or something in between, makes a huge visual and personal impact in a living room,” says Molly Torres Portnof of DATE Interiors in New York.

Retailers like Minted and Society6 offer well-priced prints, but you can also reframe photographs and posters you already own. As long as you pay attention to scale and proportion, the ceiling’s really the limit. “Rules are meant to be broken,” Portnof says. “If it feels right and makes you happy, you're on the right track.”

If your living room is blessed with beautiful built-ins, you can fill them with treasures you already own, or buy a few new pieces for well under $100.

“Take the time to create vignettes,” advises Tennessee designer Katie Kiser. “More often than not, I find my clients have all the pieces for a well-choreographed space, but they're styled improperly.”

For an instant yet affordable upgrade, head over to your local antique store to pick up some art books, jars, or a trinket tray.

#7004. SailPoint Intros New Capabilities to Address Agentic AI Identity Security Challenge

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint. In its latest Horizons of Identity Security report, SailPoint found that 79% of enterprises already have AI agents running in production, while just 2% have deployed identity security tools specifically designed to govern them.

That gap could become harder to ignore as organizations give agents more autonomy. Agents can increasingly access applications, sensitive data, and credentials, and in some cases take actions on a user's behalf, effectively creating a new class of identity that IT and security teams need to manage.

SailPoint, which develops identity security and access governance technology for enterprises, is responding with a collection of new identity security capabilities announced at its Navigate 2026 conference. The company is expanding its Agentic Fabric to discover "shadow AI" agents and provide runtime authorization and controls capable of immediately disabling an agent.

The company also introduced what it calls SailPoint Autonomous Agents, specialized AI agents designed to govern other agents. The security agents can monitor runtime activity, intercept threats, and adjust permissions while allowing legitimate agents to continue operating, SailPoint said.

The idea reflects one of the challenges created by increasingly autonomous AI systems: Traditional human approval processes may not be fast enough to govern software capable of taking large numbers of actions on its own.

"The idea that a security admin is going to review and approve access for a group of autonomous agents making 10,000 tool calls a second isn't just outdated — it's a fantasy," SailPoint CTO Chandra Gnanasambandam said.

Another part of SailPoint's approach focuses on visibility.

New endpoint and browser sensors, along with SIEM and XDR telemetry and non-human identity discovery capabilities, are designed to uncover agents, MCP servers, credentials, tools, and data stores that haven't been brought under an organization's identity program.

The company is also introducing Autonomous Identity Security Posture Management, or A-ISPM, which uses identity information from its Atlas platform to identify dormant accounts, orphaned access, shadow administrators, and other potentially risky access paths. Organizations can then revoke access, disable an identity or AI agent, assign an owner or automate enforcement.

Just-in-Time provisioning is another part of the strategy. Rather than giving people, service accounts, and AI agents permanent access, permissions can be granted when needed and subjected to conditions such as reauthentication or a business justification.

Capabilities across SailPoint's Agentic Fabric, Human Fabric, and Atlas platforms are beginning to roll out globally. A-ISPM and several other announced features are expected in the company's fourth quarter of fiscal 2027.

"Autonomous agents are no longer just experimental copilots; they are becoming the primary execution layer of modern enterprises," SailPoint President Matt Mills said.

For more information, go to the SailPoint site.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

District and state policies run the gamut from embracing to banning AI. This raises a key question: When does AI accelerate student learning and when does it interfere?

#7000. Survey: Most Organizations Are Not Consistently Measuring AI Value Against Costs

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs. The company's Global AI Pulse Q3 2026 report found that 89% of organizations report measurable value from AI, while just 12% consistently assess that value against costs across the organization.

Average planned AI investment for the next 12 months has risen to $210 million, compared with $186 million in the first quarter of 2026.

The findings are based on responses from 2,131 senior leaders across 20 countries and jurisdictions, surveyed between July 23 and August 26, 2026.

Most organizations have established ways to track AI spending: 61% review costs during project approval, and 59% monitor them once systems are operating.

Consistently connecting those costs to value remains less common. Beyond the 12% that do so across the organization, another 26% assess value against costs for most initiatives.

Organizations reporting established AI returns have stronger measurement practices. Among this group, 48% consistently assess value against costs, four times the proportion in the overall survey group.

Cost-monitoring dashboards are also more common among organizations reporting established returns: 77% use them, compared with 43% of those still experimenting.

"The organizations seeing a return did the implementation and the transformation properly: guardrails in place, workforce ready, data cleaned up," said Priya Emmanuel, global head of aIQ at KPMG International. "That is a sequence, not a secret."

Organizations are also developing more formal systems to manage AI deployment.

Fifty-five percent operate a formal AI management layer, which KPMG calls a "harness layer," comprising controls and tools between AI models and business applications.

Security and identity controls, along with data access controls, were the most common capabilities, each reported by 43% of respondents. Model routing, which determines which model handles a particular task, was reported by 23%.

Meanwhile, 53% assign accountability for AI-informed decisions to a named executive, the CEO, or an executive committee.

The proportion reporting significant employee adoption of AI agents rose from 25% in the first quarter to 34% in the third quarter.

Cybersecurity concerns were more common among organizations with mature AI programs.

Half of those reporting established AI returns identified cybersecurity as a barrier, compared with roughly one-third of those still experimenting.

Overall, 86% of respondents said their organizations are adapting their cybersecurity operating models to address AI-accelerated threats.

The report identifies accountability, operational resilience, and cost management as priorities as AI becomes more embedded in business operations.

"Many organizations have made AI models widely available and track their use," said Swaminathan Chandrasekaran, head of the US AI Center of Excellence at KPMG. "But consumption alone is not a strategy."

For the full report, visit the KPMG site.

Artificial intelligence is raising the stakes of cyber conflict as attackers use the technology to accelerate reconnaissance, uncover vulnerabilities, and launch attacks faster than many security teams can respond, according to a new report from agentic AI security company Kai.

Cybersecurity companies Rubrik and CrowdStrike have announced an expanded identity-security integration designed to move compromised identity environments from threat detection through clean recovery with less manual intervention.

Microsoft has shut down EvilTokens, a cybercrime service that used AI to help attackers break into e-mail accounts, study their victims, and build convincing financial scams.

Video game designers spend an enormous amount of time studying what keeps people engaged when something becomes difficult. They have become very good at helping people persist. Schools should pay attention.

#6998. The 12 Best Puffer Jackets You Can Buy Right Now

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

We love a barn jacket. We love a blazer. But when it’s below freezing and snowing sideways, neither is going to do you much good. That’s puffer jacket weather, and no number of layered sweaters under your favorite jacket is going to change the forecast.

Luckily, there’s no shortage of puffer jackets out there. The trick is finding one that’s warm enough, durable enough, and ready to stand up to whatever precipitation the winter sky throws its way—all while looking good enough that you don’t feel like the Michelin Tire Man every time you leave the house. The good news? There are plenty of puffer jackets that fit the bill, from luxury options from Canada Goose and Moncler to more affordable ones from REI and Carhartt.

There is a range in how exactly a puffer gets its shape, too. Most are down-filled, and the way the stitching is designed affects the puffiness. The fewer the stitches, the bigger the puffs. For something more packed down, there are ones with denser stitching that make for a slimmer-fitting jacket. Then you get into the nitty-gritty: Is the hood removable? Can you wash it at home? Is it really, truly, waterproof? Start your shopping with these puffer jackets you can buy right now and wear for many cold days to come.

One of the most iconic purveyors of the puffer jacket, TNF is a brand that deserves the hype it gets. This style, with its wide puffs, is a throwback look that’s oh so welcome in the 2020s. It's designed to tough out even the coldest winter days, with a water-repellent and windproof outer, goose down, and a three-piece hood that you can stow away into the collar.

To me, this is the perfect packable puffer: Slim enough to roll up and stash away and sturdy enough to brave wind, rain, and snow. REI designs this lightweight style for the man on the go, so for all your winter hikes and outdoor excursions, this is the perfect in-between option that won't weigh you down.

Canada Goose is a household name when it comes to winter gear, and the Lawrence Puffer is one of its best sellers for good reason. Our editors (along with actors like Daniel Craig and Ethan Hawke) love this lightweight, warm, cozy jacket, which was designed with the details in mind; it has a suede chin guard so the stand collar won’t bother you, backpack straps, reflective stripes, and even two different color options for the disc logo.

Eddie Bauer’s CirrusLite is a sleeper hit. It won’t win awards as the most wind-resistant or coolest looking, though I love the more earthy colorways. It layers well, keeps you warm, and is durable enough if you need to wear it on a legit mountain—all this while being super lightweight and easy to pack.

Whether you're commuting in a bustling city or packing for a cold-weather trip, this lightweight jacket is a winter staple. It's stylish but doesn't skimp on insulation for a chilly day. The best part, though: Unlike many other puffers, this one is about as slim fitting as you can get. Kiss all the extra bulk goodbye this winter. Or, if temperatures go below freezing, add a wool coat on top for extra coverage.

Not only is this jacket insulated, but the interior is lined with sherpa, too, making it a cold-weather superhero. The high collar and full-front zip make it virtually windproof, so whether you’re darting from building to building or staying outside for a while, you’re all set.

Don’t get me wrong, an Arc’teryx Thorium is a phenomenal jacket. I just think at that $450 price point, you’re often just as good going for a North Face Nuptse (if you want an outer layer) or an REI down jacket (for layering). I think Veilance, which is the higher-end and more fashionable Arc’teryx sub-label, is a more interesting proposition. You get a sleek city cut, a nicer matte ripstop shell, and insanely warm 1000-fill down.

Rocky Mountain makes one of the sickest jackets out there. Westernwear is on a bit of a downturn—well, it’s classic, but had its cultural peak two years ago—but you are seeing this style of working Western down jacket with leather yoke pop up more and more. Rocky Mountain Featherbed is the OG, starting in the 1960s as a real brand for cattlemen and their employees. Its current iteration is actually a Japanese-owned reproduction, which means it’s the real fucking deal. It looks cool, wears hard, and is probably warmer than anything on this list.

Lululemon is based in Canada, and maybe that explains why all of its outerwear is so good. We love its bulkier puffer jacket, but for a sleeker, winter athleisure feel, this minimally puffed jacket is great, too. It keeps the heat in and water out, and with a stretchy, down-filled construction, you’re sure to stay warm and cozy.

This puffer from Aritzia was made with the same attention to detail and tech that goes into high-performance sleeping bags. It has 700-fill down, but it’s sewn with big horizontal chambers like a sleeping bag. It’ll withstand temperatures as low as -22°F, so it’s the only coat you’ll need for surviving a serious cold snap. The fit is intentionally oversized—longer in the arms and broader at the shoulders—to accommodate bulky sweaters underneath. Plus, the Japanese fabric is both water-repellent and wind-resistant.

Is this an outdoorsy jacket? No. Is it outdoors capable? Yes, absolutely. Heavy down jackets—as real mountaineers use them—are made for base camp, when you’re not moving but need a lot of heat. This is perfect for that.

The issue with most puffer jackets is that they’re not made to be especially abrasion or weather resistant. These jackets were originally made for cold and dry days on mountaintops. But Filson takes a different approach.

The Down Cruiser shell is a classic heavy oil-finished Filson cotton. That makes this jacket more weather-resistant and abrasion-resistant than any other option on this list.

So, you want the best? For performance, you can buy something cheaper from an outdoors brand. For fashion, you can get something cool and garment-dyed from Stone Island. For a merging of those sensibilities, Moncler is the only brand—the OG that made jackets for Europe’s mountaineers—that you should be buying from.

The Grenoble name denotes Moncler’s high-end ski line. Go to a nice ski resort in the Alps, and you’ll see plenty of these. Nothing looks as good or insulates as well. And if you don’t ski but want something fashion-forward, I still prefer the Grenoble line. The abrasion-resistant fabrics on the jackets look a lot better than the high-shine nylon that Moncler and other brands like to do on puffers.

While finding the perfect puffer jacket isn’t rocket science, there are a few key features you should focus on to narrow your search down from the 20 options above.

Insulation: Some climates call for the most intense down fill power there is, and some simply don’t. You don’t want to overheat this winter, nor do you want to be too cold, so go for a puffer with fill or insulation that your climate calls for. Don’t make the mistake of buying an 850-fill-power puffer if you live in a city where the coldest it ever gets is, like, 59 degrees.

Features: Not all puffer jackets are made the same. Personally, I need a removable hood. Look for design features including adjustability at the hem and hood, windproof fabric, zippered or buttoned pockets, a mock neck, and more, depending on your preferences.

Puffiness: If you’re someone who travels a lot and needs to stash your puffer in a suitcase from time to time, going for a super-puffy option probably isn’t going to be your best bet. Instead, look for puffers with a little less, well, puff, which will make them much easier to stow and pack. Some on this list even come with their own stow bags.

Being based in New York City means we know what makes a good puffer jacket. The options we listed above bring the heat in terms of functionality and style, all season long. Over the years, our editors have tried hundreds of styles and brands. Narrowing it down to our 20 favorites was no small feat, but these are just the picks that we swear by and return to time and time again for our winter wear.

Fill power is the number that tells you the quality of down in your jacket. The number technically indicates how many cubic inches of loft a single ounce of down has, but for simplicity, just focus on whether the number is relatively high or low—the higher it is, the higher-quality the insulation is: 600 is good, 700 is great, and 800 is spectacular.

Over time, as down goes through washing machines, tough weather conditions, and daily life, it can lose its fluffiness and slightly deflate your puffer. It’ll still keep you warm and dry, sure, just not quite so puffy.

#6996. 33 Bedroom Plants That Bring a Calming Touch to Your Space

Do you have one of these in your room yet?

Want to improve your sleep naturally? Try adding a few plants to your bedroom. Beyond offering charm and a cozy atmosphere, plants have been linked to mental and physical benefits, including sleep. Research suggests that being around greenery can lift your mood, ease stress, and promote a greater sense of well-being. Bringing a touch of nature into your sleeping space may be a simple way to add those positive qualities to the room.

Plants may also help calm the mind and move the body from an alert state toward a more restful one, potentially supporting better sleep. A past study linked indoor greenery with more positive emotions and fewer negative ones, which may help you fall asleep faster. The findings point to a broader connection between greenery and well-being, including its possible role in a restful bedroom. It’s easy to see why biophilic design—an approach that evokes nature indoors—has become a favorite among designers.

Ahead, find the best plants for your bedroom. We made sure the options would thrive in all types of lights, but especially in low light, so that whether you’re a beginner or an experienced green thumb, you will find the right verdant friend to grow.

The Aspidistra elatior thrives in low to bright shade, making it perfect for bedrooms that don’t get too much direct light. A fun fact: This plant historically was referred to as the “bar room” plant or “beer plant” because it could survive smoky, low-light conditions in Victorian-era taverns and halls. It is non-toxic to humans, cats, and dogs so if you have pets this is a great option.

Native to the rainforests of Brazil, the Maranta leuconeura is now a common indoor houseplant. During the day, the oval leaves lie flat to soak up the light and at night, the leaves fold upward like praying hands, hence the name.

For a trailing plant that thrives in bright, indirect light get a wax plant. It appreciates higher humidity levels so if you live in an older home with fluctuating humidity, this is the verdant plant baby for you.

For beginner and intermediate plant owners, the nerve plant is a great option because they thrive in most indoor conditions, remain compact, and are safe for pets. They will signal to you what they need so you can water and choose the soil for it accordingly.

Pilea peperomioides, or the Chinese money plant, is a cheerful-looking, easy-to-propagate plant that is incredibly low-maintenance. It's a great plant for a sunny bedroom. All plant parents need to do is place it in bright, indirect sunlight and water only when the top inch of soil feels dry.

Aglaonema tolerates very low light levels, so it's ideal if you don't have large windows in your bedroom. Water it when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

The snake plant doesn't mind low light—and because it's a succulent, it only needs to be watered every week or two.

Purported to bring good luck, the money tree has a nice, modern vibe—plus, it's not fussy and grows quickly. Give it medium light, water it every week or two, and it'll be happy.

The pothos plant tolerates any light level from low to high. (That means it's super-easy to keep alive!) Just don't water it until the top few inches of soil feel dry to the touch—overwatering is the only real way to kill this plant.

Lavender has long been considered beneficial for relaxation and sleep. Although most research has studied the use of lavender essential oils specifically, having a live lavender plant in your bedroom probably can't hurt. It needs bright light, so make sure it's on a sunny windowsill or use a grow light. Water when the top inch or two feels dry to the touch.

This handsome vining plant is a great choice for new plant parents. Its glossy, heart-shaped leaves are beautiful as they spill over the edges of nightstands or bookshelves–and it's tolerant of low light levels. Water when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

This adorable fern is one of the best to raise indoors. Its thick, waxy leaves retain water better than many other ferns that have finer fronds—and it won't leave a mess by dropping foliage every day. Give it bright indirect light and water when the top inch of soil feels dry.

This fun plant looks like an old-fashioned ric rac trim. It will grow like crazy in bright indirect light—and because it's a succulent, it needs watering only every week to 10 days.

This social media darling is striking and easy to grow—it just needs bright indirect light. Water when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

The peace lily will adapt well to low light levels, but it needs bright indirect light to bloom. Water when the top inch or two feel dry.

This cute palm that looks just like a messy ponytail likes bright indirect light. And the bulbous base stores water, so it needs a drink only every few weeks when the soil is totally dry.

This handsome plant boasts a beautiful upright form, so it makes an eye-catching statement in the bedroom. Give it bright indirect light, and water when the top few inches of soil are dry. As an outdoor landscape plant, it will flower, though that's unusual indoors.

If you're looking for low-maintenance houseplants, this is the way to go! It will tolerate low light and is often grown in water. In water, change it weekly; in soil, water so that it stays slightly moist.

This cute tabletop palm will add loads of style to your bedroom. It prefers bright indirect light, but it will tolerate medium light better than many other types of palms. Water when it's slightly dry to the touch.

#6993. Report: Cyber Attacks Increasingly Exploit Trusted Workplace Tools

In its latest threat intelligence report, Ontinue found that attackers are increasingly using trusted workplace tools to trick users into giving them access to enterprise networks. Approximately 30% of organizations monitored by the cybersecurity company experienced at least one attempt to impersonate IT support through Microsoft Teams during the first half of 2026. About one in every 990 external Teams contacts showed signs of someone pretending to be an internal IT worker.

Ontinue's report says the trouble is that these attacks blend in with ordinary workday communication, which makes them harder to catch.

"The majority of effective attacks today don't rely on sophisticated exploits. They rely on trust," Balazs Greksza, director of Advanced Threat Operations at Ontinue, said in the company's announcement.

Ontinue broke down how a typical attack works: An employee gets an unexpected Teams message or call from someone claiming to work in the organization's IT department. The person warns of a security issue, a software problem or an update that can't wait. Then they walk the employee through opening Windows Quick Assist, installing remote-access software, or running a PowerShell command. Following those steps can hand the attacker control of the employee's computer, often without the employee realizing it.

The approach is very similar to the Black Basta ransomware group, which helped popularize similar attacks in 2024. Unfortunately, Ontinue found the technique has since been adopted by other cybercriminal groups, including some with no known link to Black Basta.

Some threat agents are also combining tactics. They might first overwhelm an employee's inbox with unwanted e-mails, then reach out through Teams pretending to be IT support offering to fix the problem.

"Phishing no longer resides solely in your inbox. It has spread across the entire corporate communications environment," said Mika Aalto, co-founder and CEO of Hoxhunt.

Teams impersonation is only part of the problem. Ontinue also found attackers taking advantage of legitimate Microsoft sign-in processes. Between May and June, researchers identified 10 confirmed device-code phishing compromises across five customer tenants.

In these attacks, criminals trick employees into completing what appears to be a normal Microsoft authentication process. But doing so can give the attacker access to the employee's account and system without actually breaking their password.

The report also found attackers using blockchain-based infrastructure to hide malicious activity, accounting for more than 25% of confirmed malicious ClickFix findings.

"Attackers are no longer looking for gaps in your perimeter. They are looking for the systems your organization already trusts and finding ways to move through them undetected," said Shane Barney, chief information security officer at Keeper Security.

The security firm says that IT needs to keep a closer eye on unexpected Teams contacts, remote-support requests, suspicious PowerShell activity, and changes to authentication settings. Employees should also confirm sensitive requests through established internal channels, rather than relying on the name or account that appears on their screen.

Aviv Nahum, co-founder and CEO of Above Security, noted that AI is making these attacks more convincing by helping criminals imitate familiar voices, writing styles, and conversations.

"The security model therefore must shift from 'does this look real?' to 'can I independently verify that this person is who they claim to be?'" Nahum said.

The full Ontinue report can be found here.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

Artificial intelligence is raising the stakes of cyber conflict as attackers use the technology to accelerate reconnaissance, uncover vulnerabilities, and launch attacks faster than many security teams can respond, according to a new report from agentic AI security company Kai.

Cybersecurity companies Rubrik and CrowdStrike have announced an expanded identity-security integration designed to move compromised identity environments from threat detection through clean recovery with less manual intervention.

Microsoft has shut down EvilTokens, a cybercrime service that used AI to help attackers break into e-mail accounts, study their victims, and build convincing financial scams.

Video game designers spend an enormous amount of time studying what keeps people engaged when something becomes difficult. They have become very good at helping people persist. Schools should pay attention.

→ Baca artikel asli

#6991. The 5 Best Aluminum Carry-ons You Can Buy

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Tumi and Rimowa lead the pack, but the competition is getting close.

If you’re serious about travel, an aluminum carry-on is worth the investment. A well-made one can outlast soft-sided bags made from nylon, polyester, or canvas, as well as hard-sided suitcases made from polycarbonate—just a high-strength plastic. And it can withstand pretty much any weather: While water can seep through a traditional zipper, most aluminum-frame suitcases use metal latches and a compressed gasket to help seal out the elements, so your belongings stay safe and sound.

A full aluminum-shell carry-on typically uses high-grade aluminum-magnesium alloy sheets, reinforced corners, and those mechanical latches that now usually have TSA locks on them. Your stuff has a bodyguard. The catch? That bodyguard is a touch heavier than its polycarbonate counterparts. Full-aluminum carry-ons can weigh ten pounds or more before you’ve packed a single pair of underwear, and their shells have little give. That can be an issue if you’re taking an out-of-the-way flight and end up somewhere with carry-on weight limits.

But what likely catches your attention when you see an aluminum suitcase in the airport is the look. It’s got the retro-futurist swagger of the golden age of aviation with a modern luxury twist (and, yes, a higher price tag). There’s a reason Rimowa’s shiny luggage has become somewhat of a status symbol. Yours will probably pick up some nicks and dings along the way, but think of them as proof that it’s been places—and that you have stories to tell from all your travels.

Ready to invest in an aluminum carry-on for your next adventure? Make it one of our favorites below.

Tumi luggage comes with a hefty price tag (we told you these would be expensive!) but it truly will last for a very long time. When it comes to material, it doesn’t get more durable than this aircraft-grade aluminum, from which the exterior of this suitcase is made. Even the three-stage telescoping handle is made of aluminum.

But what’s even better about this carry-on is that, if you do end up needing more room (and the hard outer shell won’t budge because it’s just that damn sturdy), you can expand it and add two more inches of depth—yes, that means you probably won’t be able to use it as a carry-on after expanding it, but it’s a good option for when you want a smaller checked bag or like having the ability to pack more on your way back.

We can’t talk about aluminum carry-ons without mentioning the most famous of them all: Rimowa. Yes, it’s expensive, and yes, it will probably get a few dents over time. But Rimowa’s aluminum luggage is the best of the best in terms of style, durability, and recognition. Take it from Esquire’s senior director of commerce and partnerships, Krista Jones:

“When it comes to investment pieces, it all boils down to one argument. Why buy the mid-range level when the top-level is better quality? If you want a suitcase that will last you a lifetime—and this will—why spend $500 once on something you have to replace in five years? You might as well spend [$1,550] on something you don’t have to replace again. Rimowa offers that with a lifetime guarantee and will repair your bag too.”

When it comes to style, Away takes the cake for me. I’ve always loved the simplicity of the brand’s signature look with clean lines and smooth, rounded corners, and that look translates beautifully onto the aluminum version of Away’s classic carry-on. The shell is made from premium anodized aluminum, and the handles have gorgeous leather detailing—and the included leather luggage tag is a nice touch. This carry-on is also proof that aluminum suitcases don’t always have to come in silver. Yes, the silver colorway is a classic, but if you want something different, I’m obsessed with this deep metallic blue.

Earlier this year, Carl Friedrik joined the conversation and launched an aluminum luggage line of its own. In keeping with the brand’s Scandinavian minimalist roots, the carry-on has a simple aluminum exterior with leather detailing—but inside, it has a lot more going on. The interior boasts a zipped garment compartment on one side and an open section with a compression pad and straps on the other, as well as two zippered pockets for all the small things you want to keep organized.

Want a true aluminum carry-on without the $1,000 price tag? Nobl makes one for under $400, if you can believe it. It doesn’t have the glitz and glam of the Rimowa, but it works just as well and genuinely holds up. Plus, it comes with a free Nobl Air, a trackable chip that goes in your carry-on so you know where it is at all times—just in case you have to gate check it at the last minute.

#6987. Sophos Launches Tool for Shadow AI Defense

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos. In a recent product announcement, the company highlighted the growing use of shadow AI, referring to AI tools and agents operating without formal organizational approval or oversight. The security firm cited research indicating that 69% of organizations suspect or have evidence of unauthorized AI use.

Separate research from Gartner and Cloud Security Alliance found that 82% of organizations discovered shadow AI agents in their environments during the previous year.

According to the security firm, organizations have struggled to identify which AI tools employees use, what information those tools access, and whether their use complies with security policies.

"Organizations are adopting AI faster than they can secure it, and most security teams can't answer three basic questions: which AI tools are running, what data is going into them, and whether that activity lines up with policy," said Kyle Falkenhagen, senior vice president of product management at Sophos.

The company also cited an IDC projection that the number of AI agents could reach 1.3 billion by 2028.

Sophos researchers reported observing attackers use AI agents within a customer's network to build and test nearly 80 malware modules.

The cybersecurity firm announced the general availability of Sophos AI Defense, a security offering designed to identify AI tools, monitor their activity, and enforce organizational security policies.

The product operates through Sophos Fusion, the company's cybersecurity platform, and uses information from devices, networks, identity systems, and browsers.

According to the company, AI Defense can inspect prompts, uploads, and other AI interactions to identify potentially risky activity and help prevent sensitive data exposure.

For more information, go to the Sophos site.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

District and state policies run the gamut from embracing to banning AI. This raises a key question: When does AI accelerate student learning and when does it interfere?

#6986. Designers Say This High-Gloss Paint Trick Can Make Your Kitchen Look Instantly More Expensive

Pictured above: This New York City kitchen by designer Francis Nicdao features a high-gloss-painted island.

Once you’ve pinpointed the ideal color palette for your kitchen, you might think that the work is done. But not so fast. The success of your shades really comes down to finish, and there’s real potential in choosing a high-gloss paint. After all, if your kitchen is the showpiece of your home, you should make it shimmer.

“There is a richness to high-gloss paint that really can't be achieved with any other finish or product, especially in deeper colors,” says Malorie Goldberg, a principal designer at Noa Blake Design. “A high-gloss finish adds interest to cabinets that makes them feel more like furniture, immediately elevating the overall feel of the space.”

This option might be tough to imagine, given that kitchens are typically painted in a matte or eggshell finish. But that doesn’t mean that a shinier upgrade is out of the question. “High-gloss kitchens can be in a small or large space. For a smaller space, a butler's pantry might be a great place to play,” notes Danielle Davis, founder and principal designer of 56th Street Design Studio. “A larger kitchen will likely have a lot of millwork, so high-gloss paint can be applied to surfaces where it can really shine.”

If you want to make a statement in your kitchen using the budget-friendly quality of paint, a high-gloss finish could be the right move. Here’s how the pros recommend pulling it off.

When you’re considering a high-gloss finish, think of how it can be used to complement the rest of your kitchen. If you’re leaning toward trending darker palettes—like chocolate browns, emerald greens, and deep plums—then a high gloss will help bounce light around the room and make undertones more noticeable throughout the day. But if you’re doing just fine with a neutral backdrop, then a high-gloss will only make that look brighter. Either way, “high gloss” is paint speak for highlighter.

“The way that light reflects off a high-gloss finish adds a brightness that can make the space feel more expansive, even in moodier tones,” says Ariel Fischer, who is also a principal at Noa Blake Design.

Fischer and Goldberg would likely pick a darker shade on kitchen cabinetry for this effect, which Goldberg notes works on ultramodern panels and more ornate fronts. But Jen Levy, interior designer at Surrounded by Color, says it’s OK to go lighter—while looking up. “I love a high gloss ceiling in a kitchen,” Levy says. “My team and I see it used either way, but my preference is to have this paint treatment punctuate a lighter, brighter space.”

Paint is often regarded as DIY-friendly, but choosing this finish probably shouldn’t involve you rolling up your sleeves. “Hire a professional,” Levy says. “High gloss involves a lot of prep work to look right.” Since the results should almost resemble a mirror, Davis echoes this advice. “If a surface isn't perfectly sanded or smooth, high-gloss will show those imperfections,” she says. “It will ultimately take away from that perfect finish you’re after.”

As far as the best location for high-gloss paint, Davis has one strong preference. “High-gloss cabinets, from a practical standpoint, are forgiving in terms of wear,” she says. “You can easily wipe fingerprints, spills, and food right off the cabinet with a microfiber cloth.” Keep in mind, though, that chips will be noticeable, so this probably isn’t the right finish for busy households with young children.

As you determine the remaining details of your kitchen with a high-gloss paint in mind, remember to keep it front and center. Everything else in this space has to complement that sheen in order for the effect to be successful. “In a rustic kitchen, or one where natural wood is prevalent, high-gloss paint may clash with the overall vibe,” Davis says. The opposite is also true: A modern kitchen with too many shiny surfaces will feel like a carnival fun house.

To pull your kitchen together, seek out countertops and flooring with a matte finish alongside lighting and hardware that has a slight sheen. The goal is to find ways to ground the room, even as you center its high-gloss boldness. “I would love to have a client who’s daring enough to do a high-gloss kitchen,” Davis says. “Nevertheless, high-gloss paint can still have a timeless quality, while still having a big impact on the room.”

#6984. How 12 Famous Watches Got Their Nicknames

From the 'Steve McQueen' Rolex to the 'Holy Grail' Omega.

A few weeks ago, Casio unveiled a new range of fully analogue G-Shock watches, billed as its biggest launch in decades. But what immediately caught collectors' imaginations wasn't the technology inside the new 2500 Series. It was the shape of the cases, whose rounded rectangular outlines bear more than a passing resemblance to the Patek Philippe Nautilus.

There’s precedent here. When Casio’s eight-sided GA-2100 appeared in 2019, enthusiasts immediately clocked its likeness to Audemars Piguet's Royal Oak and christened it the 'CasiOak'. The nickname stuck and has since become shorthand for the entire family. (Perhaps this is what happens when you launch watches with names like GA-2100.)

So what to call the new Nautilus lookalike? Suggestions have included “Nauty-Less” and “CasiNau,” but “CasioNaut” seems to have won out.

Elsewhere in recent watch news, Seiko has been having fun with another nickname. Most watch fans will be familiar with the term “panda,” used to describe a chronograph with a white main dial and two black sub-dials, an arrangement said to resemble the face of the bamboo-munching bear. Seiko's new Power Design Project Panda takes the resemblance to its logical conclusion, with actual panda eyes, ears and, even a little wagging tail on the case.

And next week, Piaget will release a new version of its Andy Warhol watch, a name collectors had been using for decades before the brand finally made it official in 2024, following an agreement with the Andy Warhol Foundation.

Clearly, the peculiar business of giving watches nicknames is alive and well. Which gives us an excuse, as if one were needed, to run through a dozen great watch nicknames and the stories behind them.

Some are named after astronauts, actors, and models. Others have more unusual stories, involving a collaboration that never happened, a celebrity who almost certainly never wore the watch named after him, and an American president who wasn't even allowed to accept his watch.

Piaget ref. 15102 and its modern descendants

In the 1970s, Piaget was making some of the most extravagant watches money could buy, with chunky gold cases, colorful stone dials, and an impressive list of celebrity customers. Among them was Andy Warhol, who owned at least seven Piagets, including a large, cushion-shaped gold model, reference 15102, which he bought in 1973. (Four of his watches are now in Piaget’s collection.)

Six years later, Warhol met Yves Piaget, the founder’s great-grandson, and the two became friends. Warhol joined the Piaget Society, the glamorous circle of artists, designers, and celebrities cultivated by the company.

Introduced in 1972, the 15102 was produced for just five years before disappearing from Piaget’s catalogue. The design returned in 2014 under the name Black Tie, although collectors had long since nicknamed the original the “Andy Warhol.” Ten years later, the company finally made the name official, signing a licensing agreement with the Andy Warhol Foundation for the Visual Arts in 2024.

Piaget has since developed a collection bearing the artist’s name, with designs that draw on his taste for unconventional colors and materials. Among the first was a Clou de Paris edition featuring a blue meteorite dial surrounded by rows of tiny gold pyramids.

Warhol’s association has done vintage prices no harm, either. In May this year, an example resembling his own sold at Sotheby’s in Geneva for CHF166,399 – about $200,000.

Omega Speedmaster ref. 311.30.42.30.01.004

The Omega Speedmaster “Tintin” takes its name from a collaboration with the Belgian cartoon character that never quite happened.

In 2013, Omega released a version of its Speedmaster Professional chronograph (officially called the Racing) with an unusual red-and-white checkered pattern around the edge of its black dial. It was inspired by the rocket in Hergé’s Tintin adventures Destination Moon and Explorers on the Moon.

The original plan was to produce the watch in partnership with the owners of the Tintin rights. Early prototypes even featured the rocket on the dial, with a special case back to match. But the deal fell through, and Omega went ahead without the Tintin branding, leaving a rather curious design detail whose inspiration the company couldn't openly celebrate.

The story came to light when Robert-Jan Broer, founder of the watch website Fratello, spoke to Omega’s head of product development. Once collectors understood the connection, the “Tintin” nickname stuck. Illustrations from the comics accompanying Fratello’s coverage were subsequently removed at the request of the rights holder.

That said, the owners weren't opposed to putting the character on a watch altogether. Omega’s Swatch Group stablemate Swatch had already produced two official Tintin models for the character’s 75th anniversary in 2004, including a Happy Birthday Tintin edition limited to 9,999 pieces. Omega simply never managed to get its own collaboration over the line.

Seiko 6139, particularly the yellow-dial 6139-6005

Omega may have the Moonwatch, but Seiko can lay claim to the first automatic chronograph worn in space. And unlike the Omegas issued by Nasa, the Seiko in question was bought by the astronaut himself.

In June 1972, US Air Force pilot William Pogue paid $71 for a yellow-dial Seiko 6139 chronograph. He was training for NASA’s Skylab program and needed a watch to time engine burns. Like other astronauts, Pogue was due to receive an Omega Speedmaster, the chronograph Nasa had qualified for spaceflight, but his hadn't yet been delivered.

By the time his Omega arrived, however, Pogue had become so attached to the Seiko that he decided to take it into space as well.

When Skylab 4 launched in November 1973, the Seiko travelled in a pocket of Pogue’s spacesuit. During the mission’s 84 days in orbit, he wore it on his left wrist, with the Omega on his right. (The Seiko remained set to US Central Time.)

For years, the Seiko’s part in space history went largely unknown. Then American watch collector David Bruno spotted a reference to it in a mission transcript and contacted Pogue to find out what he'd worn. In 2008, the astronaut consigned the watch through the Astronaut Scholarship Foundation. It sold at Heritage Auctions for a surprisingly modest $5,975, complete with its original receipt and Pogue’s handwritten account.

The original Seiko 6139 has long been discontinued, but yellow-dial examples are now widely known as “Pogues.” They're still relatively affordable, too. Seiko has since revisited the look with the solar-powered Prospex SSC947, but it's an interpretation rather than a direct reissue.

There is one problem with the Rolex Explorer II colloquially known as the “Steve McQueen.” Nobody has been able to establish that Steve McQueen ever wore one.

His Rolex of choice was a Submariner. Yet the Explorer II association has become so firmly established that even Sotheby’s used the nickname when offering an example in May 2022, while the description below acknowledged that no convincing evidence existed for the connection. (Who first decided to attach the actor’s name to the watch remains a mystery.)

In fact, the same model has another nickname with an easier-to-fathom explanation. Freccione is Italian for “big arrow,” referring to the whopping orange 24-hour hand that dominates its dial.

Introduced in 1971, the Explorer II was designed for cave explorers and others who might spend extended periods without natural daylight. Its extra hand completed one revolution every 24 hours, allowing the wearer to distinguish day from night.

With its fixed steel bezel, busy dial, and that unmistakable orange hand, the original Explorer II looks quite unlike most of Rolex’s better-known models. It has since become a firm favorite with collectors.

In 1987, Omega introduced a Speedmaster that put a new spin on the familiar Moonwatch. The ref. 376.0822 featured an automatic movement, a day and date display, a 24-hour indicator, and an additional hand to count elapsed minutes. It made for a busy, rather technical-looking watch, and one that wasn't destined for a long production run.

Its relative scarcity would eventually catch the attention of American collector and Omega expert Chuck Maddox.

In 1999, Maddox was offered a 376.0822 in Germany for around $600. At roughly the same time, another Speedmaster appeared for sale in Iowa at a similar price. Faced with a choice, he went for the Iowa watch, reasoning that dealing with a seller he could visit in person was less risky than buying from overseas.

Almost immediately, he regretted passing up the German example. Finding another proved far more difficult than he had expected. A watch appeared in Japan with a price tag of more than $10,000. Another opportunity slipped away at auction. Eventually, with help from a fellow enthusiast, he managed to track one down.

Maddox later wrote about his hunt on his website and in online watch forums, comparing his obsession with finding the elusive Speedmaster to King Arthur’s search for the Holy Grail. (Watch collectors have never knowingly undersold a story.) He began referring to the watch simply as “the Grail,” and the nickname caught on among fellow collectors.

It's a tale that will be painfully familiar to anyone who's ever collected watches: the one that got away, and the lengths you'll go to for a second chance to spend a load of money.

When quartz technology arrived in the late 1960s, its promise was greater accuracy and, eventually, affordable watches for almost everyone. Rolex, naturally, took a different path.

Its first quartz watch, launched in 1970, was available exclusively in gold. The white-gold version of the ref. 5100 cost 18,300 Swiss francs before tax. According to Rolex expert James Dowling, that was roughly the price of a Porsche 912 and two-and-a-half times what the company charged for a Day-Date on its President bracelet.

Inside was the Beta 21, an early Swiss quartz movement developed jointly by a consortium of manufacturers. The technology was remarkable enough, but the watch looked like no Rolex that had come before it. Its large, angular case, broad bezel and integrated gold bracelet were a world away from the familiar designs of the period.

Italian collectors took to calling it the “Texano,” or “Texan,” presumably in recognition of its considerable size and extravagance.

Only around 1,000 numbered examples are thought to have been made, mostly in yellow gold. More than half a century later, the “Texano” remains as rare and extravagant as ever.

Nothing to do with glamorous magazine models, as it happens. The nickname comes from a book published in 2009 by Zenith historian and collector Manfred Rössler, who chose a photograph of the El Primero A3818 for the cover of Zenith: Swiss Watch Manufacture Since 1865. His reason for picking that particular watch? He simply liked blue dials. Fair enough.

Introduced in 1971, the A3818 was a 37mm steel chronograph with an angular case housing Zenith's celebrated El Primero movement. Its vertically brushed blue dial featured contrasting sub-dials and distinctive shark's-tooth markings around the edge.

Only 1,000 examples were made, according to Zenith, and the model had long since disappeared from production when Rössler's book brought it back to collectors' attention. Zenith revived the design in 2020, with a lightweight titanium Airweight edition following in 2022. Both had “Cover Girl” as part of their official names.

It doesn't get much more ’60s than Finnish fashion model Nina Rindt, married to Austrian Formula One driver Jochen Rindt and a regular fixture in the paddock during the sport's glamorous heyday. Photographs from the late 1960s show her watching races from the pit lane, often wearing a white-dial Universal Genève Compax chronograph on a wide leather cuff. The combination of glamorous wearer, racing connection, and period photographs proved irresistible to watch collectors, who began calling the model the “Nina Rindt.” Its black-dial counterpart acquired the even better moniker “Evil Nina.”

The nicknames helped transform the watch's fortunes. In the early 2010s, as collectors became increasingly interested in vintage chronographs with famous associations, prices for the “Nina Rindt” rose dramatically. As Eric Wind, founder of Wind Vintage and a former Christie's watch specialist, recalled: “That watch went from $3,000 ordinarily and was worth $40,000 within a year.” One particularly fine example sold at Christie's in 2015 for $47,500.

When Universal Genève relaunched in April this year, the Compax was central to its comeback, with new chronographs inspired by both versions. The watches now have automatic movements but retain the contrasting sub-dials of the originals—and are even offered on the wide leather cuff that helped make Nina's watch so recognizable.

In the 1983 film Octopussy, Roger Moore's James Bond wore a Seiko G757 Sports 100 that had been given a special upgrade by Q Branch. Its small circular digital display functioned as a tracking screen, allowing Bond to follow a transmitter concealed inside a Fabergé egg being sought by the film's villain, Kamal Khan.

Almost 30 years later, in 2012, Casio introduced the AE-1200, an inexpensive digital world timer with a striking resemblance to Bond's gadget. It had a similarly angular case, multiple digital displays and, most importantly, a little circular clock that seemed to recall the tracking screen on Moore's Seiko.

Enthusiasts spotted the resemblance and came up with a pun on Casino Royale, the 2006 Bond film starring Daniel Craig. Thus the “Casio Royale” was born, a nickname involving a different watch, a different manufacturer, and an entirely different Bond film.

The AE-1200 remains in Casio's catalog today, costing $55 and looking much as it did in 2012. Its miniature world map indicates the selected time zone, while the little circular display shows the time. It also has five alarms, a stopwatch, and a countdown timer. The Fabergé egg tracker is not included.

By 1972, Bulova was advertising the water resistance of its Oceanographer dive watch with the words “666 FEET” printed prominently on the dial. That's approximately 203 metres, although quite why the company chose such an oddly specific figure rather than a round 200 meters isn't entirely clear.

Given the number's rather ominous biblical associations, it didn't take long for enthusiasts to christen the watch the alliteratively pleasing “Devil Diver.”

The Oceanographer had other things going for it, too. The original was a handsome example of early-1970s dive-watch design, with a substantial cushion-shaped case, a rotating bezel, and a dial with extravagantly bold markers intended to be easily read underwater.

In 2017, Bulova teamed up with New York vintage-watch dealer Analog/Shift to gauge interest in reviving one of its archive designs. The Oceanographer came out on top. A memorable nickname with a whiff of danger presumably didn't hurt.

The watch duly returned in 2018, including an orange-dial edition limited to, naturally, 666 pieces. Since then, Bulova has produced smaller versions and an Oceanographer GMT capable of tracking a second time zone.

More than 50 years later, Bulova still prints “666 FEET” on the dial, although its official specifications now give the water resistance as a rather neater 200 meters.

Audemars Piguet Royal Oak Offshore ref. 25721

“You have spoilt my watch, you're a murderer!”

According to designer Emmanuel Gueit, this was Gérald Genta's reaction when he first saw the Royal Oak Offshore, a considerably beefier version of the Royal Oak he had designed for Audemars Piguet in 1972.

AP had been hoping to recapture some of the impact of the original Royal Oak, which had caused a sensation with its unusually large steel case, integrated bracelet, and extravagant price. By the early 1990s, the company wanted something to appeal to a younger audience, and Gueit was given the job.

His answer, launched in 1993, was the Royal Oak Offshore. The original's 39mm case grew to 42mm, enormous by the standards of the day. It was thicker, too, with a chronograph movement, rubber-coated crown and pushers, and a conspicuous gasket visible beneath the bezel. The Offshore name came from powerboat racing, a suitably muscular association for such an imposing watch.

The industry quickly christened it “The Beast.” Genta himself reportedly compared it to a sea elephant, which, frankly, would have made an even better nickname.

Even within Audemars Piguet, enthusiasm was mixed. The company's archive records that some insiders hoped the project would fail, while the first hundred examples had only Royal Oak engraved on their case backs, without the Offshore name. Hardly a ringing vote of confidence.

Customers eventually came around, helped by celebrity associations, particularly with Arnold Schwarzenegger. The Offshore went on to help establish the fashion for oversized luxury watches, which must have had Genta sobbing even harder into his fondue.

Omega Speedmaster Apollo XI ref. BA145.022-69

When Neil Armstrong and Buzz Aldrin landed on the moon in July 1969, Omega had good reason to celebrate. Its Speedmaster chronographs had accompanied NASA astronauts throughout the Apollo program, and the company decided to mark the achievement by making 1,014 special gold editions.

These were considerably more luxurious than the steel watches issued to the astronauts, with 18-carat yellow-gold cases, bracelets, and dials, plus burgundy bezels. The first two were intended for President Richard Nixon and Vice President Spiro Agnew, complete with individual engravings.

There was just one problem. American rules governing gifts to public officials meant neither man could accept an expensive gold watch from a Swiss manufacturer. Both were returned to Omega, where they remain in the company's museum in Switzerland today.

Other recipients were luckier. Astronauts received their watches at a gala dinner in Houston in November 1969, while most of the remaining production was offered for public sale. Collectors subsequently nicknamed the reference the “Nixon,” after the president who never got to wear his.

The watches have since become highly collectible, particularly those with a connection to the Apollo astronauts. An example sold at Bonhams in 2024 for HK$384,000 [just under $50K], while Neil Armstrong's own gold Speedmaster, number 17, fetched $2.125 million in April last year.

Of course, Omega couldn't resist reviving the design, producing another 1,014 examples in its Moonshine gold alloy in 2019. But the originals have an appeal that can never quite be replicated, combining as they do mid-century space-age glamour with a whiff of Nixonian roguishness.

#6982. Microsoft Expands AI Security Platform to Address Agentic AI Security Risks

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

In a company blog post, Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security, outlined how Microsoft plans to use Project Perception to monitor, manage, and contain what it calls "agentic insiders." These are AI agents deployed within an environment that can access data, use software tools, and make decisions without constant human supervision. While they can improve productivity, Microsoft warns that they also introduce security risks, even when they haven't been compromised by an attacker.

"Agents are unpredictable," Gallot wrote. "They will adapt as the context changes, may not behave as intended, and will collaborate and collude with other agents to reach their objectives, without needing to be compromised."

Project Perception was first introduced in July. The platform, which entered public preview Aug. 3, is designed to help security teams respond to increasingly sophisticated cyber threats using AI-powered automation.

Instead of leaning only on traditional security alerts and manual investigations, Project Perception uses three kinds of specialized AI agents working together. Red team agents hunt for possible vulnerabilities, blue team agents look into suspicious activity, and green team agents step in to fix problems and strengthen defenses.

To spot threats and respond faster, the system pulls in security data from identities, endpoints, cloud environments, applications, and other sources.

Now Microsoft wants to use those same capabilities on the AI agents already running inside enterprise networks. Project Perception would find those agents, give them identities and permissions, and keep an eye on what they do across different systems. It would also keep assessing the risks that come with their actions.

Microsoft also described a plan for "Guardian Agents," specialized AI agents that supervise other agents and step in when their behavior gets risky. That might mean limiting an agent's permissions or blocking certain actions before they cause harm.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," Gallot wrote.

The initiative also reflects concerns raised by Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella in an Oct. 10 essay on AI security.

Nadella argued that enterprises should treat powerful AI models much like potential insider security risks, particularly when those models can access confidential information or perform critical business operations.

"We simply can't outsource responsibility for what intelligence does on our behalf," Nadella wrote.

He called for security controls that operate independently of AI models, including restricted permissions, detailed activity logging, and mechanisms that allow administrators to stop an agent's actions.

"In other words, we need to separate the supply of intelligence from the authority over it," Nadella wrote.

Microsoft admits that watching what AI agents do is only part of the answer. Gallot said that future security tools will also need to show why AI models make the choices they do, not just what actions they end up taking.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

Artificial intelligence is raising the stakes of cyber conflict as attackers use the technology to accelerate reconnaissance, uncover vulnerabilities, and launch attacks faster than many security teams can respond, according to a new report from agentic AI security company Kai.

Cybersecurity companies Rubrik and CrowdStrike have announced an expanded identity-security integration designed to move compromised identity environments from threat detection through clean recovery with less manual intervention.

Microsoft has shut down EvilTokens, a cybercrime service that used AI to help attackers break into e-mail accounts, study their victims, and build convincing financial scams.

Video game designers spend an enormous amount of time studying what keeps people engaged when something becomes difficult. They have become very good at helping people persist. Schools should pay attention.

→ Baca artikel asli

#6979. Tom Hardy’s Hair Through The Years—and How to Get Every Cut

Three decades, one head, a lot of styles. We’ll help you nail every look with advice from the pros.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

You can track Tom Hardy’s life through his haircuts. The floppy, past-the-ears noughties look. The bald Bane era. His Peaky Blinders slick-back. Then there’s the fade he wore on The Revenant press tour and the short-back-and-sides he’s settled into today. He’s been on a proper hair journey over the past couple of decades and, somehow, he’s looked great on every stop along the way.

Really, he’s done exactly what every man should do when it comes to hair. You should never have the same haircut at 60 as when you’re 16, so changing it up is essential. Sure, some, for Hardy, were for his roles. He probably wouldn’t have gone for the full shaved look if he hadn’t been cast as a masked supervillain who breaks Batman’s back. But, even so, his go-to now is a long way from the boyband look he wore when he was younger.

So, we asked some of London’s best barbers how to get each cut, as well as who each one suits. If you’re anything like Hardy, you’ll suit all six.

The haircut: Think of this as the slick-back’s more laid-back cousin. “It's a classic textured scissor cut with a low, natural finish,” says Robyn Mulgrew, a senior barber at Ruffians. “It’s kept soft around the ears and nape, with the sides relatively full and enough length to sweep it back.” No harsh fades or sharp lines, just a cut that looks like it grew naturally—and it’s a versatile shape that works for everything from oval to square and round faces.

What to ask for: Mulgrew’s advice? Keep it simple: “Don’t overcomplicate it, or you’ll confuse each other,” she says. “Use these key words: textured scissor cut with a low natural taper, swept-back top, styled to give a soft, lived-in finish.” All you need to do now is read it out with the help of a Hardy reference photo—job done.

How to style it: The idea is for your hair to look naturally lifted rather than quiffed. “I'd prep with Ruffians Beach Salt Spray, then blow-dry it in, sweeping the hair back and up,” says Mulgrew. “Keep the sides flatter so it still looks smart, then finish with out Matt Clay or Beach Fix for some texture.” Think swept-back beach hair, just the commuter’s version.

The haircut: Short, sharp, and no messing about, the soft buzz is for the guys who are confident with clippers. “This is pretty close to a buzz cut, approximately a grade three on top and a grade two on the sides, with a very subtle taper around the edges,” says Maxwell Oakley, co-founder of luxury London barbershop Supply 91. Fair warning, though: there’s nowhere to hide. “It’s quite an exposing haircut, so it works particularly well on someone with a strong hairline,” he says. Square and oval faces with a strong jawline suit it best—but Oakley’s not one for hard rules: “If you wear something with enough confidence, you can pull anything off.”

What to ask for: “Ask your barber to use clippers rather than scissors,” says Oakley. “A grade three on top and a grade two on the sides, with a subtle taper through the edges; you could also mention to lightly break into the fringe with the clippers to keep the front looking natural and textured.” Though it might seem simple, it’s so precise that you need someone who knows exactly what they’re doing—so always stick to a good barbershop.

How to style it: Less is more. “Avoid anything wet or greasy, as that can make the hair look sparse,” says Oakley. “Keep things matte to bring out the natural texture, with just a little bit of hold; work a small amount of product through for some definition without weighing it down.” Basically, a tiny bit of paste rubbed between your palms and through your hair, and you’re out the door before you can say “buzz cut.”

The haircut: Brent Pankhurst has been cutting hair for more than 30 years, from training under Vidal Sassoon to his shop in the Thom Sweeney townhouse in Mayfair, so he knows a good crop when he sees one. "It’s disheveled elegance,” he says. “It has a relaxed, slightly rugged feel, but the foundation is a strong, carefully considered shape.” It suits most faces, too, as long as the proportions are tailored to you. “For gentlemen approaching their forties and beyond, I often prefer a little more length through the sides for a softer, less harsh finish,” Pankhurst adds.

What to ask for: It all starts with the temples: “The most important thing with this haircut—and, for me, every man’s haircut—is to cut a square shape through the temple area,” says Pankhurst. Ask for scissors throughout, with scissor-over-comb on the back and sides, which “gives the haircut better structure and helps it hold its shape as it grows.” You don’t want to be going to the barbers every couple of weeks, after all. The top should be cut with texture for movement and separation; if your hair is thick, ask for more texturing to take out some bulk.

How to style it: Work some styling conditioner through damp hair, then give it a quick blow-dry using your fingers. Finish with a nickel-sized amount of molding cream. “Spread it evenly between your palms, then work it vigorously through the hair for that rugged, disheveled finish,” says Pankhurst.

The haircut: The slick-back, minus the Wall Street energy. “This is a textured slick-back or flow cut,” says Chris Merrick, head barber at Murdock Soho. “Keeping it relaxed rather than tightly slicked allows the natural movement and texture to come through.” Basically, it should look like you’ve just run your hands through it rather than set it hours earlier. It suits most faces, too. “It works particularly well on oval, square and rounder face shapes; styling the hair back opens up the face and highlights the cheekbones, while some height on top can help elongate a rounder face.”

What to ask for: “Ask your barber for a textured slick-back or flow cut, with the sides slightly shorter than the top but enough length throughout to push the hair back,” says Merrick. “You want to retain movement and avoid a traditional short back and sides.”

How to style it: Towel dry your hair after showering, leaving it slightly damp, then work in a sea salt spray. “Blow-dry while using your fingers to guide the hair backwards and encourage natural movement,” says Merrick. Finish with a small amount of Murdock Matt Mud, worked between your hands and through the hair for a strong hold with a matte finish.

The haircut: Think of this as the haircut equivalent of rolling out of bed and looking like you meant to. “This is a classic textured cut with a messy, tousled finish,” says Guray Kesman, founder of luxury barbershop Kesman Grooming, “and it’s got that effortlessly undone look that Tom Hardy wears so well.” It’s also one of the most versatile cuts going: the fringe softens the forehead, so it suits oval, square, and longer faces, and it works best on straight to slightly wavy hair with some thickness.

What to ask for: “Ask for a scissor-cut textured crop, keeping the sides and back shorter but avoiding a harsh skin fade,” says Kesman. “You’ll want a good amount of length and movement through the top, with the fringe left slightly longer. The key is asking your barber to add texture rather than cutting everything too uniformly, so the hair has that relaxed, lived-in finish.”

How to style it: Work a small amount of lightweight styling cream or matte paste through slightly damp hair, then rough-dry it, directing the fringe forwards and slightly to one side. “Use your fingers to separate a few pieces,” says Kesman. “You don’t want it looking too polished or overly styled; a little movement is what makes this cut work.” When it looks like you’ve spent as little time as possible trying to perfect it, you’ve nailed it.

The haircut: If you were around in the ’90s, chances are you’re familiar with curtains—and you probably have the school photos to prove it. “To me, Tom Hardy’s curtains were something of an homage to Jonny Depp,” says Jay Hecker, master barber at Truefitt & Hill, the world’s oldest barbershop based in St. James’s, London. “We've seen this look before—it was everywhere when Brad Pitt broke though with Thelma & Louise and when David Beckham wore it in the ’90s—and you can trace it right back to old Hollywood’s Robert Mitchum.” Now, it’s making a comeback—and it suits most face shapes. “Because the hair is tucked behind the ears, face shape isn’t too much of a factor,” continues Hecker. “And if you want to accentuate your cheekbones, it’s definitely one to consider.”

What to ask for: “Ask for layers at the back to take out any weight, and keep most of the length on top so you can tuck it behind your ears,” says Hecker. A center part takes care of the rest. If you’re growing it out from a shorter cut, tell your barber so they can shape it in stages—just make sure you take a visual reference so you’re both on the same page.

How to style it: “Apply a cream like our C.A.R. cream or Circassian Cream to towel-dried hair,” says Hecker. “Then use a diffuser on your hairdryer on medium to low speed and heat for that textured finish.” The C.A.R. Cream has some history, too: Truefitt & Hill made it in the early 1900s for members of the Royal Automobile Club to keep their hair in check while driving open-top cars. Your day at the office, then, will probably be safe.
