#6976. Survey: Organizations Report Positive ROI on AI Investments, Yet Data Readiness and Oversight Lag Behind

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

The company's recent State of Enterprise AI Maturity research surveyed 515 U.S.-based AI decision-makers. Just 5% believed their organizations' data was largely ready for AI.

The findings suggest that gaining value from individual AI applications and preparing to operate them more broadly are separate challenges. Reported returns alone do not establish that an organization has the foundations to sustain or expand those results.

Further found that 37% of respondents' organizations had paused, scaled back, or abandoned an underperforming AI initiative in the previous 12 months. Among that group, data quality or availability was the most frequently cited main reason, at 24%.

Meanwhile, 29% reported completing a formal data audit and remediation plan. The research also placed 69% of organizations in the exploring, experimenting, or implementing stages of adoption.

Further distinguishes data prepared for conventional reporting from data prepared for AI. Its explanation emphasizes data origins and documentation, along with access controls that support ongoing evaluation and governance.

For readers assessing their own readiness, the practical issue is whether data supports the requirements of a particular application. A collection suitable for a dashboard may need additional preparation before it can reliably support an AI system.

Seventy-seven percent reported a positive return on AI investment. However, only 3% said their organizations had a documented AI-audit process.

The findings measure different things: perceived financial benefit and the existence of a repeatable oversight process. Neither substitutes for the other.

"In reality, confidence without a documented process is exposure," said Kierra Dotson, associate director of AI strategy and governance at Further. "Closing that gap doesn't take new technology; it requires an auditable governance process."

For the full report, visit the Further site (registration required).

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

District and state policies run the gamut from embracing to banning AI. This raises a key question: When does AI accelerate student learning and when does it interfere?

#6975. 6 Pieces of Wearable Tech That Solve Real-World Problems

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Health tracking, AI-powered notes, and more—behold, the most useful accessories on the market.

At this point, it’s normal to have a wearable, something like an Apple Watch or Fitbit, or an Oura ring to track your sleep and activity levels. You’ve maybe also heard of Whoop, pioneer of the screenless tracker. At the end of the day, all these devices do the same thing to varying degrees of success. If your focus is on training then the Whoop band is made for you, if you’re outdoorsy you have Garmin, and if you want to get better sleep maybe try Oura. But if you have other, more specific goals, it might seem like there’s not a lot out there for you. But worry not, all that is changing, and it’s changing fast.

These devices represent the latest breakthroughs in wearable technology, going beyond the basics and swinging for the fences. From rings and bands dedicated to blood pressure monitoring to an AI note-taker that you clip on your lapel, these aren’t trying to become your new all-in-one wearables. Instead, they are each trying to be a tool for those who need it most. With an eye toward mindfulness and focus, instead of overwhelming with data, these unique wearables go beyond just tracking sleep and activity level. These devices will make you think maybe the future really is here, and not so bad after all.

If you have one of many medical conditions (I genuinely do) that requires you to monitor your blood pressure you know how annoying those cuffs can get. Hilo suggests a better solution; Hilo Core is dedicated to providing users with insights into their blood pressure over time. As a result, the system is a bit more complicated than just a single wearable with an app. That’s the main part, but the FDA-cleared blood pressure cuff is the third part of the equation. The three aspects of the Hilo work in tandem to give accurate, consistent blood pressure readings with as little invasiveness as possible.

To start, you need to use the cuff to take baseline readings for a few days but after that you should only have to use the cuff once per month to calibrate your readings. After that, you can rely on the Hilo Band itself for accurate and consistent readings thanks to its advanced optical sensors. All of that is viewable in graphs and charts that put your hourly, daily, and weekly readings into a greater context.

Hilo isn’t the only company making wearables that are trying to replace chunky old-fashioned blood pressure cuffs. And unlike Hilo, Signal even does it without needing any kind of cuff at any point. All you need is the single Signal ring, and no, there’s no subscription to pay.

Compared to an Oura ring and other similar watches and bands, the Signal Ring uses more advanced sensors to capture your systolic and diastolic readings to display your blood pressure in real time. It’s not measuring general cardiovascular strain or anything vague like that, it’s actually measuring blood pressure. And like with the Hilo, same situation; you can view everything on the app.

It’s worth noting the device itself is not yet FDA cleared (and neither is the Hilo Band to be clear). A 451-person study the company commissioned did meet the requirements of ISO 81060-2, the international accuracy standard for non-invasive blood pressure devices.

This isn’t a watch or ring, but it definitely works best as a wearable. Plaud makes AI powered audio recorders that can also automatically transcribe everything for you. As a writer and editor, this is somewhere AI could have saved me time in my intern days.

The NotePin S is the size of a lapel microphone and clips right on to your shirt just like one too. At least that’s my favorite way to wear it—feels professional. You can wear it like a necklace or in a wrist band too, all options that are possible with attachments that come in the box.

I’ve written about vagus nerve stimulators before. It’s an underwritten trend, and the mindfulness tech that’s been coming out over the past year or two has been embracing it. Essentially, the vagus nerve runs from the brainstem all the way down to, really, every other major organ in your body. vagus nerve stimulation devices send electrical signals that can supposedly fix many ailments. In my experience, they are great for relaxation and can help with anxiety and stress relief. But really, it’s meant to aid your parasympathetic nervous system. This impacts your digestion, and is also the source of your “fight or flight” response.

Like other handheld devices I’ve used, you have to apply a layer of electricity conducting gel before a session with the Pulsetto. After that, put it on (it’s adjustable to any neck size) and let the electrodes work their magic. It’s certainly a well-built, futuristic feeling piece of tech, but as with any of these actual results may vary.

Pulse takes a different approach to smart rings. It’s not about becoming the best version of yourself by optimizing your sleep, fitness, and recovery every second of the day. Instead, this is a ring that senses your stress levels throughout the day and gives you vibrations (pulses) when you might, based on your vitals, need a breather. Be it meditation or just a minute away to get your composure, sometimes taking this time can be the difference between turning a bad day around and having it go worse.

Like the Pulsetto device, Sona is making a vagus nerve stimulator. This one goes on your ear like a single earbud. From there it sends stimulating signals to your vagus nerve, no goop required. Once you’ve onboarded the device and set up your protocol, the promise is that the AI is always adapting to you and your body’s rhythms.

Sona’s focuses are stress relief and better sleep. Yes, you are supposed to sleep with it on. Full disclosure, I can’t say I’ve tried this one and for nearly $1,000 I don’t want to recommend it wholeheartedly without hands-on time. But the promise is... well promising, compared to other similar devices out there, even if the price makes it currently unattainable for most.

This is far from all that’s out there. I’ve seen smart rings like Kiwear that control your phone with a flick of your finger. Or similar rings that let you control the display on your smart glasses. I’ve tried exoskeletons, like the upcoming Hypershell Halo, that genuinely feel like a robot is moving your body for you. And that’s just scratching the surface—we have a long way to go, and plenty of concerns to address along the way, but wearable tech isn’t going anywhere.

#6973. How Often Should You Be Cleaning Your Leather Furniture? It's More Often Than You Think

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pictured above: A leather seat in a space designed by Jill Tran of Tran + Thomas Design Studio.

No one invests in a piece of leather furniture with plans to replace it again in just a few years. After all, a full-grain leather sofa can go decades without cracking, and minor scuffs simply add to the patina.

But the key lies in how well you care for your leather furniture. Leather may be lower maintenance than fabric upholstery, but it still needs occasional TLC to last.

We reached out to textile experts for tips on how to extend the life of leather furniture. Here's how often you should clean and condition these pieces, and how to tell the difference between normal patina and damage that needs attention.

Lisa Beach is the director of operations at BenchMade Modern, a furniture company that specializes in leather. According to her, cleaning your leather furniture regularly protects it from damage.

She recommends vacuuming your leather furniture and wiping it down with a dry cloth any time you notice dust, pet hair and dander, or crumbs starting to accumulate. This can range from every one to two weeks, depending on how often you use the furniture. The goal is to prevent these particles from settling into the furniture and possibly scratching the surface.

In general, she warns against using a damp cloth during these quick and frequent cleanings. "For semi-aniline or protected leathers, you may wipe down your furniture every few months with a damp cloth, but be sure to follow with a dry cloth to remove any excess moisture," she says. "Natural full-grain leathers are more absorbent, so we don't recommend using damp cloths or moisture unless you need to clean a spill or stain."

Leather furniture also benefits from a deeper clean with a specialized pH-balanced product every few months.

Shawn McGowen is the CEO of Leather Honey, a family-owned company that has been producing leather care products since 1968. He suggests blocking off time for a deep clean every three to six months, and following it up with a leather conditioner.

Leather naturally ages and develops a patina over time. McGowen says patinaed leather shows off a slightly darker color, softer sheen and smoother feel. You'll first notice this starting to develop in the places you sit and rest your arms most often.

"This is where your natural body oils slowly burnish the leather’s surface, giving that lived-in look that people seek after," he says. "On the flip side, damage often occurs due to neglect and looks more like cracking, flaking, stiffness, or wrinkling that doesn’t go away with cleaning. Patina comes from properly using your leather while damage comes from neglect."

Note that leather furniture can fade instead of darken with age if it sits in a particularly sunny room. However, this doesn't mean you need to keep the curtains drawn or move your leather furniture to a darker corner. McGowen says that at-home recoloring balms or leather dyes are very easy to apply to small areas.

Even if you're a very determined DIYer, it's important to know when it's best to reach out to a leather care professional to restore your furniture. Otherwise, you may end up doing more harm than good.

"When leather becomes too far gone and starts to show signs of neglect or wear such as tears, holes, large areas of discoloration, or stains, that is when the DIY becomes more difficult and potentially a waste of time," McGowen says. "These should often be handled by professional leather care specialists because they have years of experience in color matching and upholstery, and have a wide range of tools needed for larger restoration projects."

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6972. The 25 Sexiest Movies on Prime Video Right Now

Including the best erotic thrillers, raunchy comedies, and all the mess in between.

Even in just the past decade, sexy cinema has come a long way from Fifty Shades of Grey. No judgement here—the entire trilogy is available to stream on Prime Video if you haven’t seen the Dakota Johnson and Jamie Dornan-starring franchise. But if you’re looking to expand your range a bit, we have a lot to recommend.

Sexy movies are having a comeback in 2026, thanks to Wuthering Heights, I Want Your Sex, and—another Olivia Wilde film—The Invite. While Emerald Fennell adapted Emily Bronte’s novel into a tortured and depraved sexual experience, Wilde’s I Want Your Sex and The Invite used sex more as a vehicle for dark comedy. But it’s not just new films on this list. We can also point you to Weekend, Ghost, Before Sunrise, and just any kind of sexy movie you’re looking for on screen.

Below, you’ll find our pick of the 25 sexiest movies you can stream on Prime Video right now.

Olivia Wilde and Cooper Hoffman star in Gregg Araki’s raunchy workplace romantic comedy about a high profile artist (Wilde) who ropes one of her new assistants (Hoffman) into a dominant/submissive relationship. At first, he enjoys the attention, but the rollercoaster of their sexual power dynamic eventually drives them both insane.

Sydney Sweeney does Rear Window meets Peeping Tom in this sexy crime drama about a couple who becomes obsessed with the sex lives of the people they can see from their apartment window across the street. The film also stars Justice Smith and Ahsoka’s Natasha Liu Bordizzo.

No Hard Feelings follows a down-on-her-luck Uber driver (Jennifer Lawrence) who is hired by a very wealthy couple to take their inexperienced nineteen-year-old son (Andrew Barth Feldman) on a date to boost his confidence before he leaves for college. The film treads dangerous waters, but Lawrence won over the audience and earned herself a Golden Globe nomination for the sexy comedy.

Love her or hate her, Emerald Fennell can make a steamy movie. Wuthering Heights is a little more of a reimagining than an adaptation, taking quite a few liberties with the text, but it puts the romantic and sexual dynamics right at the forefront of the story. And there’s no beating Margot Robbie and Jacob Elordi as the leads.

The Fault in Our Stars director Josh Boone teams up with It Ends With Us novelist Colleen Hoover for a romance drama about a teen pregnancy that alters the course of two lovers’ lives. That is, until later in life, when the couple (Allison Wiliams and Dave Franco) reunite to explore what could have been.

Emerald Fennell’s steamy The Talented Mr. Ripley–esque thriller, Saltburn, follows a young student at Oxford University who becomes infatuated (read: dangerously obsessed) with one of his aristocratic classmates, who invites him to his family’s grand estate. It’s a darkly funny and wildly intense ride.

The phenom that saved dark romance novels: Fifty Shades of Grey and the entire Fifty Shades trilogy is available to stream on Prime Video. The story, for those who still don’t know, follows a recent college graduate (Dakota Johnson) who signs a contract to take part in a sexual bondage relationship with billionaire Christian Gray (Jamie Dornan).

L’inconnu du lac (known as Stranger by the Lake) is one of the most acclaimed erotic thrillers of the 21st century, picking up Best Director and the Queer Palm awards in the year it premiered at the Cannes Film Festival. In Stranger by the Lake, Franck, a man in his 30s, meets Michel by a lake where gay men often bathe and immediately falls for him. What he doesn’t know, however, is that Michel is a psychopathic murderer. Stranger by the Lake is as smart as it is sexy, and it will truly keep you on your toes.

Who said rom-coms can’t be sexy too? Secretary charts the dominant-submissive relationship that blossoms between a lawyer and his secretary. It’s certainly on the crazier side and may not be for everyone, but Secretary is a brilliantly acted film that feels quite far ahead of its time for such a mainstream movie in its original take on a romantic BDSM relationship.

Carol does something a little different: It’s a slow-burning romance with sensuality bubbling beneath the surface. It follows a department-store worker and aspiring photographer whose life takes an unexpected turn when she falls in love with a mysterious, beautiful older woman going through a difficult divorce. Todd Haynes’s Oscar-nominated period romance is a masterpiece of modern queer cinema, with career-defining performances from Rooney Mara and Cate Blanchett. Hugely romantic and remarkably executed, Carol is a special film that’ll certainly stick with you.

Alongside Four Weddings and a Funeral, Sirens was one of the films that brought generational British heartthrob Hugh Grant to American audiences. Grant plays a progressive reverend who, with his wife, pays a visit to an eccentric artist whose work is viewed as blasphemous. His wife soon finds herself drawn to the three models working with the painter, and what ensues is a clever breakdown of 1930s sexual repression.

Everyone loves a meet-cute, and Weekend’s is as brilliant as they come. Russell thinks he’s signed up for a one-night stand with Glen, a man he meets in a club, but over the course of a single weekend, a deeper relationship quickly grows between the two. Touching, oddly realistic, and distinctly British in its sense of humor, Weekend is sexy, funny, and grounded in a way that few romantic comedy-dramas manage.

In Eternity, Elizabeth Olsen plays a woman who, upon reaching the afterlife, is forced to choose between two people to spend eternity with: her first husband, who died young, and her second husband of 65 years. Eternity is infectiously sweet, a little sexy, and an essential modern comedy.

It might be a stretch to call this one “sexy,” per se, but you won’t find a romantic drama quite like this one—and you definitely won’t find one that’s more intense. Luca Guadagnino’s Bones and All is a road-trip drama that follows a young woman who develops feelings for a disillusioned young drifter on a 3,000-mile journey across America. Oh, and they’re both cannibals.

Wong Kar-wai’s romantic masterpiece proves that a film can be truly romantic, sensual, and intense while still holding solidly on to a PG rating. In the Mood for Love follows a man and a woman who discover that their spouses are having an affair with one another, but as they bond, they begin to develop their own connection. This is one for the yearners.

Sexy films are by no means a new phenomenon, and Caligula is one of the originals. Though the original was pretty poorly received, the film recently received a restoration and rerelease that reconstructed the film entirely from footage that didn’t make the final cut.

Love triangles don’t get much more complicated than this, and sports dramas don’t get much sexier. Challengers follows two friends who fall in love with the same young tennis prodigy and the complicated chaos that ensues when their relationships get messy. Tennis has never looked so thrilling

Set in the dreamy Italian countryside, Call Me by Your Name remains the film that introduced Timothée Chalamet to the masses as a future leading man (and got him his first Oscar nomination). Here he plays a teenager who falls in love with his father’s assistant and comes to terms with his sexuality. The film is sad, symbolic, and deeply romantic.

The legendary leading duo of Patrick Swayze and Demi Moore speaks for itself. Ghost is maybe a little more on the sentimental side than some of the other “sexy films” in Prime Video’s lineup, but it’s a deeply moving one worth your time.

Portrait of a Lady on Fire was an immediate hit with critics and audiences when it was released in 2019. The film follows an artist commissioned to paint an aristocratic heiress, but things become more complicated when the two fall in love. Romantic films don’t get more French than this one, which is also loosely based on the timeless tale of Orpheus and Eurydice.

Nicole Kidman and Harris Dickinson are an absolutely electrifying onscreen pair in this erotic thriller that sees a CEO begin an affair with her power-hungry intern, putting the stability of her family life and career on the line. Kidman picked up the Best Actress award at the Venice Film Festival for her role, and it’s been called one of her most ambitious performances to date.

Can anything capture your heart like a meet-cute romance? American traveler Jesse and French student Céline unceremoniously meet on a train from Budapest to Vienna, deciding on a whim to spend his last hours on the continent together. It’s the first in Richard Linklater’s acclaimed Before trilogy, and it sets you up for a brilliant slow burn.

If a Skarsgård signed up for it, I’m interested. He’s about to play a love interest made out of wicker, but last year Alexander Skarsgård played the mysteriously sexy biker-gang leader Ray in Pillion. The romantic black comedy tells the story of a shy man who begins a dominant-submissive relationship with the aforementioned biker, and it traces his personal growth as he navigates their dynamic. Pillion is sexy, crazy, and downright hilarious. It’s British black comedy at its finest.

Starring the king of crazy, surprisingly sexy indie movies, James Spader himself, Sex, Lies and Videotape is a true cult classic. The film revolves around a married couple with intimacy problems whose lives are changed when a mysterious drifter with a peculiar interest comes to stay with them.

David Cronenberg’s notorious erotic thriller is perhaps the most transgressive entry on this list, following a group of people who become sexually fixated on car crashes. Adapted from J.G. Ballard’s novel and starring James Spader, Holly Hunter, and Deborah Kara Unger, Crash is disturbing, provocative, and about as daring as erotic cinema gets.

#6971. Researchers Find AI Is Accelerating Vulnerability Discovery

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In a recent blog post, the company stated that in September 2026, vendors addressed 998 common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVEs) around Patch Tuesday, compared with 96 during the same period last year. In addition, the three-month rolling average of patched Linux kernel vulnerabilities tripled year over year, while Google's Chrome browser experienced a 36-fold increase.

The researchers point to advanced AI models, including Anthropic's Claude Mythos Preview, as a major factor behind the increase in vulnerability discovery.

Introduced in April 2026, Mythos Preview is being used through Project Glasswing, an initiative involving Microsoft, the Linux Foundation and other organizations to identify vulnerabilities in critical software.

While AI could ultimately make software more secure, the cybersecurity company warns that vulnerability discovery is accelerating faster than organizations can deploy fixes.

IT teams must still evaluate patches, test compatibility and schedule updates without disrupting critical business operations.

The challenge is particularly significant for organizations already struggling with patch management.

The cybersecurity vendor cites Verizon's 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, which found that 35% of known actively exploited vulnerabilities remained unpatched after 28 days in 2025.

Ryan Powell, vice president of IT and security at ThreatDown, said automated patching systems can help organizations manage the growing workload. However, additional patches still require more testing and validation, increasing operational overhead and the risks associated with frequent system changes.

The report also highlights the risks of vulnerabilities in widely used software components such as OpenSSL, embedded in applications, network devices, and security products.

Unlike standalone applications, these components often depend on downstream vendors to release updates before organizations can deploy fixes.

The security firm's analysis points to CVE-2022-0778, an OpenSSL vulnerability patched in March 2022, as an example. Some affected products did not receive updates until several months later.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, these dependencies could delay remediation and extend organizations' exposure to known security flaws.

ThreatDown's findings illustrate a growing challenge for enterprise cybersecurity: AI is making vulnerabilities easier to discover but fixing them remains a complex and time-consuming process.

For the full report, visit the ThreatDown blog here.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

District and state policies run the gamut from embracing to banning AI. This raises a key question: When does AI accelerate student learning and when does it interfere?

#6967. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

#6966. This Charming 1960’s Alabama Cottage Unites Classic Southern Style With Family Heirlooms

There’s room for everyone, grandchildren included.

The best houses keep family close, whether that means an expansive living room that fits all the siblings and their spouses, room for another generation under the same roof, or grandparents just a few minutes down the road. For Leigh and Kevin Misso, founders of River Brook Design & Construction, that sentiment became the starting point for a deeply personal project: transforming a dated 1960s house in Mountain Brook, Alabama, into a new home for Leigh's mother and stepfather, who moved to be closer to their grandchildren.

At 2,600 square feet, the three-bedroom house wasn’t particularly grand, but that was precisely its appeal. “We wanted to embrace its modest scale rather than fight against it,” says Leigh. The couple undertook a complete renovation, adding square footage, vaulting ceilings, and reimagining the once-cramped kitchen as an airy gathering place. Throughout, the goal was to preserve the home’s cottage-like charm while giving it the breathing room to accommodate a growing family. “Every detail was thoughtfully considered to make this a place where children and grandchildren feel at home, generations can gather, and meaningful family traditions and memories can be made for years to come,” adds Leigh.

The resulting interiors are crisp and refined, while still holding decades of family history. Leigh’s grandmother's antique sideboard anchors the dining room, while her wicker sofa finds new life on the sunroom porch, dressed in blue-and-white Les Touches fabric. A four-poster bed passed down through her stepfather’s family takes pride of place in the primary bedroom. Elsewhere, mustard velvet, richly colored built-ins, and playful painted stripes add a fresh perspective to traditional Southern decorating.

Of course, the house’s greatest luxury might be its expansive rear porch, where a custom hanging daybed beckons, and where Leigh’s mother spends much of her time. It was one of the renovation’s biggest investments, but for a family whose entire project was predicated on spending more time together, the payoff was worth it. “Ultimately, our goal was to create a home that feels just as beautiful and comfortable for quiet everyday moments as it does when the entire family comes together,” says Leah.

FAST FACTSDesigner: River Brook Design & ConstructionLocation: Mountain Brook, AlabamaThe Space: A three-bedroom, two-and-a-half-bathroom home across 2,600 square feet.

“This is one of my favorite little rooms in the house,” says Leigh of the family room, which doubles as a play space. “We wanted to create a cozy, intimate retreat that felt full of personality, warmth, and charm. I love that we embraced the smaller scale of the space rather than trying to make it feel larger. The result is a room that feels incredibly inviting, layered, and collected.”

Vaulted ceilings and an exposed wood beam give this modestly sized living room an impressive sense of scale. Below, Leigh and Kevin balanced a tailored Coley Home sofa with dark upholstered seating, antique armchairs, and a Louis Philippe mirror.

“Ultimately, we wanted this room to embody the charm of the entire home—small in scale but never small in feeling,” says Leigh. “It’s a space that feels elegant enough for entertaining yet relaxed enough for grandchildren to curl up on the sofa. That balance of beauty, comfort, and livability is always at the heart of our designs.”

Proof that the smallest corners can have the biggest personalities, this charming game nook was designed with the grandchildren in mind. “I especially love the juxtaposition of the modern white pedestal table with the rich mustard velvet chairs,” adds Leigh. “The sculptural shapes and playful color bring a fresh energy to the space, while the gallery wall of collected artwork adds history, personality, and a sense of nostalgia.”

The sunroom acts a bit like a family scrapbook, compiling a slew of heirloom and inherited pieces into a comfortable place to unwind. “We wanted this space to feel as though it had been collected over generations rather than decorated all at once. It's comfortable, timeless, and a little whimsical—the perfect place for grandchildren to gather, family to visit, and everyday memories to be made.”

“I love how this dining room feels intimate, layered, and full of personality. We wanted to create a space that felt a little more formal and collected, while still being warm and inviting for everyday family gatherings,” says Leigh of the gathering space, which includes an antique sideboard that used to belong to her grandmother. “The mix of family heirlooms, traditional patterns, and unexpected pops of color gives the room a sense of history without feeling overly serious.”

A far cry from the home’s original 1960s kitchen, this reimagined space pairs vaulted ceilings with traditional, furniture-inspired details. A built-in china cabinet lends a sense of old-world formality, while marble countertops, warm wood floors, and oversized pendants keep the room feeling polished. Behind the scenes, a concealed coffee bar and appliance cabinet ensure everyday necessities don't interrupt the view.

Sleepovers get their own stylish headquarters in the grandchildren's bunk room, where the team channeled a playful and imaginative attitude. “One of my favorite details is the painted stripe treatment that wraps the walls and ceiling,” says Leigh. “Rather than using wallpaper, we chose to paint the stripes, creating a custom look that adds depth, dimension, and a wonderful sense of enclosure.”

The heart of the primary bedroom is an eye-catching antique four-poster bed, which has been pass down through several generations of Leigh’s family. “It’s such a special piece, and I love that it brings a genuine sense of history and heritage to the space,” she adds. “Rather than designing the room around something new, we embraced this treasured family heirloom and allowed it to become the focal point.”

Leigh and Kevin Misso are the husband-and-wife founders of River Brook, a Birmingham, Alabama–based design-build studio specializing in thoughtfully crafted homes that balance timeless architecture with modern livability. Together, the duo brings a refined yet approachable perspective to residential design, emphasizing traditional details, layered interiors, and spaces that feel collected rather than decorated.

#6963. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

Artificial intelligence is raising the stakes of cyber conflict as attackers use the technology to accelerate reconnaissance, uncover vulnerabilities, and launch attacks faster than many security teams can respond, according to a new report from agentic AI security company Kai.

Cybersecurity companies Rubrik and CrowdStrike have announced an expanded identity-security integration designed to move compromised identity environments from threat detection through clean recovery with less manual intervention.

Microsoft has shut down EvilTokens, a cybercrime service that used AI to help attackers break into e-mail accounts, study their victims, and build convincing financial scams.

Video game designers spend an enormous amount of time studying what keeps people engaged when something becomes difficult. They have become very good at helping people persist. Schools should pay attention.

#6961. The Unexpected Benefits of Actually Calling Someone

Thirty minutes on the old blower is better than therapy.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The options for communication are endless. Getting in touch with someone has never been easier, which also means we are subject to frequent digital conversations we’d rather not have. You can thank our overlords in Big Tech for that. Friends, family, enemies, and strangers alike can pepper open in-boxes with questions and concerns ranging from the unnecessary to full-scale attacks on your character, tastes, and loosely held opinions. Nearly 90 percent of Americans use text messaging or messaging apps daily, with 29 percent saying they’re texting more than they were one year ago, according to a 2026 YouGov study. Even with notifications turned off, they lurk in the background, and the social pressure to respond is hard for almost anyone to ignore.

But there is an anecdote to this scourge of modern life.

As an older millennial, I am part of the generation that remembers when the Internet changed our lives, but I also experienced defining moments using a simple telephone. Even in the infant stages of the Internet, the humble telephone, attached via cord that plugged into a wall, was the conduit for good and bad news; it was how we made plans and how relationships grew and solidified. I spent hours with the handset cradled between my teenage double chin and shoulder, plotting to see bands play, staying up too late talking to girls, and calling phone-sex hotlines to see if they were real. (They were.)

What younger generations don’t understand—to their detriment—is that the immediacy and intimacy of the phone call produce different results from a text or direct message. Imagine asking your crush to prom and getting three dots on the screen over and over, your stomach doing backflips as you wait for a yes or no. The phone call is the communication version of getting punched in the face. Your knee-jerk reactions are on display. The ear on the other end of the receiver can sense emotions: anger, reluctance, joy, excitement. You feel all of it innately without FaceTime video.

As adults, there are several instances—even today, especially today—for which a phone call is superior to a text message. One of the greatest is simply catching up with an old friend. Seriously, when’s the last time you cold-called a buddy? Thirty minutes on the blower with one of your besties can be exhilarating. You cover far more ground with less wrist pain, and you can do it while driving or sitting as a passenger princess, putting your wired earbuds to good use. Yes, the shock of seeing your name appear might cause a moment of panic in your friend—Who died? But they’ll get over it fast, and quickly you’ll fall into an easy conversation. Don’t overthink it; just call someone.

In business, the phone call is even more crucial. If you can get someone on the phone, they almost have to give you an answer. Even if it isn’t the one you want to hear, you can move on with your life and stop sending follow-up emails every 48 hours. Efficiency is king, and the phone can help. After all, there’s a reason that in nearly every photo of a captain of industry he or she is walking and talking on a phone. They didn’t get to where they are through text alone. Plus, if you’re under 30, the person on the other end might even be impressed that you made the effort to pick up the phone and call. They’ll certainly remember you!

Of course, nostalgia plays a part, but I’m not suggesting you buy an old-school phone and hook it up to the nearest wall. Let’s focus on the practice; your iPhone has a calling function. Use it more frequently. Sometimes the newest thing isn’t the best. We have yet to improve on the phone call. Don’t be scared to call someone or to answer when it rings. Pick up and be happy someone is on the other side. That’s a blessing in its own right.

#6960. The Biggest Home Trends Buyers Will Want in 2027, According to New Zillow Data

Buyers want homes that feel polished, personal, and efficient.

The new year is on the horizon, and if you’re hoping to have a home that’s on trend for 2027, Zillow’s annual home trends report is a must-read. In the newly released study, the real estate marketplace analyzed the language in millions of for-sale listings to highlight the features most likely to drive the market.

In the year ahead, all signs point to a demand for a more polished aesthetic as Americans look to bring bold color and less tech into their homes.

“Digital detox is the new luxury,” says Amanda Pendleton, Zillow’s home trends expert. “Homes are getting smarter, but buyers also want tech-free zones where nothing needs charging. Taken together, this data signals that buyers value homes that are functional and efficient, but still feel polished and personal.”

Read on to discover the home trends setting the scene for 2027.

The word “cozy” may be among the most common adjectives in listings, but in the new year, folks are after a more refined look. Mentions for “elevated” homes are up by 71 percent, but buyers aren’t necessarily seeking the classic midcentury modern aesthetic. The emerging design scene favors bold expressions of painted brick, board-and-batten siding, and color-drenched interiors, all refined features that make a personal statement.

As the culture becomes increasingly entrenched in all things AI, some homebuyers are going all-in with tech immersion while others are begging to unplug. Mentions of AI are up a staggering 119 percent in listings, but homes that boast “digital detox” spaces are 34 percent more common than last year.

The market shows a decrease in the demand for sustainability features and a greater focus on home efficiency as “green” terms fall 26 percent. While you won’t see these details in listing photos, buyers are enticed by mentions of whole-home batteries, which have risen by 43 percent, water filtration systems, up by 22 percent, and radon mitigation, up by 18 percent.

While homebuyers haven’t completely abandoned the open floor plan, data shows a greater interest in defined spaces within the home as the “sandwich generation” revives the normalcy of multigenerational living. Interestingly, kitchens are not as enticing to buyers as home offices (up by 42 percent), media rooms (up by 37 percent), and nurseries (up by 9 percent) begin to demand greater focus.

This year saw an increased interest in at-home wellness features, and in 2027, the demand is only growing. Mentions of home gyms are up 18 percent, spa-inspired bathrooms have risen to 60 percent, and golf simulators are up 29 percent. Jacuzzis are fading from the design scene, replaced by cold plunge pools, with mentions rising by 44 percent.

Dogs may be man’s best friend, but cats are emerging as America’s pet, as mentions of dog parks are flat and dog-grooming stations have fallen 16 percent. Meanwhile, catios, which are screened-in outdoor spaces for cats, are appearing 32 percent more in for-sale listings.

#6959. Why Is It So Hard to Call Out Your Buddy?

Your friend does something terrible. You know you should say something, but you don’t. There’s a reason for that.

I joined three men for drinks recently, two of whom I am close with, one a casual acquaintance. A couple hours into the evening, once our french fries were soggy and the Modelos had done their work, the acquaintance told us about an argument he’d had with his girlfriend a few months back. It got physical, he said, leaning back in his chair. The fight ended with her in the hospital. He told the story like a man pleading his case before a jury, his eyes full of defiance and indignation but also fear. He rejected responsibility for her injuries and was desperate to be told he had done nothing wrong.

“There is just a lot of passion in our relationship,” he said. “If it was a big deal, she would have left me then.”

I watched him across the dark wooden table, my face as blank as I could manage. He looked to the other men as he spoke. I do not remember all of his excuses, only that they tumbled from his lips like a flood. I do remember that my two friends said nothing.

I know these two as good men. They are courageous and righteous in their hearts. Both would come running to stop an injustice. Neither abides a bully or petty authority. They have rescued adults from floods and picked up teenage daughters too afraid to drive home. But that night, faced with a buddy looking for forgiveness for something unforgivable over a beer, they could not muster a weak rebuke.

Later, I asked them why. Neither had a clear answer, both seeming troubled by their silence.

“If he had said all that about his dog instead of his girlfriend,” I told them, “I know you would have spoken up.”

They would have, they agreed. Immediately. Forcefully. Without hesitation.

These men do not value women less than dogs (though, in my opinion, valuing dogs very highly is a sign of good character). Yet they do not know why they missed this opportunity for bravery.

That decision is more significant than it seems in the moment. It is the kind of behavior that, at its extreme, can lead to situations such as the sexual-assault scandal at Cornell University. What makes that case so chilling is not just what Jane Doe alleges seven men did to her. It is that the story came with a glimpse into the fraternity’s culture through its group chats. There, we witnessed these men offer up Jane Doe like a party favor. And no one—not a single one of these promising young men at this Ivy League university who pledged this fraternity—had either the inclination or the courage to speak up against it.

This kind of behavior is a symptom of what is termed “precarious manhood” by social psychologists and researchers like me. And it’s harmful not only to women but to men too.

Precarious manhood does not mean that men themselves are fragile. Rather that their social status as a “real man” is.

Manhood is precarious in a way womanhood is not. Womanhood is seen as a natural process; it is biological. A girl develops physically and is deemed “a woman.” She may behave in myriad ways that are not considered feminine, but rarely is she declared “not a real woman.” Masculinity, on the other hand, is hard won yet easily lost. It is constructed and conditional. Men have to prove it over and over through acts and attitudes—get strong, fight back, run fast, dominate—not through biology.

As President Barack Obama said in 2021 on the Renegades podcast, “So much of popular culture tells [young men] that the only clear defining thing about being a man, being masculine, is you excel in sports and sexual conquest and violence.”

As boys learn early, the smallest transgression—cross your legs the wrong way, walk with the wrong gait, or cry in public—can lead to an accusation that you are “not a real man” or “girly.” To be weak and harmless is bad; to be womanlike is even worse. Even the insults used to humiliate men—you know the ones—are often descriptions that liken them to women.

When men try to prove that they fit into this standard of masculinity, other men are expected to acknowledge it. When you object to that performance, it is a challenge to the other man's behavior and, importantly, to his masculinity. That makes the objection more aggressive. It’s no longer just peer pressure trying to get you to go along with the group. Refusing to join in is a masculine threat. It signals that you are not fully aligned on the manly ideal, which means your own manhood can be questioned.

To measure the prevalence of precarious manhood, researchers have asked tens of thousands of men around the world to take a survey that, on a seven-point scale, asks how much they agree with statements like the following:

“The title of ‘manhood’ needs to be reserved for those who deserve it.”

“A male’s status as a real man sometimes depends on how other people view him.”

Countries that exhibit higher levels of precarious manhood are less happy, with lower life expectancy, gross domestic product, institutional trust, and social support. Men in these countries engage in riskier behavior and have higher rates of risk-related health problems, like cirrhosis of the liver and early death. Women, of course, also suffer. Heightened prevalence of precarious manhood predicts higher rates of violence against women, including sexual assault and domestic violence, as well as fewer legal protections for them.

The link between violence and misogyny can be seen clearly among those insecure in their manhood. Take the incel community as an extreme example. This dark corner of the manosphere is occupied by men who have trouble with women and blame them for their failures. Since these men are not sexually successful, and they see that as a challenge to their manhood, they ramp up their misogyny and violence. Incels have committed dozens of violent crimes, including mass shootings, to express their rage.

In 2025, I wrote about one self-described incel, who spent hours filming himself sexually harassing women in public and posting the videos online. Eventually, when the women rejected him, he pepper-sprayed them. The videos were wildly popular on incel discussion boards, and the perpetrator was considered a hero among them. His misogyny and violence helped increase his social standing.

Research has revealed how distressingly deep misogyny is entrenched in these perceptions of masculinity. In one study, men were confronted about either racist or sexist remarks. When they were challenged about racism, they tended to be apologetic. When they were confronted about sexism, they were amused. This tells us that, on average, these men see misogyny as not worth correcting.

When women confronted men about sexist comments, the men tended to think the women were overreacting. When they were confronted by other men, however, they took the corrections more seriously. Because participants tended to be generally less concerned about sexist remarks, the researchers mused that men may be less likely to intervene, believing it wouldn’t make a difference. That, in turn, creates a culture of passive acceptance.

If men have the strength to stand up, though, research also shows that it makes a difference. When people are confronted about biased statements, they are more likely to change their behavior going forward. Further research has shown that group norms can influence and explain men’s misogynistic behavior. When men are with other men who are less tolerant of sexism, even if they have just met, the group is less likely to use sexist behavior as a way to prove their masculinity. But when men are in a group where sexist behavior is common, they are more likely to sexually objectify and disrespect women. They do this explicitly to gain social status—exactly as precarious manhood would suggest. These same men are also less likely to step in and help a woman who is in a sexually uncomfortable situation, like what we’ve seen with the Cornell case.

There is a better model for masculinity. “Expansive masculinity” describes a vision of manhood that values flexibility as a strength, leadership through connection and empathy, and the bravery in vulnerability. This model rejects that masculinity must be earned through dominance or proved constantly. It allows men to define themselves by their values and relationships rather than by their conformity to a narrow standard.

Such a model shifts masculinity from something that is externally validated to something embodied in personal integrity. It finds strength not in stoicism and domination but in the capacity for empathy, understanding, and connection with others. When other men behave badly, expansive masculinity calls for men to object, to stand up for their personal values, and to protect their communities.

My two friends have taken steps toward expansive masculinity. One of them was approached by a colleague who was accused of sexually harassing women at work. Like our acquaintance at the bar, that colleague was brimming with excuses and confrontation, looking for absolution. He wanted my friend to tell him it was okay or to tell him the women overreacted.

Instead, my friend told him, “You have made serious mistakes in the way you treat young women.” The colleague argued, but my friend held firm. “It’s more than one incident,” he said. “And just one is unacceptable.”

Meanwhile, my other friend came up with a simple practice: Whenever another man behaves out of line, make it muscle memory to say, “What the fuck?”

When bad behavior spreads in barrooms and chat rooms and locker rooms, it infects boys and young men. Promoting a healthier side of masculinity, one that sees care, protection, empathy, and vulnerability as strong character traits and misogyny as unacceptable, offers an alternative path. That leadership, strength of conviction, and commitment to others is ultimately what makes someone a good man.

#6958. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

District and state policies run the gamut from embracing to banning AI. This raises a key question: When does AI accelerate student learning and when does it interfere?

#6955. I Asked Designers for Their Secrets to an Expensive-Looking Guest Bedroom. They All Said the Same Thing

Skip the fussy pillow pile and focus on the details guests will actually notice.

Pictured above: a bold aubergine double guest bedroom designed by Nicole Fisher.

The holiday hosting season is upon us, and while you’re planning your tablescape decor and seasonal trimmings, you might want to take a hard look at your guest bedroom.

“Most guest rooms get treated like overflow storage, and guests feel that the second they walk in,” says Marie Cloud, principal designer at Indigo Pruitt.

Even if you host only occasionally, your guest room should give visitors a comfortable place to unwind. No need for a guest book or fuzzy slippers: Designers say these four thoughtful touches can make the room feel luxe without costing a fortune.

Regardless of the design aesthetic, the ambiance of a room always hinges on lighting. “I always pay attention to the lighting,” says Louis Lin, founder of his eponymous New York City–based interior design studio. “A beautiful bedside lamp with a warm, flattering glow can transform the atmosphere of a room. Layering different light sources, rather than relying on overhead lighting, can do that. It's a relatively simple gesture that makes a room feel much more intimate and refined.” This also gives guests the freedom to tailor the air of their space to their liking. It’s one of the few details they can truly control in their temporary lodging.

While a pile of pillows might look inviting, designer Marie Cloud says the bedtime legwork promptly cancels out the aesthetics.

“Skip the stack of eight decorative pillows your guest has to relocate before bed,” says Cloud. Her perfect formula for a top-notch guest setup? Two sleeping pillows, plus one lumbar pillow, all set atop a good set of sheets.

“Sheets with real hand-feel, like a crisp percale or washed linen,” Cloud suggests. “Finish with a throw folded at the foot in a color pulled from the room. It looks considered, and it’s actually comfortable at 2 a.m.”

Most guest rooms get the all-white wall treatment in the name of neutrality, but all it really says is that your space lacks intention. Just because this room is (mostly) reserved for guests doesn't mean it has to lack character. In fact, Cloud says you should not hold back.

“Drench the room,” says the designer. “Walls, trim, and ceiling in the same color, ideally something deep and warm. Continuous color reads custom, it makes a small room feel wrapped rather than cramped, and it tells your guest the space was designed for them. A guest room is also the lowest-risk room in the house to be bold, so take the swing.”

The best guest setups make visitors feel anticipated, even when they show up unexpectedly. To achieve the boutique-hotel feel, designers recommend investing in small luxuries that leave a big impact. A few items Cloud and Lin always recommend having on hand are a small luggage rack, real hangers, fresh towels, and a select set of toiletries reserved especially for guests.

“The real luxury is making guests feel that the room has been prepared just for them,” says Lin.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6954. Everyone Wants to Know if the MacBook Neo Will Go on Sale for Black Friday

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The cheapest MacBook might get even cheaper.

I am not the first person to point out that MacBooks are expensive. I will not be the last. If you monitor this stuff like we do, you’ll have seen the irony that Apple released the budget-conscious MacBook Neo earlier this year—only for the global computing shortage to almost immediately raise the price from $599 to $689.

But, the Neo is still cheap. You can buy it full-price and get your money’s worth. That said, we’d take the bet that it will go on sale. A handful of Prime Days have come and gone without any discounts to the newest MacBook, but with the new flagship released, we suspect that it Black Friday might give us a Christmas miracle.

Designed as Apple’s answer to the lower budget laptops running Windows, the Neo’s target audience is technically students—hence the good branding and cute colors. That being said, if you need a personal computer for watching Netflix and doing your taxes, but don’t want a full desktop, it’s a perfect choice.

The Neo runs on Apple’s A18 Pro chip—the same one that powers the iPhone 16 Pro and Pro Max. If the idea of buying a laptop that runs on a phone chip gives you pause, your stress is better channeled elsewhere. While this isn’t the right device for someone who is planning to run heavy-duty software, it can handle standard internet browsing, video calls, and the like with ease. Just keep in mind that it only has 8GB of RAM, so it’s best-suited to basic workflows.

Of course, this computer has the added benefit of four jazzy color options: blush and citrus for the bold, indigo for those more subdued, and silver for that classic MacBook aesthetic. Wrapped in an aluminum body with a 13-inch Liquid Retina display, the Neo weighs in at just 2.7 pounds (same as the MacBook Air) and is 0.5 inches thick. (For reference, the iPhone 17 Pro Max is 0.35 inches thick.)

Despite our best efforts, we don’t quite know how Apple determines its deals for big sale events—which means if this laptop does indeed get discounted, there’s no telling exactly how deep the price cut might be or when it’ll hit. Maybe it’ll shave $100 off to get back to its pre-increase price, or maybe we could be looking at a sub-$500 MacBook for the first time ever.

Whatever the discount may be, the Neo is undoubtedly a smart buy for anyone who wants a laptop that can handle all of the basics. Even at full price, it’s a fraction of the cost of a discounted MacBook Pro or Air, which reliably go on sale for Black Friday.

If you’re not willing to wait for a potential Neo deal, a handful of other MacBooks are already on sale.

#6953. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

Cybersecurity companies Rubrik and CrowdStrike have announced an expanded identity-security integration designed to move compromised identity environments from threat detection through clean recovery with less manual intervention.

#6945. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

District and state policies run the gamut from embracing to banning AI. This raises a key question: When does AI accelerate student learning and when does it interfere?
