#6907. Stop Hunting for Pricey Antiques—Designers Say These 9 Humble Finds Give a Home So Much Character

They’re affordable, widely available, and stylish.

It’s any avid thrifter’s dream to stumble upon an extremely valuable piece of decor while browsing the shelves of their favorite antique store. But while you’re hunting for rare treasures, don’t discount the less-expensive finds—they can be the secret to a home that looks instantly filled with character.

“To me, a collected home is much more interesting when everything isn’t perfect or chosen for its monetary value,” designer Lauren Sullivan of Well by Design tells House Beautiful. “Often, it’s the less precious pieces that give a room its personality.”

Even as older generations are continuing to downsize and offload unwanted things to local thrift stores, it still might be hard to find the diamonds in the rough. Keep reading to find out what Sullivan, and a handful of other designers, say you should be on the lookout for on your next thrifting visit.

The most valuable antiques are the ones that have been passed down through generations. In Sullivan’s own bathroom, the designer keeps her great-grandmother’s vanity set on the countertop, its aged finish providing beautiful contrast with the more contemporary marble and finishes. In her bedroom, the same great-grandmother’s small bedside clock found a place on Sullivan’s nightstand.

“Neither is particularly valuable monetarily, but I love incorporating pieces that have been passed down through generations into a home filled with both old and new,” Sullivan says.

Designers love incorporating artwork into homes to add personality and dimension, but Sullivan, as well as designer Becca Meyer of BB Meyer Design, believe that antique shops are where you can really find some hidden gems.

“Some of my favorite pieces of art have come from flea markets or antique shops for very little money,” Meyer says. “I much prefer these to mass produced prints.” You’re more likely to find pieces with aged textures at these types of shops, and that patinaed quality creates an entirely unique look.

Sullivan explains that she’s actually particularly drawn to prints, as well as smaller paintings and silhouettes. She even has an inexpensive print of The Skating Minister in a vintage frame in one of her guest rooms, pictured above. “These smaller, or sometimes quirky, pieces can bring so much personality to a space without requiring a significant investment,” she adds. They may not be originals, but they’re definitely unique.

Old pottery and ceramics are widely available, and they are usually affordable as well. Designer Brooke Snyder of Design Hutch loves how they make any place feel more curated.

The imperfections these handmade pieces carry, from uneven shapes, variations in glaze, or evidence of years of use, are what help these items bring a warmth to a room, particularly when placed near more refined materials, according to Sullivan. They feel humble, maybe even utilitarian, especially when displayed in a kitchen. Or, better yet, used in a kitchen (as long as they’re safe).

“They're beautiful on their own but also so functional for holding utensils or branches or flowers,” Meyer adds. “They make any space feel a little more lived-in and loved and help tell a story—and they don't break the bank!”

“I love incorporating vintage plates, old copper pots, and woven baskets into a home,” explains designer Joe Waroquier. Though these are run-of-the-mill, everyday objects, they can take on and provide an entirely different life when displayed in the home as decor. Mismatched plates on a wall, a collection of copper pots hanging on a rod or resting on shelves in the kitchen, an old crock filled with branches—these items invite in warmth, texture, and more personality.

“I’m particularly drawn to pieces that show their age, whether it’s a little patina, a worn finish, or an imperfect glaze,” he adds. “I love mixing these finds with more contemporary furnishings because that contrast makes a room feel collected and personal rather than overly designed.”

Mirrors can be intriguing focal points of a room, or they can add dimension to a gallery wall. However you want to style them, Snyder loves finding these reflective pieces at antique malls and vintage shops.

“Purchasing something that you don’t see every day from a retailer allows this unique piece to stand out on its own, without feeling like something you see everywhere or feels too simple,” she explains.

As Snyder puts it, antique table lamps provide high visual impact with low investment. Adding one to your accent table or on a bookshelf is an easy way to make your space feel more curated while inviting in another level of ambience with the lighting.

These vintage lamps in the above space, for instance, were bought from a collector and make the primary bedroom feel much more charming and curated. And unlike antique sconces, there’s no need to hardwire these into the wall.

Besides pottery and lamps, designer Ryann Swan Hackett of Ryann Swan Design loves to find and collect other small tabletop decor for her projects. Think candleholders, vintage coffee table books, and trays—things that can layer a little history and individuality into a room or simple vignette.

“If you’re lucky,” Swan Hackett says of the vintage coffee table books, “you’ll find an old inscription inside, which gives the book a story of its own.”

In terms of midsize accent pieces, Meyer says that she has a soft spot for finding antique stools “because you can pop them into any space to add depth and dimension.” Tuck them beside an armchair in the living room, add them to an empty kitchen corner and place a plant on top, or stick one in a bathroom by the tub for somewhere to keep a drink while relaxing. And if you find an upholstered one that could use new fabric, switching it out for a pattern you love is probably worth it.

“These bring a natural warmth that can be difficult to replicate with something new, and their small size makes them fairly reasonable in price for a vintage furnishing piece,” Meyer says.

A larger piece of furniture is likely always going to be more expensive than a smaller one, but designer Yena Jung of By Yena Designs thinks vintage hutches are the versatile antiques people should pay more attention to. They come in many different shapes, styles, and sizes, and can easily make a home in any room of the house.

“A beautiful vintage hutch can add warmth, charm, and a sense of history to a space,” Jung says. “I especially love how their imperfections and natural patina make them feel collected over time.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6902. Absolutely Everything You Need to Know About the Citizen Watch Catalog

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The Japanese brand offers endless variety, appealing affordability, and incredible technological innovation.

Whether you encounter them in multi-brand boutiques and stand-alone stores, in duty-free shops while on vacation, on the Internet, or at auction, Citizen’s watches are everywhere.

Founded in 1918, the brand that began with the simple objective of producing a Japanese-made watch has evolved into a truly global juggernaut, with annual turnover of approximately $2.1 billion for the fiscal year that ended in March 2026. Vertically integrated, it produces everything from mechanical divers of the traditional ilk to tech-forward, satellite-guided modern tool watches that update time zones instantly as one zigzags across the world. Its light-powered Eco-Drive technology essentially constitutes a movement category in its own right, while its wafer-thin Eco-Drive One models—some of the brand’s highest-end wares—command luxury prices.

Like Seiko—another homegrown Japanese brand—Citizen’s considerable scale and power has compounded over more than a century. And also like Seiko, it has grown its manufacturing capabilities to such an extent that it has successfully expanded into industries other than watchmaking. But where Citizen truly shines is in offering a remarkably capable and robust product at a price that elicits few complaints from the watch-buying public. Available in points of sale in roughly 130 countries—500 alone in the U.S.—Citizen’s timepieces are not only affordable but also truly ubiquitous.

For someone in search of a relatively inexpensive watch—any type of watch, really, save perhaps a high complication in precious metal with a dial made by a lone monk living in the Swiss Jura—that will hold up under adverse conditions, Citizen is a natural answer. Whether you’re looking for a feature-laden dive watch (Promaster Aqualand), an elegant, ultrathin dress watch (Eco-Drive One), a luxurious, design-forward everyday watch (Series 8), or a vintage-inspired take on a Genta-esque design (Zenshin), there’s a good chance that there’s a Citizen timepiece that will satisfy your desires.

The only issue is parsing the firm’s gigantic catalog in search of an ideal choice. To help you in this regard, we produced this handy guide.

While not as old as Seiko, Citizen has a long and storied watchmaking history filled with a staggering amount of technological innovation. In fact, it wouldn’t be too bold to claim that in a world based primarily on centuries-old technology, Citizen is one of the most forward-thinking marques.

The brand’s story begins in 1918, when entrepreneur Kamekichi Yamazaki established the Shokosha Watch Research Institute in Tokyo with the goal of providing a domestically produced alternative to imported Swiss and American watches. By 1924, it had produced its first pocket watch, which it named “Citizen” at the behest of Tokyo mayor Count Shinpei Goto, who imagined a watch available to ordinary members of society. Six years later, the Shokosha Watch Research Institute was officially incorporated as the Citizen Watch Company and began producing wristwatches in 1931.

In 1936, the company established its Tanashi factory in Tokyo, allowing it to significantly expand its industrial capacity and begin exporting watches to Southeast Asia, China, and elsewhere. By 1941, it established a machine-tool business that allowed it to move beyond watchmaking and that still exists to this day. Postwar expansion continued at a dizzying rate, with Citizen developing shock-resistant Parashock technology in 1956 (which it demonstrated by dropping watches out of a helicopter). In 1959, it debuted the Parawater, Japan’s first fully waterproof watch, and in 1962 came the Diamond Flake, an ultrathin model equipped with a hand-wound movement.

By the 1960s, Citizen began experimenting with technologies that would come to define its place in the contemporary watch market: First, in 1966, came the X-8, the country’s first electronic wristwatch, followed in 1970 by the X-8 Chronometer, which holds the distinction of being the world’s very first titanium wristwatch. And while the brand wasn’t the first to embrace quartz technology, it did so with vigor by 1973, introducing the Crystron Mega in 1975, which was accurate to an astonishing ±3 seconds per year.

The technology that would eventually evolve into Eco-Drive—the brand’s signature system that converts ambient light into electrical energy, thus reducing the frequency of battery replacements—debuted in 1976 with the advent of its Crystron Solar Cell, the world’s first light-powered, analog-dial quartz watch. Then, in 1982, Citizen released the Professional Diver, which boasted an incredible-for-the-era 1,300 meters of water resistance; it followed up three years later with the Aqualand, the first dive watch with an onboard electronic depth gauge. In 1989, it gathered its diverse sports watch offerings together within the Promaster line.

Innovation continued throughout the 1990s, with Citizen introducing the world’s first multi-zone radio-controlled wristwatch in 1993. In 2011, it debuted the Eco-Drive Satellite Wave, a watch that synchronized with satellites to provide hyper-accurate timekeeping across different time zones. Five years later, in 2016, the Eco-Drive One hit the market with a light-powered movement just 1mm thick, building upon ultrathin offerings from the brand’s back catalog. Then, in 2019, it brought forth the Eco-Drive Caliber 0100, a movement with stunning accuracy of ±1 second per year.

Today, Citizen Group is more than just Citizen, comprising movement maker Miyota, the aforementioned precision machinery business, watch brands Bulova and Frédérique Constant, and much more. It’s a vertically integrated behemoth with more than 12,000 employees worldwide. Founded over a century ago, it’s grown into one of the most important brands within the watchmaking sphere—and beyond.

Like fellow Japanese watchmaker Seiko, Citizen produces such a dizzying array of timepieces that it’s nearly impossible to categorize them in any definitive manner. Many modern collections overlap in remit with older historical collections that are still kicking around, while certain collections are available only in certain markets. (Some frankly defy categorization entirely, and others should probably be cut from the brand’s catalog.)

What follows, therefore, is a helpful (albeit admittedly non-exhaustive) list of Citizen’s most important and recognizable product families, delineated with the understanding that certain watches—including several dedicated ladies collections from the Japanese domestic market—will invariably be left out. Still, the following rough descriptions should help orient the new (or seasoned!) collector.

The Rolan Collection is an excellent place to search for a highly affordable, versatile everyday watch. Using Citizen’s signature Eco-Drive technology, each model is easily powered by ambient light and virtually never requires a battery change. While certain references are paired to matching metal bracelets, others come on stylish straps, and all take their aesthetic inspiration from classic mid-century horological tropes. Simple and handsome, the Rolan Collection watches are perfect for someone in search of their first “nice” watch.

The ref. AW2110-06E is everything you need in a dress/everyday watch and nothing you don’t. Measuring 41mm in brushed stainless steel, it features a radially brushed black sector dial with applied indices and a luminous dauphine handset. Paired to a black leather strap with stainless-steel hardware and powered by Eco-Drive technology, it’s a timepiece that could conceivably be paired with just about anything. Moreover, at just a hair over $300, it makes a highly affordable gift for a recent graduate who needs a solid timepiece to wear on an interview or to a first job.

Notable Reference: AW2110-06EType: DressDiameter: 41mmMovement: Citizen cal. J830 Eco-DrivePrice: $296.25MORE INFO HERE

The Garrison Collection contains military-inspired field watches and chronographs in a variety of styles, most of which are widely available in the United States both online and at authorized dealers. Powered by Eco-Drive technology, they’re reliable, good-looking pieces on bracelets or straps that all retail for less than $500, making them—like their counterparts in the dressier Rolan collection—excellent gifting options. For someone who’s after mil-spec looks but who doesn’t want to shell out for a vintage watch, the Garrison is a perfect choice.

The ref. BM8180-03E is ubiquitous at Citizen points of sale and neatly encapsulates the brand’s ethos. Measuring a comfortable 37mm in brushed stainless steel, it features a black dial with luminous Arabic indices, an outer five-minute track, and a day-date display at three o’clock. Powered by Eco-Drive, highly legible, and wildly affordable, it still boasts 100 meters of water resistance and a two-piece green fabric strap for less than $300. Looking like an issued military watch from the 1960s, it’s ideal for outdoor use while hiking or on vacation

Notable Reference: BM8180-03EType: FieldDiameter: 37mmMovement: Citizen cal. E100 Eco-DrivePrice: $221.25MORE INFO HERE

Established in 1989, the Promaster Collection is where Citizen parks its most formidable tool watches. Organized into Marine, Sky, and Land subcategories, they localize many of the notable innovations that the brand developed over the past 60-plus years, from the early Parawater’s water resistance to the Aqualand’s electronic depth gauge to the Navihawk’s multifunction timekeeping and more. Today the collection encompasses everything from mechanical divers to radio-controlled pilot’s watches to exploration-oriented field watches.

Though Citizen is famous for its Eco-Drive technology, it’s worth noting that the brand has a long history of crafting hardcore mechanical tool watches. The modern Promaster Fujitsubo ref. NB6026-56L—a limited edition based upon a vintage 1977 diver found still ticking on an Australian beach in 1983—is one such timepiece. Crafted from Super Titanium with a platinum DLC coating, it features a marine-blue bezel and dial, a matching triple-link bracelet, 200 meters of water resistance, and an automatic movement with a power reserve of 42 hours.

Notable Reference: NB6026-56LType: DiverDiameter: 41mmMovement: Citizen cal. 9051 automaticPrice: $1,195MORE INFO HERE

Launched in 2008, discontinued in 2014, and relaunched in 2021, the Series 8 Collection is based upon the idea of an angular, eight-part case design containing a mechanical movement. Though the collection has expanded somewhat beyond its original aesthetic remit, it still emphasizes the idea of a contemporary mechanical offering with clean lines and bold geometric forms. Comprising several model families, the Series 8 features integrated-style bracelets, textured dials, and enhanced resistance to magnetic fields of up to 16,000 A/m.

Executed in a tasteful two-tone colorway, the Series 8 831 ref. NB6059-57P offers outsize value from a variety of angles. There’s its 40mm stainless-steel case with surprisingly sophisticated geometry. But there’s also its handsome Champagne dial with rose-gold-tone furniture, its caliber 9051 automatic movement, its tapered H-link bracelet with brushed and finished surfaces, and its 100 meters of water resistance. For someone looking for a handsome and versatile everyday watch that can be worn to the office or to the beach, it’s an excellent, relatively affordable option.

Notable Reference: NB6059-57PType: Everyday Diameter: 40mmMovement: Citizen cal. 9051 automaticPrice: $1,125MORE INFO HERE

Dating to 1987, the Attessa Collection focuses on the marriage of advanced materials with technologies such as Eco-Drive, radio control, and GPS timekeeping. Building upon Citizen’s innovations with titanium from 1970’s X-8 Chronometer, it offers lightweight, function-forward watches in a variety of categories. Sporty, practical, and good-looking, the collection’s tool watches ditch classically inspired looks in favor of aesthetics that balance practical functionality with sleek lines, subdued materials, and elaborate case finishing.

The Attesa F950 ref. CC4107-80H encapsulates many of the technologies that make Citizen’s wares stand out in an extremely crowded marketplace. There’s the 43.2mm Super Titanium case in dark gray DLC with recrystallized titanium accents; the Eco-Drive Satellite Wave GPS movement with time-only signal reception in under three seconds; and the multifunction display with world time, chronograph, perpetual calendar, power reserve, and numerous other features. Fueled by exposure to ambient light, it’s a futuristic tool with streaks of ’70s inspiration.

Notable Reference: CC4107-80HType: Everyday Diameter: 43.2mmMovement: Citizen cal. F950 Eco-DrivePrice: $2,195MORE INFO HERE

While Citizen has several lines that focus on mechanical watchmaking, Tsuyosa is particularly notable for combining this with stainless-steel construction, integrated-bracelet styling, colorful dials, and accessible pricing. Rather than emphasizing advanced materials or modern timekeeping tech, it uses classic 20th-century watchmaking as an inspiration, seemingly adopting the idea that simpler is better. From pastel-hued 37mm models to small-seconds designs with undulating dial patterns, it’s one of the brand’s most compelling offerings.

The Tsuyosa 37mm is truly “everything you need and nothing you don’t” in wristwatch form. Constructed from stainless steel, it features alternating brushed and polished surfaces throughout its multifaceted case and integrated multi-link bracelet, lending it a sophistication that belies its sub-$1,000 pricing. Available in multiple colors, its sunray dial with luminous indices and magnified date window is legible and direct, while its automatic caliber 8210 movement and 50 meters of water resistance offer more than enough utility for daily wear. A set-and-forget watch.

Notable Reference: NJ0200-50MType: Everyday Diameter: 37mmMovement: Citizen cal. 8210 automaticPrice: $371.25MORE INFO HERE

The Zenshin Collection brings together several compelling features in a single product family: Super Titanium (titanium construction with Duratect surface-hardening treatments), automatic or Eco-Drive movements, angular cases with integrated bracelets inspired by Citizen’s 1980s output, textured dials, sapphire crystals, 100 meters of water resistance, and relatively accessible pricing. Available in three-hand and chronograph configurations, Zenshin brings a bit more materials science than Tsuyosa but costs much less than comparable Attesa fare.

Though a 42.4mm chronograph might smack of early-2000s excess, Super Titanium construction and a comfortable integrated bracelet help keep the Zenshin Chrono under control on the wrist. Available in several colorways, the “panda” colorway ref. CA4610-85A with a textured white-silver dial and contrasting black outer tachymeter scale and chronograph registers is particularly attractive and versatile. Powered by Eco-Drive, it features a 24-hour display as well as a date window—and its sub-$600 price is tough to argue with.

Notable Reference: CA4610-85AType: Everyday Diameter: 42.4mmMovement: Citizen cal. B620 Eco-DrivePrice: $506.25MORE INFO HERE

The Citizen is Citizen’s purest expression of horological excellence. Building upon the firm’s legacy of highly accurate mechanical timekeeping in the mid-century Chronomaster Collection, it launched the collection in 1995 to emphasize its commitment to precision, simplicity, and longevity. Today it comprises both mechanical and Eco-Drive movements, marrying them with traditional Japanese craftsmanship in the form of washi paper dials, hand-dyed indigo treatments, and other decorative techniques. Wildly accurate, they’re among Citizen’s most expensive offerings.

If you’re the type of person who values accuracy above all else in a wristwatch—but wouldn’t mind great looks thrown in for good measure—then chances are the Citizen ref. AQ4100-65X might be the timepiece for you. Measuring 38.3mm in the brand’s Super Titanium, it’s powered by the Eco-Drive caliber A060, which is accurate to ±5 seconds per year. (And that innocuous-seeming date window? It’s actually a perpetual date window that doesn’t need manual updating.) Oh, and the red dial? Ultra-thin Tosa Tengujoshi paper. (Hence the price.)

Notable Reference: AQ4100-65XType: Dress/everyday Diameter: 38.3mmMovement: Citizen cal. A060 Eco-DrivePrice: $2,995MORE INFO HERE

If the Citizen Collection is all about accuracy, then the Eco-Drive One Collection is about combining that accuracy—achieved via the brand’s signature Eco-Drive technology—with mind-bogglingly thin case architecture and precision manufacturing. Launched in 2016 to coincide with the 40th anniversary of Eco-Drive, the lineup features several watches powered by the caliber 8826, which was introduced as the world’s thinnest light-powered movement at just 1mm tall. When it’s combined with Super Titanium and other advanced tech, these pieces are truly remarkable.

Not only is the Eco-Drive One a spectacularly thin timepiece at just 4.5mm tall, but it’s also a remarkably handsome dress watch. Measuring 36.6mm wide in stainless steel, it features a flat gray dial with a contrasting dark-gray minute track, applied gold-tone indices with a matching dauphine handset, and a dark-gray sub-seconds display above six o’clock. That its crown is tiny shouldn’t be much of a concern—the caliber 8845 Eco-Drive is so accurate that you’ll hardly ever need to adjust it. (Unless you change time zones, that is.) Truly a one-and-done dress watch.

Notable Reference: AQ5010-01AType: DressDiameter: 36.6mmMovement: Citizen cal. 8845 Eco-DrivePrice: $2,395MORE INFO HERE

A limited-edition offering celebrating 50 years of Eco-Drive technology, the Eco-Drive Photon Collection combines highly geometric case shapes with dials inspired by the physics of light transmission. Currently available in two references, each features a slit dial that dynamically shifts in color as light interacts with its surface. Paired with integrated bracelets and measuring 39.6mm in Super Titanium, they’re more premium offerings than many Citizen core-catalog models but significantly less expensive than Eco-Drive One or Citizen watches.

A distinctive, contemporary take on the luxury sports watch, the Eco-Drive Photon ref. BJ6569-59X combines dark-gray and amber-yellow Super Titanium case construction to eye-catching effect. The three-dimensional dial with overlapping slits creates even more visual interest, while the caliber E036 Eco-Drive movement turns any light source into a consistent source of accurate timekeeping. Water resistant to 50 meters, the Photon is an excellent everyday option for someone who appreciates a bit of Genta-inflected styling in their wardrobe.

Notable Reference: BJ6569-59XType: EverydayDiameter: 39.6mmMovement: Citizen cal. E036 Eco-DrivePrice: $1,195MORE INFO HERE

#6898. So Is Lanterns Canceled Or What?

It was one of the best new HBO shows in recent memory, but it seems like that won’t matter at all.

You know what happens when you blow up a universe? You’re left sifting through the rubble. Since the news that James Gunn and his producing partner Peter Safran have been ousted as co-CEOs and cochairmen of DC Studios, there are many questions about the future of their DC Universe. Chief among them is Lanterns. So is Lanterns canceled? That’s what everyone wants to know.

The confusion started when Variety published its report about the exit of Gunn and Safran on November 10. The brunt of the story details how Gunn and Safran’s leadership of the studio was a modest, but not big enough, success for Warner Bros. (and now Skydance) brass. “Efforts to right the ship were mixed at best—with tentpoles and television shows that didn’t always generate enough excitement to justify their price tags,” wrote Variety. “Sources suggest the thinking was they spent big—and lost too much—on projects that just weren’t resonating with audiences.”

In the story, Variety explored the studio’s TV efforts, which we should note have plenty of asterisks. Under Gunn and Safran’s leadership, DC Studios released The Penguin, a spin-off of The Batman that won awards; the caveat is that it was already in development before Gunn and Safran took over. There was also the second season of Peacemaker, a series created by Gunn (as a spin-off of his 2021 movie The Suicide Squad) but whose creation also, weirdly, predates Gunn’s reign over DC. It was also the second season of a television show that premiered three years earlier.

This leads to Lanterns, the first DC show to grow from conception to air under Gunn and Safran. In Variety’s original version of its story, the outlet’s phrasing about the fate of Lanterns made the Internet jump, as it suggested the show, which cultivated a dedicated audience late in the summer, was canceled. “An insider at the company suggests the crime drama isn’t quite capturing the zeitgeist, pointing out the show didn’t get renewed for a second season,” Variety wrote.

Hours later, Variety changed its story to its current phrasing (the new words in bold): “Yet an insider at the company suggests the crime drama isn’t quite capturing the zeitgeist, pointing out the show has yet to be renewed for a second season.”

Variety also added the same source, saying, “People cannot wait to make another season of a hit show.”

You can see why there’s confusion. Gunn and Safran’s dismissal from DC was sudden, blindsiding even the men themselves (according to trades like The Hollywood Reporter) at a time when DC Studios seemed to be in a steady rhythm. Sure, this year’s Supergirl was a fumble, but last year’s Superman had gotten the refreshed DCU off on the right foot, and the recent mid-budget horror thriller Clayface grossed a clean $100 million. Though none of the Gunn/Safran–era movies were eye-watering successes, you also can’t measure long-term impact and sustained popularity of a franchise in box-office milestones alone. That’s not how art works.

It’s especially confusing with Lanterns. Back in October, outlets like Variety reported strong viewership figures for the series on HBO and HBO Max. In Skydance’s own third-quarter earnings call, the studio touted Lanterns as a rousing success, with an average of 22 million global viewers, “among HBO Max’s strongest launches” ever for the platform. To now read from insider sources claiming Lanterns wasn’t “quite capturing the zeitgeist” is downright absurd and frankly an insult to anyone’s intelligence.

Is Lanterns canceled? The good news is it isn’t. The bad news is that it isn’t canceled yet. Lanterns was a key part of Gunn’s grand plans for the DCU. Now that he’s been removed—and his future at the company unclear, despite more projects in development under his guidance—the fate of Lanterns is uncertain, as is the whole DCU.

A likely scenario is that Skydance will burn through all the remaining DC projects and dump them on HBO Max—stuff like Gunn’s Superman sequel Man of Tomorrow, the mockumentary comedy The People vs. Gorilla Grodd, and the animated film Dynamic Duo, which is said to be a highly experimental project using puppetry and animation. Lanterns season 2 could be among them, perhaps fast-tracked to HBO Max, where it would get a swift cancellation regardless of its performance (especially now that audiences will know not to get invested), or it simply doesn’t get made at all. But it’s anyone’s guess what will actually happen.

Whether audiences will get to see John Stewart be the Green Lantern is now in the hands of new studio leaders like Jeremy Latcham, who will replace Gunn and Safran starting December 1. We’ll see if Latcham leads with willpower or through fear.

#6895. This Colorful South Carolina Home Marries Mediterranean Elegance With Lowcountry Charm

Designer Megan Molten worked handmade details and custom elements into every room.

How do you blend the magical ease of Mediterranean life with the coastal charm of South Carolina’s lowcountry? After an enchanting vacation in Greece with their three daughters, Megan Molten’s clients wanted their new build on Daniel Island to capture that unique, transportive feeling they fell in love with—and they asked the Charleston-based designer to help them achieve it.

Alongside builder Wes Whittle of EasTech Property Development, Molten was involved from the very beginning, crafting the blank canvas into a residence that balanced the sun-drenched elegance of the Greek islands with traditional Lowcountry living. The designer went all in on every detail, relying on high-quality craftsmanship, custom pieces, and handmade elements to bring life to every room.

Upon entry, guests are greeted by a hand-painted, botanical-themed mural by local artist Jenn Griffith. “The mural’s objective was very specific,” says Molten. “The homeowners wanted a design that captured elements of both the Mediterranean and the Lowcountry.” Turn to the other side and find a plaster-covered stairwell by artist Emily Pope Harris, an addition that sets the tone for the whole home. Move further into the home and find a hand-painted floor that shines in the craft room, created using a stencil from Etsy and a few Sherwin-Williams paint colors—it’s playfully fabulous and still somehow timeless. “Clients do not typically splurge in rooms that are not front and center, so this was a real treat to bring to life, especially knowing how much their daughters love to craft,” the designer adds.

Elsewhere, a larger portion of the budget was split between the lighting and the kitchen. “Statement lighting in the house was always prioritized,” Molten says. “The homeowners also agreed that the lighting was important and needed to be special. I was finally able to use some light fixtures that I have been eyeing for years!”

As for the kitchen, it was more difficult to know where to splurge and where to save, a predicament the designer finds herself with on most projects. However, looking now at the honed limestone backsplash, green quartzite countertops with an integrated sink, and a plaster hood by Pope Harris, it’s clear that she made the right choices.

Even with all the home’s beautiful elements, the fan-favorite among Molten’s team ended up being the pool just outside the kitchen. It’s lower than most Molten has worked on in Charleston, making it one of the most unique she’s seen. Flanked by archways and paired with two spas and a raised hot tub, it fully captures a Grecian grotto feel.

Even with all the one-of-a-kind features and elevated furnishings, the home is a place where life is meant to be experienced to the fullest. “The spaces are to be enjoyed without hesitation,” Molten explains, “creating a beautiful, practical environment for a life well lived.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Megan MoltenArchitect: DLB Custom Home DesignBuilder: EasTech Property DevelopmentLocation: Daniel Island, South CarolinaThe Space: A six-bedroom, seven-bathroom new build spanning 6,000 square feet.

The beauty of this entryway starts outside with the front door. Made locally by Maebeck Doors, it’s an arched shape with a grid pattern that serves as a charming “wow” moment upon entering the home.

Once inside the house, the awe continues, from the curved plaster stairwell to the custom botanical mural by artist Jenn Griffith. “The homeowners wanted a design that captured elements of both the Mediterranean and the Lowcountry,” Molten adds. The final touch? A stone table in the notch at the base of the stairs, perfect for placing keys or bags at the end of the day.

Molten’s client wanted a large, comfortable sectional that could easily seat her family and any guests, so when the team saw these two at High Point Market, they knew they were perfect for the space. A travertine table, nestled between the two sectionals, made the pieces feel like one long, continuous design, and both were upholstered in Crypton performance fabric for added durability.

To complement the space, Molten added a plush armchair and topped the room off with a modern light fixture that doesn’t add much noise. “The artwork above the sectional is from the homeowner’s own collection,” Molten says. “It served as the catalyst for the colorways used throughout the home.”

A calming, functional space, the brightness of the kitchen is achieved not through a classic all-white kitchen, but through shades of light green, from the quartzite countertops to the greenish-gray cabinets. Rattan pendant lights bring in that casual Mediterranean feel, especially since the floor-to-ceiling windows on the left overlook the pool below.

To keep it family-friendly, Molten applied one of her favorite tricks: leather on the counter stool seats and fabric on the back. “Wiping off messes and letting the leather patina is both practical and pretty,” she adds.

This dining nook really needed to be functional, since it’s where Molten’s clients and their three children sit for breakfast every morning and dinner every night. Durability for daily use played a huge part in the furniture choices; they sourced a long wooden table and used the same seating upholstery trick here as they did with the counter stools in the kitchen. For a touch of whimsy, Molten crowned the nook with a coco bead chandelier.

Instead of plaster on the double-sided fireplace as she had initially intended, Molten went with a zellige tile façade that produces a lovely shine.

Easily accessible from the living room, kitchen, and screened-in porch, this breakfast area—complete with a full bar—is perfect for hosting. Anyone can come in and make themselves a drink whenever they want.

Arched upper cabinets with lattice details create more movement in this space, as well as add in charm, and the chandelier has been on the designer’s list of favorites for a while. “I particularly love the contrast of the black countertop with the coral cabinets,” Molten says.

The first thing Molten selected for the scullery was the charming lattice window over the sink looking out onto the yard. Then came the bold wallpaper, which inspired the vibrant cabinet paint color, balanced out with a light Calacatta marble countertop with hints of yellow and gold throughout.

“This scullery is a very functional space that houses their microwave, coffee maker, refrigerator, and drawers with snacks, all built-in and panel-ready,” Molten says.

When her clients told her they wanted a dedicated craft room for their girls, Molten was just as excited at the prospect as their daughters. “A dedicated specialty room like this typically provides a great opportunity for lots of creativity and color,” she explains.

They started with the colorful striped wallpaper on the ceiling, pulling colors from it for the built-in cubby shelf and the painted floors. A neutral light fixture keeps the room from feeling too busy, giving it a more timeless feel. “The floor design is so special, and a bit of a splurge,” Molten adds. “I was so pleased the client embraced this idea!”

The unique wallpaper served as the starting point for this powder bath, as Molten designed everything else around it. “The Jamie Young mirror looks like an oyster shell, and the floating stone was a nice feature,” she says. “Painting the trim and ceiling the pink shell color made the wallpaper, tile, and stone come together.”

Molten had never done a wallpaper border before, but she adores how it turned out in one of the daughter’s bedrooms. “It’s a great way to add thoughtful design details to a room and is cost-effective too, as opposed to covering the full room in wallpaper,” she says. The trim color was pulled from this border, which she paired with a signature shade of pink from her own collection with Sherwin-Williams.

“For the daughter’s bathroom, we opted for a tub/shower combo to accommodate young children,” Molten explains. These children-friendly details are found elsewhere in the room too, like a pull-out step stool under the sink cabinets.

The pink-and-green palette from the daughter’s bedroom is continued here, though in slightly more saturated shades. A local Charleston brand, Mirth Studio, created the painted wood valance to pair with the Pepper Home shower curtain, and Pope Harris continued her plaster work here, covering a Visual Comfort & Co. sconce over the mirror in a custom pink hue.

Located on the small third floor, the only rooms that occupy this level are the office, the office den, and a guest suite, making it a quiet place to escape when the clients, who share this space, need to get work done. “Our client loved the Tiger Palm wallpaper, so we applied that on all of the walls and at the low part of the angled ceiling to help mask all of the ceiling lines in this room,” Molten says. They even have their own wet bar up here for post-work happy hour.

Designed as a dedicated no-kids zone off the office, this private room is where Molten’s clients get away to watch movies after bedtime, decompress after a long day, and just retreat to when they need time to themselves.

“We opted for a performance velvet on the sofa with velvet textured pillows to create a luxurious, cozy feel,” the designer says. “I love the Currey & Co. chandelier with green glass—it works so perfectly in this small room.”

“Like the primary bedroom and primary bathroom, the closet is not huge, but it’s efficient and thoughtfully designed,” Molten explains. With her team, they designed a wall of built-ins and paid close attention to the functionality of each drawer and shelf while maximizing the space; then a local cabinet maker brought them to life.

Molten chose soft, luxurious carpet that feels like stepping into a high-end department store and a fabulous Made Goods light fixture.

The architect who worked on this house, Damien Busillo of DLB Custom Home Design, had this lower-level pool incorporated in the original plans, and Molten was able to assist with the tile selections.

“This is such a creative way to use empty space under the house,” she says. “Homes in Charleston are often raised when built due to coastal flooding; however, this was the most interesting and exciting design concept I have seen for this area of the home.” It truly captures that Grecian grotto vibe.

Megan Molten has always been creative, but her love of interiors didn’t fully manifest until she bought and decorated her first house in Charleston, South Carolina. From there, she started experimenting with design as a hobby, sharing her eye with friends and family, before devoting all her time to it and finally deciding to open her own eponymous firm. Now, she shares her elevated coastal style and luxurious Lowcountry vibe with clients across the country.

#6894. The Best Sci-Fi Movies of 2026 (So Far)

Before Avengers: Doomsday and Dune: Part Three compete at the box office, Godzilla returns to remind everyone why he’s called the “King of the Monsters.”

Two of the biggest sci-fi movies of the year are upon us, when Avengers: Doomsday and Dune: Part Three both hit theaters on December 18. We’ll see if either multimillion-dollar blockbuster has the juice to compete with this year’s crop—namely Project Hail Mary, which kicked off the year on a very high note for sci-fi films.

Directed by Phil Lord and Christopher Miller, the Ryan Gosling-starring astronaut film that plays like Interstellar but if Matthew McConaughey had a talking rock for a friend was easily one of the best sci-fi films of the past five years. We also saw The End of Oak Street earlier this summer, and Steven Spielberg returned to the world of aliens with Disclosure Day. Plus, Godzilla Minus Zero just stomped into theaters to remind everyone just why they call him the “King of the Monsters” right before Dunesday.

Until then, these are the best science-fiction movies of 2026.

The atomic monster returns to the screen following the success of Godzilla Minus One, as director Takashi Yamazaki sets up another stellar sequel for Godzilla to pulverize in his fight against King Gedorah. “In this series, Godzilla is a metaphor for war,” the filmmaker told Esquire. “What I wanted to do with this one is see how this family recovers and thrives after having suffered, and then see them survive another challenge.” Plus, leveling some cities with giant monster fights doesn’t hurt either.

The End of Oak Street is the best dinosaur film since Jurassic Park—and yes, I am including all the Jurassic Park sequels in that ranking. David Robert Mitchell’s sci-fi film about a neighborhood that is semi-transported back to prehistoric times stars Ewan McGregor and Anne Hathaway—in yet another great performance this year following The Odyssey—as the parents navigate their new Jumanji-like world.

Esquire’s Eric Francisco said it best when he described the beat-’em-up action flick based on the popular video game series as “incredibly stupid fun.” Noah Centineo and Andrew Koji star as friendly rivals Ryu and Ken alongside an oddball and eccentric cast including David Dastmalchian, Roman Reigns, Cody Rhodes, Andrew Schulz, Eric Andre, Orville Peck, 50 Cent, and Jason Momoa as the game’s various fighters. Still, amid all the chaos, it’s probably Callina Liang’s dedication to Chun-Li that shines through the brightest.

Following Barbarian and Weapons, Zach Cregger’s take on Resident Evil marks the horror director as 3-for-3 in my book. The contained yet still incredibly terrifying story follows a medical courier (Austin Abrams) on the night that a violent virus turns everyone into crazed, mutated killers. While the sci-fi/horror film definitely packs in the scares, I was still surprised how funny and accurate the film was to its video game source material. Among all the video game adaptations over the past few years, Resident Evil felt the most like actually playing the game.

One of the biggest movies of 2026 is, without question, Project Hail Mary. Based on the 2021 novel by Andy Weir, the smash hit follows a middle school science teacher (Ryan Gosling) who awakes with amnesia aboard a spacecraft light-years away from Earth. The newest movie from comedy giants Phil Lord and Christopher Miller was the right movie at the right time, its release accidentally coinciding with the Artemis II mission to the moon. That it was still playing in theaters more than a month after its release—an eternity in today’s theatrical landscape—speaks volumes about its out-of-this-world appeal.

The man who invented the modern summer blockbuster came back to theaters in 2026 with his first sci-fi in years: Disclosure Day. Emily Blunt and Josh O’Connor costar as two people who try to reveal the existence of extraterrestrial life, while the government tries to suppress the truth. It may not have been the glorious throwback to the days of E.T. and Close Encounters, but Disclosure Day gets by on career-best work by Blunt and another immaculate score by John Williams.

Alan Ritchson flexes his leading-man muscles in War Machine, a Netflix original that’s actually kinda worth watching. The Reacher star leads the movie as a staff sergeant for the Army Rangers who encounters a massively overpowered alien force during a training exercise. Sure, you’ve seen movies like this before—Predator, anyone?—but this throwback action flick is the best kind of turn-your-brain-off junk food we secretly crave.

You don’t have to be an overgrown ’80s baby to dig Masters of the Universe. This deeply affectionate reboot of the Reagan-era toy and TV franchise once again tells the story of Prince Adam (Nicholas Galitzine), who must save his fallen home of Eternia from the evil Skeletor (Jared Leto). The franchise started out as a cheap competitor to Star Wars, but the 2026 reboot is anything but a pale copy, with its dazzling visual design, snappy humor, and, most of all, a moving story about reasserting one’s place in the world.

How much you want to consider Marvel superhero movies “sci-fi” depends on you, but we safely consider radioactive spiders, mutant telepaths, and gamma-infused monsters to be firmly in the realm of science fiction. The fourth Tom Holland–led Spider-Man movie sees the web slinger working on his own until an invisible menace with the power to hijack people’s consciousness threatens everyone he still loves. It’s a box-office (Hulk) smash, but Spider-Man: Brand New Day remembers that the true story lies in the hearts of heroes.

If Guy Ritchie made Looper, it might look like Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice. Emerging out of SXSW, the Hulu exclusive follows a gangster (Vince Vaughn) who, after regretting murdering his friend (James Marsden), uses a time machine to stop his past self. There are an awful lot of action comedies nowadays, but Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice stands out from the pack with some novel twists on well-worn time-travel formulas.

Sydney Chandler (Alien: Earth) and Takehiro Hira (Giri/Haji) hit the road in this tender and emotional sci-fi road trip from first-time director Brian Tetsuro Ivie. Set in a speculative near future in which one’s consciousness can be uploaded into the cloud, Anima sees Chandler star as an unemployed engineer who drives a wealthy and terminally ill man (Hira) across Connecticut in the hopes of finding his estranged son before the end of his life. Anima premiered to rave reviews at SXSW earlier this year and may soon find wide-release distribution.

Animated children’s films about woodland creatures are usually a mixed bag. For every Bambi or The Wild Robot, you’ll find a dozen films trying to become the next Over the Hedge. Hoppers dodges the repetitive trend by magically turning a young girl into a woodland creature, and the box-office numbers prove that the gimmick has paid off so far. The film follows Mabel Tanaka, who transports her consciousness into a robot beaver. She wants to rally the animals to protect their wilderness from becoming a highway. It’s a crazy idea. But considering the way we’re headed in 2026, I’m down for anything.

This SXSW darling stars Lewis Pullman and Maya Hawke as a couple with the ability to literally shape their environment. After attending a couples-counseling session run by a set of magic wonder twins, the two on-again, off-again lovers learn that their emotions have dire effects on the world around them. The silly, video-game-like premise isn’t Wishful Thinking’s only draw, however. At its heart, director Graham Parkes’s feature debut really touches on a question we all ask ourselves. How do we change for the person we love without sacrificing what makes us who we are?

Ralph Fiennes’s Dr. Ian Kelson experiments with some real science fiction in 28 Years Later: The Bone Temple, which is one of the first zombie flicks I’ve ever seen to reckon with the idea that a cure is physically attainable. This second entry in a planned sequel trilogy to the original 28 Days Later film also features Jack O’Connell (Sinners) as a maniacal cult leader styled after Jimmy Savile—just tearing up the apocalyptic landscape with Danny Boyle’s zany, violent humor.

#6893. Is There a Hookup to Relationship Pipeline?

Yes, it’s true: my relationship started off as a hookup. My boyfriend—who I now love, am fully committed to, and have way too much fun in Costco with—used to send me 2AM “WYD” texts and avoided any sort of conversation about the future.

Yes, I made it out of the situationship trenches. I’ll hold for applause.

While I would like to pretend that I’m better than everyone else, the reality is that there is no secret method to turning your situationship into a real relationship. Sometimes—like in my case—it’s just science.

A 2018 study revealed that sexual desire can actually encourage people to engage in behaviors that help initiate romantic relationships. Through different activities like slow dancing, researchers found that sexual attraction made people want to get physically closer, mirror each other’s behaviors, become more emotionally responsive, and even become more helpful.

We often hear of relationships that begin with emotional connection and then turn into something more romantic (and eventually physical), but the other side of the coin exists—and I’m proof of it.

“Sometimes physical attraction is what initially brings two people together, and that attraction creates opportunities to spend more time together, get to know each other, and potentially discover a deeper emotional connection,” licensed psychologist Dr Matthew Leahy tells Cosmopolitan. “What starts as something purely physical can open the door to something much more meaningful.”

I started hooking up with my now-partner purely because he was hot. Turns out, he was great in bed, too. And because we enjoyed having sex so much, we just ended up spending a lot of time together—so that we could have more of said sex, of course. Eventually, in between water breaks and refueling with Uber Eats orders, we got to know each other. I remember being shocked at what a great guy he ended up being—you know, when we actually took the time to get off each other and talk. Eventually, the booty calls turned into regular phone calls, and late night hookups turned into week-long sleepovers.

But our romantic relationship wasn’t inevitable just because we were sexually attracted to each other. We had to have a lot of conversations about our values, our hopes, and most importantly, what we wanted out of a relationship.

There were also a lot of check-ins I had to do with myself. The sex was great, so I had to constantly make sure I wasn’t confusing a sexual connection for an emotional one (AKA being “dickmatized”). Dr Leahy explains that one way to do this is to ask yourself objective questions about the other person: Are they asking you questions? Is the communication strong? Are they paying attention to you? Do you have things in common besides sexual desires?

“I think it’s good to always take a step back, have some self-awareness, and truly ask yourself: Is this going somewhere? Do I want it to go somewhere, or am I OK with it the way it is?”

Coming to terms with the reality of the situation is hard, but the hardest part of it all? You have to actually put yourself out there—especially if you really want to be with that person.

“Communicating how you truly feel about somebody and what you’re looking for can happen at any time in a relationship,” Dr. Leahy says. But when you know you’re falling hard for someone, the sooner you express those things the better.

I think it’s important to take a beat and remind you that developing feelings for someone you’re constantly being sexually intimate with is totally normal. I know we’re doomed to a dating era of nonchalance, but there’s nothing wrong with liking someone you’re exchanging bodily fluids with—especially if you’re spending a lot of time together.

You just have to know the difference between when your hookup is veering into a romantic connection and when it’s purely physical.

Before you send this article to your situationship, there’s a reality check that needs to happen. Movies like Friends With Benefits and No Strings Attached made us believe that any situation where we’re hooking up with someone we like as a person is bound to lead to us falling in love. I’m sorry to tell you this isn’t true, and he won’t be waiting for you with a flashmob.

I had situationships before my current boyfriend that went nowhere but down the toilet. While the sexual chemistry was there, nothing else quite was.

So how do you tell when something is just a hookup versus the potential of something more?

“It’s important to pay attention to how the other person responds when you communicate what you’re looking for,” Dr. Leahy says.

My partner and I actually stopped talking for a while after I first initiated a conversation about wanting more than just sex. He was very honest with me and said that he wasn’t in the place in his life to pursue a commitment. I had my answer and could decide what I wanted to do with that information. Knowing that I couldn’t just continue to hook up with him without my feelings getting complicated, I cut things off.

“If you’ve been clear about wanting something more and the other person isn’t showing that same interest, you have to be willing to recognize that,” Dr. Leahy says.

We went our separate ways and grew as people. Years later, we reconnected and found ourselves on the same page.

While I’d love to tell you that this is how it always works out in the end—it’s simply not true. Unfortunately, sometimes no matter how much we like someone—despite having intense sexual chemistry—nothing comes of it. Even if you both do have romantic feelings, you both need to be in a place where you want to pursue a relationship. And had I let myself linger in the liminal space of hooking up with someone I had romantic feelings for, I wouldn’t even be in the right headspace to be in the healthy relationship I’m in now—even if it was with someone different.

“Sometimes we get caught up in hoping a relationship will become something different, rather than accepting what it actually is,” Dr. Leahy says. “You can’t convince somebody to want the same kind of relationship you do, and that’s an important part of knowing when it might be time to move on.”

So if you’re currently hooking up with someone you’re starting to develop feelings for, maybe it’s time to have a talk with yourself, and then them. You’ll either end up in a relationship, or better off for knowing that they don’t feel the same way.

#6890. Kings of Leon’s Caleb Followill Is Finally Ready to Bare It All

The lead singer tells Esquire in the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned” that the band’s new record features its most honest music to date.

Last year, Kings of Leon was forced to cancel part of its summer tour. The band’s lead singer, Caleb Followill, broke his foot and severely displaced his heel during a freak accident that he jokes was “a rock ’n’ roll injury.” But really, he admits to Esquire, he was just playing tag with his kids—a little roughhousing that turned into eight months of recovery before he could walk back onto the stage.

“I was kind of feeling sorry for myself,” Followill tells Esquire editor in chief Michael Sebastian on the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned.” The singer felt like he had let the band’s fans down. He turned 44, he lost his mother not too long ago, and he channeled all that grief and negative energy into a new, stripped-down Kings of Leon record, O My Beloved, which was released on November 6. “I had a weight on my shoulders,” he says. “And one day I picked up the guitar, and it was almost like I walked through a door and I could see things a little more clearly.”

O My Beloved is the most melancholic and reflective album in Kings of Leon’s ten-album discography. Followill tells Esquire that he wanted to speak about his childhood and the family that shaped him on the new songs because he used to feel embarrassed about his past. His family grew up poor—traveling around the South and living out of their car at times. (Kings of Leon is a family band, with Caleb joined by his brothers Nathan and Jared Followill, as well as their cousin Matthew Followill.) But now Caleb wants to pass all his memories on to his kids, and he doesn’t want them to feel ashamed about any part of the journey that led them here.

“When I was writing these songs, my mind just kept going back to small moments in my life—moments that had no significance,” he says. “I wanted to tell these stories, and I wanted to be honest about parts of my life that I didn’t want to be honest about before."

O My Beloved is the band’s first self-produced record since parting ways with RCA, its longtime record label. It’s also the band’s tenth studio album, celebrating nearly 20 years since the 2008 record Only by the Night rocketed it to stardom thanks to hit songs “Sex on Fire” and “Use Somebody.”

“I was always scared of fame, and I think I still am,” Followill says. “That’s the good thing about having your brothers next to you. They’ll rip you right back down, shine a light in your eyes, and say, ‘Hey man, this can all come crumbling down and we’ll be back in our crumbling house.’ ”

You can listen to the episode on YouTube and Spotify. Elsewhere, Followill shares his secret to 15 years of marriage (to model Lily Aldridge) and what he’s learned from touring with Pearl Jam, U2, and Zach Bryan.

#6886. Should We Be Worried About How Much Sex Married People Are Having?

Does saying “I do” mean saying “I don’t” to sex?

Stand-up comedian Greg Fitzsimmons famously said, “If you want to stop two people from having sex, let them get married.” Turns out, he’s not that off base.

According to the recent General Social Survey, 32.5 percent of married U.S. adults said they haven’t had sex “at all” in the last 12 months. That means that almost 1 in 3 married people aren’t really having sex with their spouses. I knew that the state of dating was bleak and that Gen Z was having less and less sex, but I wasn’t aware that this issue of lack of intimacy was prevalent in marriage too.

While it’s important to note that sexual frequency doesn’t give us a complete picture of what’s going on in a relationship, there is still something to be said about the state of sex in this country. So what’s going on in the bedrooms of married couples?

Ilana Grines, LMFT and licensed sex therapist, shares that she often hears couples say that they’re exhausted by the end of the day.

“Between shuffling children, work, and other responsibilities, sex frequently takes the back seat and is deprioritized,” she says. And because couples spend so much of their time mentally occupied by their to-do lists, there’s not a lot of time (or emotional energy) left over to transition into a sensual state. Intimacy requires a lot of mental and emotional space—which can be hard to tap into if you’re on “go” from the moment you wake up to the moment you go to bed.

Plus, there are a lot of mental hangups that get in the way of sex. Grines says that unresolved resentments have a lot to do with this. In heterosexual relationships, women take on a majority of the household and childcare labor. We’re often told that “foreplay starts at breakfast.” Well, if you’re the one cooking breakfast alone, feeding it to your spouse and kids, cleaning up afterwards, and then spending your entire day tending to a majority of the household tasks thinking about how your partner is pretty useless, it’s hard to be enthusiastic about getting into bed with them.

Now, some changes in your sex life are just inevitable. There’s work stress, medication changes, pain, and moving through different life stages. These things, Grines says, can cause shifts in desire.

“These ebbs may be met with a decrease in sexual activity, but do not typically include a change in affection, warmth, and trust,” she says.

But how a partner shows up during these times does affect the relationship. If a partner is unkind, unhelpful, or detached during life changes, it can result in the other partner being closed off to sex or any kind of intimacy.

How do you both open up (literally and figuratively) again?

“A good way to nurture a long term sexual relationship is to understand what conditions are required for desire to exist in the first place,” Grines says. The first step is to get curious.

If you notice your partner pull away—or notice yourself pulling away—it’s important to try to understand what’s happening in your relationship or your own life that may be contributing to this change. Grines suggests posing questions like, “What do we do that allows us to make sex more inviting?” “Are we always just leaving it to be the last item on our to-do list.. or prioritizing it?,” and “Do we need to start scheduling time to just be intimate, without the pressure of having sex?”

By making these questions part of a regular relationship check in, you and your partner can mentally make a note that sex is an important part of your relationship.

“It is also really important to redefine what it means to have sex,” Grines says. She explains that some couples think penetration is the only way to maintain an intimate connection. But there’s affectionate touch, cuddling, kissing, lap-sitting, and basically any kind of touch that can help create a sense of connection without the pressure of full-blown sex.

And of course, you have to get to the root of the issue. No matter how much you cuddle with your partner, if there is a feeling of resentment around your relationship, then this needs to be addressed. Intimate touch is not a bandaid for the relational issues you and your partner are facing. You’re going to have to either talk it out or seek the help of a professional to guide the conversation. Yes, sex is important for most relationships—but the actual relationship part should be a priority.

If you’re worried that saying “I do” is going to automatically mean you’re never going to have sex again, relax. Marriage is not some death sentence for your sex life. But it will take some work to help create a sexual connection with your partner that withstands all of the change that marriage (or any serious, longterm relationship) brings.

“New relationship energy comes with anticipation, separateness, novelty, and a lot of intentional time devoted to each other,” Grines says. “That intentionality often fades in long term relationships which often contributes to the lessening of sexual intimacy.”

“Change over time happens for many couples, but losing intimacy altogether is far from inevitable,” she says. “Couples can not control every factor that influences desire, but they can influence and understand which conditions exist that make desire more likely to exist.” For you and your partner, this may look like spending uninterrupted time together, being playful, addressing any issues as they arise, working to create an equal partnership, and constantly talking about what pleasure and connection means to the both of you.

The earlier in the relationship you talk about sex, the better chance you have at preserving a sexual connection throughout it.

Grines encourages couples to refrain from measuring themselves against some invisible standard when it comes to sex. Sure, you might want to have sex more than once in a year—but maybe you don’t need it all the time. And there’s nothing wrong with that.

“Focus on creating a satisfying sex life to the parties involved,” Grines says. “Pne that is able to be discussed and adapted together as their life changes.”

#6877. The Ultimate Guide to the Rolex Daytona, the World’s Most Wanted Watch

From the hand-wound watches dealers struggled to sell to the steel model that commands an impressive premium today.

In September, four Rolex Daytonas sold at a charity auction in London for a combined £37.3 million (about $49.5 million). The most expensive, at £10.3 ($13.7) million, had a turquoise dial and a ring of pink sapphires. That same month, Rolex unveiled a steel-and-platinum Daytona with a white enamel dial and a speed scale that recalled the original from 1963.

The regular steel Daytona with a white dial costs $16,900 from Rolex, if you can get one. In September, WatchCharts put its secondary-market value around $35,000. It wasn’t always like this. In its early years, according to Sotheby’s, retailers discounted Daytonas to clear stock.

Rolex launched the Cosmograph in 1963. An early advert called it the “Le Mans,” after the French endurance race. The name that stuck came from Daytona, Florida, where cars raced on hard-packed sand before the city had a speedway.

The first model, reference 6239, had a movement you wound by hand and two buttons to operate the stopwatch. Rolex put the speed scale on the steel bezel, where a driver could read it at a glance. The three small counters stood out against the dial, black on a light face or light on a black one.

By the 1970s, two models had emerged that collectors still prize today. The 6263 had a black acrylic bezel, and the 6265 had a metal one. Both had screw-down collars around the stopwatch buttons. On some, the word Daytona curves in red above the bottom counter. Collectors call these “Big Reds.”

Joanne Woodward gave Newman a 6239 around the time he made Winning, the 1969 film that sparked his interest in racing. She had “DRIVE CAREFULLY ME” engraved on the back. “There’s something almost magical about it,” James Cox, who later owned the watch, told Phillips. In 2017 it sold for $17.75 million, then an auction record for a wristwatch.

A “Paul Newman” usually refers to a type of dial. Look for the unusual numerals and small square-ended markers in the counters. The dials were unpopular when new. By 2013, Christie’s could sell 50 Daytonas in a single Geneva auction for $13.2 million. The top lot was a steel watch with a Paul Newman dial. It fetched $1.09 million, four years before the actor’s own watch went to auction.

On one particularly rare black-dial 6263, the word Oyster appears below Rolex Cosmograph. Collectors call it the Oyster Sotto, from the Italian for under. Phillips sold one in 2025 for 1.39 million Swiss francs (approximately $1.67 million). “One mistake people make is that they expect everything to fit neatly into a specific set of rules,” Geoff Hess, global head of watches at Sotheby’s, once explained. A dial can be genuine Rolex and still have come from a different watch.

In 1988 the steel Daytona became the 16520. It grew to 40mm, gained raised gold hour markers and a glossier dial, and wound itself as you wore it. Rolex extensively reworked Zenith’s El Primero movement for the watch. It was the first automatic Daytona and the model that established the basic dimensions of the watch Rolex still makes today.

On some early examples, a gap makes the word Cosmograph appear to float beneath the rest of the lettering. On certain black dials, the rings around the counters have turned brown with age. Those are known as Patrizzi dials, after the auctioneer Osvaldo Patrizzi, who noticed the change.

Three steel references are worth knowing. The 116520, introduced in 2000, has a steel bezel and the first chronograph movement Rolex developed and made itself. The 116500LN, from 2016, brought back the look of the black acrylic bezel in ceramic. The current 126500LN, introduced in 2023, has a slim steel rim around that ceramic bezel, a revised dial, and a new movement that runs for about three days when fully wound. The two ceramic models can look remarkably similar in listing photographs.

The unicorn is a hand-wound 6265 that Phillips described in 2018 as the only known vintage Daytona in white gold. It sold for 5.94 million Swiss francs (about $7.1 million). The Rainbow gets its name from the graduated sapphires around its bezel. A white-gold example made around 1994, nearly two decades before the production versions appeared, sold in 2024 for 5.505 million Swiss francs (approximately $6.1 million).

Sixty years after that early advertisement, Rolex returned to Le Mans. Its 2023 watch for the race’s centenary has a stopwatch that counts the full 24 hours. The regular Daytona counts to 12.

An official Rolex retailer is the place to start if you want a new one at list price. Retailers manage their own allocations, so availability varies. Rolex addressed the shortage in 2021. “The scarcity of our products is not a strategy on our part,” it said, explaining that it could not meet demand without compromising quality.

On the secondary market, compare the same reference and dial. The WatchCharts September estimate for the current black-dial steel model was about $29,000, roughly $6,000 less than the white. If you’re buying vintage, ask a specialist dealer or auction house for a condition report. Find out whether the case has been heavily polished, which parts have been replaced, and whether the dial is correct for that particular watch. On a Daytona, those details can explain more about the price than the reference number.

#6874. Nicole Kidman’s Long ‘Almond Cream’ Nails Are the Sophisticated Go-With-Everything Trend to Try Now: Get the Look

This manicure proves long almond nails never go out of style.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There are certain manicures that never go out of style, regardless of the season. Case in point: Nicole Kidman’s long ‘almond cream’ nails. Spotted at the LACMA Art+Film Gala wearing softly tapered almond nails in a glossy milky shade, the actor’s manicure served as a reminder that winter manicures needn’t be dark or glittery to make an impact.

“Kidman’s nails are a great example of how to wear a longer length without them looking too dramatic,” says Susan Carroll, founder of Young LDN. “The almond shape is one of my favorites because it’s incredibly flattering on the hands. The sides taper in slightly before finishing in a soft, rounded point, which helps elongate the fingers and gives a much more delicate appearance than a square or coffin shape.”

Serving as an ideal entry point for anyone looking to experiment with longer nails without committing to a dramatic stiletto shape, the almond silhouette offers a more wearable alternative. Carroll also recommends adapting the length to suit your lifestyle and comfort level. The appeal lies in its versatility, as it can be easily achieved without the need for extensions. “The shape does a lot of the work, and keeping the color subtle makes the overall look feel much more elegant,” she adds.

Re-creating the look is very straightforward, as the key lies in the shape. Ask your nail technician for a soft almond silhouette with rounded tips, rather than anything too sharply pointed. “The key is making sure the nails are evenly shaped and the cuticles are tidy, as there’s nowhere to hide imperfections with such a simple manicure,” says Carroll. To finish, simply apply two coats of a sheer milky beige polish before sealing with a glossy top coat for a light-reflecting finish.

The result is an effortlessly elegant manicure that feels equal parts timeless and wearable.
