#6871. Hanifa Is Back: Inside the Return of the Black-Owned Luxury Fashion Brand After an Eight-Month Pause

After eight months away, Anifa Mvuemba reflects on the decision to pause and shares what's ahead.

“Is that Hanifa?” is a question I got often walking down any street, from Miami, New York, to D.C. and Los Angeles. The clothing brand built a cult following over the years, largely made of career-focused, fashion-obsessed Black women around the world who loved existing as a “Hanifa woman.” Anifa Mvuemba originally launched the label in 2012, initially gaining traction after she posted a self-made 21st-birthday dress on Instagram. The brand slowly continued to grow, and then eventually blew up on a widespread global stage during the COVID-19 pandemic when it launched its groundbreaking “Pink Label Congo” collection via a viral 3D virtual runway using headless digital avatars.

The independent brand became synonymous with luxury, sensuality, and well-made designs. But that all came to a halt this past March when Mvuemba announced a pause for the brand due to an assortment of backend issues—from shipping delays, warehouse complications, and burnout.

But after taking time for herself and resetting, Mvuemba is entering a new chapter. And Hanifa is set to return November 18, 2026. We caught up with the 35-year-old founder to talk about her time away and what shoppers can expect now that Hanifa is coming back.

Honestly, Hanifa is like a part of my DNA at this point. I started this brand when I was 21, and so I think I just needed a moment to just take a breather, rest, be with my new baby, enjoy my family, enjoy this time, and just think about how I wanted to move forward. It was a lot, but eventually I was just like, ‘Okay, I need to get back to work,’ because I genuinely enjoy and love what I do, creating. I'm such a creative person, and I love business. I can't leave a Hanifa alone like that. I can't do it.

I really was thinking about the opportunity that this pause awarded us. It gave us the opportunity to build this dream world of Hanifa that I've always wanted to have. To be completely honest, within the last couple of years, I always wanted to have a moment to fix things, rebrand a few things, and I felt like I finally got the opportunity to be able to do that without feeling the pressure of doing all of that. So we were able to go back into our archives. I really wanted to start there, and think about the woman that we are building for. Just looking at those moments that the customers fell in love with from the beginning, and reintroducing that in different ways. Of course, keeping the woman, her body, at the center. I wanted for this new era for the brand in design to feel a little bit more elevated, a little refined, while still remaining true to Hanifa. And then just exploring different things. We really wanted to try different materials. Knitwear being our biggest category, wanting to explore different ways that we can do knitwear, color, textures, fits, patterns, while still staying true to the brand and what people love us for.

I really understood the importance of resting. I'm someone that enjoys working, but I think rest is actually necessary to be able to have peace of mind. I thought it was necessary for me to be present for my family, also for me to be the best leader that I could be for my team, and I think it's okay to rest. I'm grateful that we were able to even pause for this long. It's not usually the norm for independent brands. But I'm grateful that we even had that opportunity to be able to say, “Hey, we're gonna step away for eight months, 10 months, and just figure it out and do all the things that we needed to do.”

I'm excited for people to experience this new era of Hanifa. We've been working so hard, considering every touchpoint for the customer, restructuring the business. I mean, we've literally been uprooting everything, and refining, going back and forth and redoing the website. I'm very, very excited for everyone to really experience this world that we're building. And so the collection is called World of Anifa. We're now allowing them into this space and making the brand feel more full. We always speak about how we've grown with our customers, we're journeying with our customers, and wanting them to experience what it is to be in this world, and how we actually consider them in the decisions we make too.

She's evolving. I think we're all evolving. I was 21 when I started, my perspective was different. Now I'm turning 36 this year, and everything is shifting. So she's grown with us. We've grown with her. She's rediscovering herself. She's maybe a new mom. Maybe she just got her new executive position. Maybe she just started a business. She's in a new season in her life, and so she still wants to feel seen. She still wants to look good. Also a big thing that we're building is the categories, really diversifying that so that she can really have Hanifa all the time. Kid drop-offs, airport outfit. You know, we're thinking about every single detail.

I don't care. Period. But seriously, it took a while for me to get to this space. I do care actually because I see it affecting my peers and people that I care about, too. When I announced the pause, I received so many DMs, messages, and support from other women who own businesses, Black women, too, who shared what they've experienced, similar situations of what we went through. And I realized that there really isn't a safe space for us in this world, and so that's also something that I'm working on on the back end, figuring out how we can have these safe spaces for founders like me, where we can lean on each other, support each other, because I think we'll just be stronger as a unit. Literally, a friend of mine reached out to me recently. Was just like, “Oh my god, this is going on.” I'm just like, “Here's what to do. Here's what you could do.” Being able to support each other in that way, I think that's the most important thing because you really can't focus on the noise. I think that was the hardest thing: just type it, delete it. Respond and delete your response. But at the end of the day, the algorithm makes you feel like everyone is talking about it when in reality they're not. So you just kind of have to take it for what it is and move on.

I think in this new era, I really want to focus on educating the customer and just letting them know what it is, and figuring out the best way that we can talk about what goes into building these brands that we have and building this world and letting them in, giving them a little bit more of that insight, so that there's more of an understanding. Because even with the chatter that happened earlier this year, as I was just processing and reflecting and reviewing, I was like, “There's also a lot of like misinformation,” and I'm like, “I don't even know if they really even understand.” We want to make sure we have an educational component within that so that we can make sure that that's clear because the girls need to know.

Aiyana Ishmael is the style editor at Cosmopolitan magazine. In her work, Aiyana focuses on the culture of fashion and how it intertwines and shapes the zeitgeist. She is an award-winning journalist from Miami, Florida, and a graduate of the historically Black university, Florida A&M. She is a 2024 Forbes 30 Under 30: Media honoree, and her debut romance novella PASSING GAME is set to release March of 2027 (831 Stories/Simon & Schuster).

#6869. Tommy Lee Jones Couldn’t Care Less About His Legacy

The screen legend has more interesting things going on—including a stellar turn on The Lowdown season 2, as an estranged, crusty old father.

Tommy Lee Jones, 80, an Oscar winner and a star of the second season of The Lowdown, the noirish Tulsa crime story undercut with terrific dime-store waggery, sits flat-footed in the green room at Good Morning America, in downtown Manhattan. He has just done an on-camera interview with Ethan Hawke, who stars as the show’s protagonist. In the next dressing room are many adorable, pawing puppies being primped for a segment on National Adopt-a-Dog Month. Jones is adorable too, in his avuncular way, his face an old-dog scowl that softens when you ask the right question, as if you’d scratched him in the right spot.

On The Lowdown, he plays Hawke’s estranged father, a crusty old bastard who holds secrets. There’s a bad history between them, and Hawke’s character, an impulsive, truth-seeking investigative journalist, at first wants nothing to do with his dad, but that situation evolves. They’re both named Lee Raybon.

Jones wears a glen-plaid sport coat, a blue dress shirt, and comfortable sneakers. When the interview begins, he looks at you, ready but giving nothing until he’s asked to.

ESQUIRE: I just watched the first six episodes of this season, and man, I wasn’t ready for [REDACTED].

TOMMY LEE JONES: Oh, you watched all those episodes? The [REDACTED] scene was—I hope that was kind of shocking.

We didn’t see it coming, which I guess is a testament to you all.

That’s the whole point. You didn’t see it coming. That’s the reason we did it.

The way you developed your character’s way of speaking, the way he trails off here and there, tells us that this guy knows some stuff he’s not giving away. He’s shifty. It’s just brilliant. I’m just trying to give a compliment, not really asking a question.

You said to Charlie Rose in an interview a long time ago that there’s no through line that runs through the characters you play. At the same time, you don’t take just any role that comes along. Do you ever think about legacy? How are they going to remember Tommy Lee Jones? Or is it really just “This role would be a challenge to play, so I’m going to take it?”

I don’t have long-term thinking, and I don’t even think in a term like legacy. I think more like a dog. I’m absorbed with what’s in front of me. I might run off and chase a squirrel.

I believe you. It’s just that I’ve started thinking recently, What am I writing about? Is there a body of work here? I hadn’t thought that way before. With each story it was just This is a cool story. I’ll do this story.

That’s what I thought. I thought this show was really cool. The people that were working on it were cool, and Tulsa is an extremely cool place. Ethan made a good point on TV just now when he said good noir stories always have a sense of place, so the location was very important. It’s true about film noir, and this show really qualifies as part of that genre. A keen sense of place, leading to inarguable originality.

How far are Tulsa and eastern Oklahoma from where you live in Texas?

Oh, I don’t know. It’s northeast of Dallas. So I think the drive up—

[He turns to his wife of of 25 years, Dawn, who sits in the corner looking at her phone.]

How long would it take to drive to Tulsa from San Antonio?

When I was in prep school, we went up to Oklahoma to play a school in football, and they always cheated. Also when I was in high school, they would sell 3.2 beer to minors, and it was fun, a big adventure every once in a while, to pile into a car and drive to Oklahoma to buy a beer.

Ethan’s hilarious on The Lowdown, but you’re also very funny! Always have been. The warden in Natural Born Killers, Dwight McClusky, is your funniest role, I feel. Samuel Gerard in The Fugitive. And then I’m watching this show and was reminded how funny you can be. How would you describe your sense of humor? What’s funny to you?

People. People are hilarious. The best humor is found in human behavior. Any student of human behavior is going to be highly amused, when they’re not horrified.

Did you watch stuff as a kid that made you laugh?

The Three Stooges. Slapstick. Warner Brothers cartoons. That’s fundamental humor. I mean, if I see a fat lady slip on a banana peel, I swear I’m going to laugh.

You share some beautiful scenes with the young actress who plays your granddaughter, Ryan Kiera Armstrong. I couldn’t think of many films in which we see you spend a lot of time with children.

There was a movie called River Rats, and I was on a boat for a good part of this movie with two children, one of whom was Martha Plimpton, as a little slip of a girl, playing my daughter. We became good friends on that, and I was happy to see her on The Lowdown.

You and Ethan, and Martha now too, are seasoned veterans. Is the approach different when you’re working with young Ryan, who doesn’t have 50 films under her belt? Is there a greater need with a child actor to get to know each other before filming?

Oh, actors are actors, so it’s not difficult for me to relate to an actor regardless of their age. And she’s a pro. Actors, they have a way of getting to know one another very quickly.

Yeah, she is. And she’s a fearless and admirable young lady.

[Shit.] Not from you. I don’t mean in your scenes. I mean her other scenes.

Getting back to the idea of a through line: If I may point out one commonality across your performances, it’s that your characters have wisdom. You yourself are obviously well-read. You went to Harvard, and, for example, you’ve compared the satire of Natural Born Killers to Molière. Is reading still a big part of your life?

I’ve been making a study of Texas history, which begins with—well, there is no beginning. It’s always been there. But its experience as an American territory, for me, begins with James K. Polk. I read a political biography of Polk. That’s a good place to start a study of modern Texas history.

Yeah, well, he was Andrew Jackson’s bitch.

Getting back to Lee Raybon Sr., whose nickname is Frog, and his son. Fathers and sons are always rich territory.

Ethan’s character changes his attitude toward you. It gets better. Did you see that as Frog’s project, what this character is after—his son’s love?

[Mildly annoyed.] Well, I compared myself to a dog earlier because I really deal with what’s in front of me.

And all the qualities that you see in that character are ones that Sterlin Harjo, the show’s creator, wrote. We learn that this character is dying. That slowly begins to dawn on us, and we see that he’s trying to somehow reconcile life with his son and get to know his granddaughter. But that quest in this character is not advertised. It’s discovered as we watch things unfold in an often amusing way, and always original. That was appealing. I mean, I think that’s the way it works.

Can I, at the risk of pissing you off: I know you say that you’re like a dog. Are you telling me that—

I don’t want to belabor that too much. I’m a little bit like a dog. [Laughs, kind of.]

But this is what I’m getting at. I’ve seen every film you’ve ever made, and you don’t give a lot of interviews, but in the ones I’ve seen over the years you’ve made it clear that each character is its own separate project. No connections. But at the same time, I know how smart you are, and so part of me thinks, Come on. Of course you must see some continuum along the characters you play. We see similarities among characters in literature, even across different authors in different centuries. The through line of all your parts is that you chose them. So pushing back a tiny bit, I’m suggesting that your performance as Lee Raybon has inherited something from these previous men that you’ve inhabited in the last 50 years. Is that fair at all?

[Breathing a sigh of relief, and perhaps satisfaction.] Okay.

[Pauses, hoping this line of questioning was over.] Oh. Well, I don’t know. I’m not aware of any continuum. [Any sense of Esquire’s relief or satisfaction disappears.] I don’t think it’s in my best interest, and I’m not inclined to think about what all the characters I play have in common. The only thing they have in common is that I’m playing them. And they’re all built out of experience. Let me refer you to an essay by T.S. Eliot called “Tradition and the Individual Talent.” It’s an indication of what happens to actors, really. Their performances are made out of the shards and the pieces and the bits of your experience of life and your thinking about it. One of the reasons you read that essay is so you have a better understanding of The Waste Land or even James Joyce. Modern art is comprised of the shards of experience, reassembled in a way that has a dramatic form, with a beginning, a middle, an end, and a purpose.

Do you see a performance such as this one as a piece of art?

No. Not really. I don’t know what it—you know, this is the thing I never give any thought to: What is art?

Compare your view of God when you were six or seven years old to now.

[Coming to life a bit.] Oh! Well? I never imagined a man in a white robe with a long white beard sitting on a throne mounted on a cloud with some lightning bolts in his hand. That’s one image. It’s probably almost as good as any other, but the question is, you either believe in such a thing as God or you don’t. I do.

Has your view or understanding changed over your life?

Not really. My grandmother and my aunts and my mother always took me to a Southern Baptist church. I was baptized in the Southern Baptist church. And, like all organized religions, there’s a good deal of nonsense in it. But it also leads to an awareness of the deity, which by definition exceeds our capability to define it.

[A long pause as Jones stares at the floor, then looks up, eyebrows raised and smiling.]

That was pretty good. I’m gonna write that down.

If you had to cook a meal for dinner, what’s the best thing you could come up with?

[For the first time during the interview, Jones’s body relaxes—arm over the back of the chair.]

I’d probably buy some pork ribs, an entire side of a little young pig, and slowly barbecue that over a mesquite fire and serve it with a mixture of sautéed yellow squash and onions and probably a sweet potato. You can scratch them with a fork and rub them down with butter, wrap them up in tinfoil and cook them alongside the pig right over the fire. You can feed a lot of people that way, and it tastes good. And it’s fun to eat.

There’s a product you can buy in San Saba, Texas, called Bill’s Season All. Bill’s Season All is something you sprinkle on pork ribs and it makes them taste real good. I don’t know what all’s in it, but it’s delectable.

Texas chili: Do you have strong opinions?

[Jones snaps to even more, speaking a little louder and with some of the gusto and urgency of U.S. Marshal Samuel Gerard saying, “Listen up, ladies and gentlemen. Our fugitive has been on the run for 90 minutes. Average foot speed over uneven ground barring injuries is four miles an hour, and that gives us a radius of six miles. What I want out of each and every one of you is a hard-target search of every gas station, residence, warehouse, farmhouse, henhouse, outhouse, and doghouse in that area. Checkpoints go up at 15 miles. Your fugitive’s name is Dr. Richard Kimble. Go get him.”]

Don’t use ground beef. You need to cube it. Cube that beef into pieces about the size of the dice that you play Monopoly with. And after that, your own creativity comes in with the spices. But it’s important to avoid ground beef. And you don’t put beans in chili. That’s on the side. That’s at the discretion of the diner. Some people just like chili, but they just don’t necessarily want to see a bunch of beans floating in it. You’ll find one recipe in the Hill Country of Texas and another one in the hills above Santa Fe. Quite different.

Last night, I was watching either the fifth or sixth episode of The Lowdown, and Martha Plimpton’s character talks about putting a couple spoonfuls of Tang in chili.

I can’t imagine putting Tang in chili. But chili is like gumbo. Or Kentucky burgoo. Or Irish stew. It’s a food that’s in and of the people, so the way of cooking it and the ingredients vary from place to place and from circumstance to circumstance. Real gumbo has always got—well, it can have anything in it. Duck … goose … whatever crawled across the porch last night. Chili’s the same kind of thing. It’s the food of the people, and as such, the main ingredients are whatever you’ve got.

I don’t think so. I’m closer to profligate than frugal. We spend a lot of money on horses. My wife is one of the better female polo players in the United States. I played polo for 40-some-odd years. I’m a bit too old for it now. “Old and worn-out” is what they would call it.

Where did you live when you lived here in New York?

Yeah, it was. When I first came here in 1969, the streets were covered in dog shit. And it really got to be bad. Land mines. They passed some ordinances in the city that people had to clean up after their dogs. You’d have to be very careful walking down the street. Nothing more embarrassing than going into a meeting with dog shit on your shoes.

So now that you’ve turned in this astonishingly beautiful performance as Lee Raybon—

—and are there hills left to conquer as an actor? Are there types of roles you have yet to play?

I don’t know. There’s a schedule for the day. And I looked at it, but I don’t really—I’d have to ask her. That’s the reason I don’t have to know. I have her.

Okay, well, I appreciate this. I hope it wasn’t too painful and I didn’t—

I know talking about acting is not the most interesting thing.

Well, it’s breakfast time. When it’s first thing in the morning at home, if your cook’s not there and you’re hungry, what could you whip up?

I have a French press for coffee, a toaster for a bagel. And we’ve always got cream cheese and smoked salmon. Bagel, lox, cream cheese, coffee, and a grapefruit. That’s what I ordinarily eat for breakfast.

You’re right, this is more interesting than talking about acting.

Oh, breakfast. Breakfast is more interesting than anything.

#6866. Clueless Limited TV Series: What We Know

Hollywood is still deep in the throes of ’90s nostalgia, as evidenced by the current slew of turn-of-the-millennium projects studios are eager to remix or reboot into prequels, sequels, and spinoffs. The 1995 fan-favorite film Clueless was initially set for a sequel TV series on Peacock, with star Alicia Silverstone meant to reprise her original, iconic role as Cher Horowitz, but the show fell out of development—until another major streamer picked up the show after Peacock and gave it a green light as a limited series instead.

Here’s everything we know about the progress of the Clueless reboot.

CBS Studios and Universal Television planned to develop Cher’s latest saga for Peacock. Josh Schwartz and Stephanie Savage of Gossip Girl fame were slated to write and executive produce alongside Dollface creator (and Freakier Friday writer) Jordan Weiss. The 1995 film’s writer-director Amy Heckerling and producer Robert Lawrence also signed on as executive producers.

Deadline reported in July 2026 that Paramount+ picked up the project as a limited series. Weiss, Savage, and Schwartz are still writing and executive producing the project.

“We decided to make it an event series because it deserves to be an event,” Paramount+ head of originals Jane Wiseman told Deadline in an August 2026 interview. “We feel like this is going to be something that people are going to come to in droves—already you can tell—if you want to get the cast back together, if you want to see where everyone is.”

Per Deadline, the story will pick up 30 years after the original film: “Beloved Beverly Hills icon Cher Horowitz (Silverstone) has figured out a lot: She’s successful in business and has mastered motherhood—that is, until her daughter’s high school years, and Cher finds that parenting a teenager makes her feel ‘clueless’ all over again.”

Silverstone is still attached to star in the project. “She will reprise her role as the Beverly Hills’ It girl Cher Horowitz and will serve as an executive producer on the sequel,” Deadline shared in its August 2026 report.

While unconfirmed, the show is aiming “to bring as many of the film’s stars—which include Paul Rudd, Stacey Dash, Donald Faison, Wallace Shawn, Jeremy Sisto, and Breckin Meyer—back.”

Meyer did join the show. In addition to acting in it, he will also be writing new plotlines for his character, Travis Birkenstock. During his November 2026 appearance on One Question, he said he’s contributing “writing on a character that I played 30 years ago, and trying to figure out what he would say now, and it’s really fun to put it in my head and try to think.”

“When [the new series] came about, I was like, ‘I’m curious, what are you guys thinking?’” he added. “When I read it, I was like, ‘Oh, that’s actually pretty damn interesting, so let’s delve into that.’”

Not yet. Deadline noted that Clueless was able to receive its green light because of a pilot script, but the following episodes have not been written yet. The show will begin filming in Los Angeles in 2027.

In June 2025, Alicia Silverstone, Elisa Donovan, Breckin Meyer, Amy Heckerling, as well as costume designer Mona May and casting director Marcia Ross celebrated the film’s 30th anniversary at a screening and panel at the Academy Museum of Motion Pictures in Los Angeles. At the time, Silverstone said there was “nothing” she could reveal about the TV sequel in an interview with People at the event, but Heckerling admitted she was “thrilled to death” it was happening. Silverstone was also asked if she thought she would ever play Cher again.

She replied, “I didn’t. And then I did it for the Super Bowl, yeah, and it was so much fun. We did it really at the Super Bowl.” The star was referring to her ad for Rakuten that aired during the big game:

In August 2025, Silverstone shared more about the project in an interview on Today, saying, “I’m really excited about it. I think we’re going to try really hard. The goal is to make it honor what everyone loves about Clueless and Cher. So we want to honor that, but also bring something fresh and new to it. I’m confident that we’ll be able to do that, but we’re in baby stages right now.”

#6865. I Paid a Matchmaker $250,000 to Find My Soulmate. The Result Surprised Me.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Sure, I could go on Tinder, swipe through profiles, and find women interested in me. But that’s a long process, and I don’t want to put in the same effort I did 20 or 30 years ago.

Dating apps have become a special kind of hell for singles. People are sick of swiping. They’re tired of the road-to-nowhere conversations and disappointing dates. For all their predictive power, algorithms simply aren’t very good at finding the one.

As a result, people are dumping Tinder left and right. But they haven’t given up on love entirely. In fact, quite the opposite. After years of letting the apps play Cupid, they’re calling in the experts. Google searches for “matchmaker” more than doubled between January 2025 and 2026. And as interest in matchmaking grows, prices are climbing, too. Average fees jumped 26 percent last year. Prices vary enormously, but at the highest end of the market, finding a girlfriend can cost more than a Ferrari.

For the latest installment of The Secret Lives of Men, we spoke to Henry*, a 70-year-old man from Atlanta who spent $250,000 on a matchmaker. His fee covered a year of personalized matchmaking, complete with a wardrobe consultant, dating coaches, personality tests, and a nationwide search for eligible women. After eight months and roughly ten introductions, Henry finally met someone he liked.

*Name and identifying details have been changed to protect anonymity.

I’m what I would call a silver fox. I’m 70 years old now, and I’m in excellent physical, mental, and financial shape. I’ve been married twice and dated plenty of women. Over the years, I’ve tried just about every way to meet someone: bars, introductions through friends, dating sites, apps. But at this stage of my life, I can afford the very best—so I decided to shell out $250,000 for a top-of-the-line matchmaker.

Some people spend $250,000 on a car or $75,000 on a vacation in Positano. Others fly private. Sure, you could spend a fraction of that money, but you’d get a lesser experience. I look at matchmaking the same way. Sure, I could go on Tinder, swipe through profiles, and find women interested in me. But that’s a long process, and I don’t want to put in the same effort I did 20 or 30 years ago. I'm interested in finding someone I’m actually compatible with.

I’ve actually used cheaper matchmakers before, the kind who charge around $25,000 for a few months. Mostly, they just save you the trouble of going through dating apps yourself. They’ll find a few women on Tinder, a few more somewhere else, and send you their profiles. This time I wanted someone who would actually get to know me and put some thought into who she set me up with. That’s why I hired Sameera Sullivan. She works with only three or four clients at a time and charges $250,000 for a year of services that go far beyond what the other matchmakers offered. Those guys were like a Lexus. Sameera is a Bugatti.

The first thing she did was bring in a few professionals to help with my dating profile. A wardrobe consultant went through my clothes and suggested a few changes. A photographer took my pictures, and a professional writer put together my bio. Then I had several sessions with a dating coach and took a bunch of personality tests to figure out what kind of woman I’d be most compatible with.

Each date I went on helped Sameera narrow things down further. After every introduction, she’d ask both me and the woman she'd set me up with how it went. Even if we stopped seeing each other after two or three dates, she’d try to figure out why and use what she learned to make a better match next time. It’s kind of like AI. The more information she had, the better the results. The tenth woman she introduced me to ended up being the one. Sameera actually found her on LinkedIn and reached out to see if she’d be interested in going out with me.

We’ve been dating for almost three months now, and things are going extremely well. Everyone says we have big couples energy. She’s in her mid-50s, which is good because I look and act younger than my biological age. Our love languages match, our personalities complement each other, and I feel more compatible with her than I did with either of my former wives.

One thing I really like about her is that she’s got a bit of an adventurous streak. I’m naturally pretty steady and set in my ways, but I have an adventurous side, too, and she brings it out in me. Like, the other night, we were having dinner at a Mexican restaurant, and someone mentioned there was a speakeasy downstairs. Normally, I would've said we should try it another time. But I saw her face light up, so I said, “Okay, let’s go.” We went in and had a drink. I probably wouldn’t have done that on my own.

I’ve put my matchmaking contract on pause for now. If this relationship doesn’t work out, I still have about four months of service left. For now, I’m very happy with how things are going. The funniest thing about it all, though, is that the woman Sameera found for me, the one she searched all over the country for—she actually lives in my neighborhood.

#6863. Nicola Coughlan Goes “Cowboy Beige” For Fall

#6860. Dialed In: All the Time in the World

Accutron’s bold new Horizon Chronograph unites all the best elevated complications in watchmaking in a single watch at a fantastic price.

Every now and then, a watch comes along that demolishes all your preconceived notions of the way things are in watchmaking. This week, Accutron released the Horizon, an entirely new timepiece (in two colorways, blue and black) that positively bristles with complications normally associated with five- and six-figure watches and at a very attractive price to boot.

It's a bold move from the brand that created the very first fully electronic watch back in 1960. With that model, Accutron debuted its tuning fork technology, by which a traditional mechanical watch escapement, with all its susceptibility to friction and gravity, was replaced with a tiny metal tuning fork powered by electricity via a built-in transistor. What made the tuning fork ground-breaking was the fact that, under power, it vibrated at a constant and continuous rate. And it made the aptly named Accutron (hence the name) accurate to plus or minus one second a month, an unimagined degree of accuracy at that point, and, frankly, for a long time after too.

Back to the Horizon. First up in a litany of nifty and rather mind-blowing gizmos is the fact that this Master Complication tells you the time not only with the hands of the watch in the conventional way, but also with sound. This watch is a minute repeater, chiming the current time in hours, then quarter hours, then minutes at the press of a button. Derived in the 18th century from the need to know the time at night a good century before electric light and SuperLuminova markers made their appearance, the minute repeater could—once you got the hang of it—tell you the exact time even in pitch darkness. But it's a complication so rare and expensive to make, that we honestly can't think of a minute repeater ever produced at such a compelling price.

The bells and whistles don't stop there. With three sub dials in glittery black sandstone that looks like the night sky) set into a granular dial made from Denchu, another granular sandstone, all beneath the conventional center hands, this watch is both a Perpetual Calendar and Chronograph. It features the day and the date, the leap year, the phases of the moon, a second time zone and a day/night indicator, all packed into an elegant and sporty 41.5mm stainless steel case. This, by the way, is made in a superior 904L steel with a matching steel bracelet, an alloy known for its deep luster when polished, as well as its resistance to corrosion and pitting. Underlying all this style is the Horizon's substance; the watch is driven by a quartz movement that offers excellent accuracy.

If you want to understand the meaning of time, you only have to gaze into the Horizon's dial, which breaks it all down for you in a very cool package. Reading it will keep you busy for days.

#6856. Justin Theroux Took Me on a Nearly Perfect Trip to Japan. Here’s the Itinerary.

“There’s tourism, and there’s travel,” the actor says. After four days across Tokyo and Kyoto, he reveals exactly what that means.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Justin Theroux carefully holds the brush perpendicular to the sheet of paper. For the last twenty-odd minutes, he’s been learning how to press, glide, press a single Japanese kanji character: 永, eternity.

The fifty-five-year-old actor has been practicing each one methodically, including a particularly tricky “airplane” maneuver in which he must hold the brush outward, away from his chest, and guide it gently across the sheet to make a tail that broadens as it travels before ending on a punctuating point at which he must deftly lift his hand away.

Despite praise from his instructor, a renowned Japanese calligrapher called Mamimozi—“Sugoi!” she exclaims, which means amazing—Theroux groans at his subpar airplane attempts. He’s not satisfied with good enough, especially now that the stakes have heightened: He’s been handed a thicker sheet that, once it’s inked with 永, will be framed as a gift to take back home to New York after this trip to Japan.

On this particular journey, he’s partnered with the Luxury Collection, a wide portfolio of award-winning hotels and resorts like the Gritti Palace in Venice, or the Royal Hawaiian in Honolulu, to curate experiences like this calligraphy session as a gateway beyond Japan’s typical touristy activities.

“I always feel bad when you see your buses pull up to places, and you see people file out, they take pictures, then they get back on the bus, and they go somewhere else,” he tells Esquire. “Anytime you can get access behind walls to a place—that’s what truly unlocks any travel experience.”

The actor has visited Japan numerous times, and over the course of those visits, has connected deeply with Japanese culture. As a “global explorer” and face of the Luxury Collection Expeditions campaign, Theroux has shaped those life-changing travel experiences into two itineraries.

First, there’s the Global Explorer Edit: Tokyo with Justin Theroux, based out of the five-star Prince Gallery Tokyo Kioicho hotel, which offers panoramic views of Mount Fuji when the weather’s right. If travelers want to branch out beyond Tokyo to Japan’s imperial capital, the Global Explorer Edit: Kyoto with Justin Theroux offers a different perspective of the Land of the Rising Sun. Guests stay at Hotel the Mitsui, a Michelin three-key accommodation, where modern comforts and historic sensibilities collide for a beyond memorable stay.

There’s a noticeable theme to Theroux’s itineraries, visible even in the mundane experience of day-to-day Japan. Everyone here, from the citizen on the street to the service worker to the artist, aims to do their very best, much like Theroux does as he makes his first attempt at 永 on the thicker sheet. The streets are litter-free, despite—or because of—the lack of public garbage cans, and the public transit is perpetually, impeccably, on time.

Though Theroux fails to land the airplane this time, there are a few more sheets lying around, a few more chances to try again—and afterward, a few more stops along his itinerary.

It’s late morning on a partly cloudy September Saturday when Theroux and company—press, PR, friends, guides—leave the Prince Gallery for a speakeasy hidden on the third story of a nondescript building in Tokyo’s Shibuya district. The calligraphy experience awaits inside.

After Theroux decides on his best 永 to frame, Mamimozi, the artist, prepares to perform. She steadies a brush as big as her forearm before making decisive motions, quickly and smoothly, across a sheet the size of a side table. Her movement is entrancing, engrossing—and then, snap, it’s over.

“How does she decide she’s pleased?” Theroux asks Mamimozi’s translator, who passes the question on.

Normally, Mamimozi would do 100 of these before she decides on the best one, the translator relays. This particular character, though? She’s about “92 percent” happy.

“If I ever get to 100 percent,” Mamimozi says through the translator, “that’s the end of my pursuit.” She’s always trying to grow as an artist.

Theroux is presented with his calligraphy in a sturdy frame. For Mamimozi to have taught Theroux from this particular kanji—永, eternity—suddenly makes a whole lot of sense. “I approach everything artistically, whether it’s writing or acting, as a monastic experience where you’re never going to be completely pleased with everything you do—but you’re always striving for something,” he tells Esquire.

The next pursuit? Scoring fashionable vintage wear, of which Tokyo supplies in high volume. Theroux’s Tokyo Edit ensures that travelers are able to take advantage of the spoils of one of the world’s premier fashion markets, focused on four of his favorite shops: Portration, Visvim, Old Joe, and Brackets, all in close proximity in Shibuya. Theroux and co. then attend the Nanzuka contemporary art gallery, also in Shibuya, but travelers should expect a visit on the earlier side of their itinerary.

If shopping makes guests hungry, Theroux’s itinerary recommends Yoroniku, an omakase that serves world-class wagyu. But on this particular trip, Theroux and co. first head to Bar Centifolia, where “samurai bartender” Yuzo Komai puts on a show that’s earned him the title of world’s best cocktail artist.

When an order for a smoked old fashioned is received, the house lights go down, leaving a spotlight on Komai. He unleashes a big blue fan and blows air on a large, perfectly clear ice cube to sweat it just a little before unsheathing a katana and chopping it into a trapezoidal prism, which he slides neatly into a rocks glass. Next he pours in a sweet tincture and Beyoncé’s signature SirDavis whiskey and stirs them together with a cocktail spoon. Then, he places a circular cut of wood atop and lights the whole damn thing aflame with a blowtorch.

A billow of smoke arises from the concoction, which tastes of sweet and smoky caramelized popcorn. For those less adventurous, the bar owns dozens of rare Japanese whiskies, so delicate and floral and complex that they make the perfect nightcap to relax the shoulders after a dizzying, rewarding day.

To begin the Kyoto itinerary, Theroux finds himself sitting cross-legged in the home studio of third-generation lacquerer Nishimura Keikou. His bowls and cups—which go for $650 a pop—begin their lives as a base of wood whittled to a thickness of less than a millimeter before he begins to layer lacquer, a brownish sap that’s extracted from the tree of the same name. It’s a precious resource, “like the blood of a human,” Keikou says through a translator. The trees do not give much, and once depleted, they die. A single piece of lacquerware can take an entire year to craft, as each of the requisite fourteen layers must dry completely before another can be shellacked over top.

Unlike Mamimozi, the calligrapher, Keikou doesn’t consider himself an artist. He doesn’t create because he has an innate need to express; he creates because clients request his goods. Theroux feels a connection nonetheless, especially when Keikou mentions the plight of the woodworkers who make the base of his pieces. They’re behind the camera, so to speak, receiving little glory or credit. Theroux, who is not just an actor but a writer, seems to internalize this as Keikou hands him a bowl as a gift.

Next up on Theroux’s Kyoto itinerary: sushi. His preferred spot, Otachidokoro Sushi Ki, is a stand-up joint where guests can order à la carte or in a set menu. Guests are encouraged to eat with their hands here, which they can rinse between bites using spouts of water that trickle from below a raised edge on the sushi bar.

Theroux’s sushi artisan, befuddlingly, is an Italian immigrant from just outside Milan. How the hell did he get here?

“I came here by plane,” Francesco jokes. Seven years ago, at twenty-seven years old, he committed himself to making sushi at its highest level. Where to go to complete his training? Kyoto, of course, without a return ticket. Now, he’s serving Justin Theroux scallop over soy sauce-soaked rice with a sprinkle of salt and a zest of lime on top.

Bellies full, it’s time for a Zen garden tour. Kyoto is home to many temples and shrines and gardens of both Buddhist and Shinto origin, Japan’s two national religions. But this garden—situated in the Konchi-in Buddhist temple and named, simply, the Crane and Turtle Garden after the shapes of its central rock formations—doesn’t get much foot traffic. It’s more peaceful than the bigger, more popular, tourist-mobbed shrines, ideal for a thoughtful traveler like Theroux.

It’s probably “97 out of 100” in terms of popularity, says tour guide Andrew William. Like the sushi chef from earlier, William is not from Japan. He is from Maryland, not too far from Washington, D.C., where Theroux grew up. Like Francesco, he uprooted himself to perfect his art elsewhere, though instead of making sushi, he designs and builds gardens. He’s worked on big projects in the past, including Ben Stiller’s estate on the north shore of Kauai, Hawaii. William’s pursuit, however, belies the reality of chasing dreams: they often turn to nightmares.

William once wanted to be head groundskeeper of this idyllic garden. But it’s not so easy for foreigners to rise in Japanese hierarchies. He claims to have suffered abuse at the hands of his employers and endured long working hours for low pay—“a lot of bad things to maintain beauty,” he says, still apparently chewing on the irony.

Theroux is no stranger to that feeling. Sometimes, he says of acting, “... you’ll have a miserable experience that’s received very well. And you think, God, I was so miserable on that job. And yet the product was so good, cause so many craftsmen are bringing their talents to bear on it. The editor really helped it, the music really helped it, the director was very good—although they might have been an asshole.”

Theroux and William sit side by side on a broad wooden step that leads into the temple and overlook the amorphous stony shapes of the crane and the turtle. He and Theroux are perched, William says, on what Buddhist philosophy considers the ship of life. Out directly in front of them is an expanse of pebbles raked into a motif of alternating swirls and straight lines, an ocean of respective rough and smooth seas. On the other side, the crane and turtle await, representing the harmony that anticipates us at the end of our earthly journey.

There is still an evening of activities to go: A visit to the temple. Matcha and handmade sweets in the temple’s tearoom. An intimate geisha dinner complete with drinks, cultural conversation, and traditional Japanese food. All of which prove to be enriching experiences for travelers. Yet it is here, facing the crane and turtle, where visitors can meditate on their journey and appreciate the unique experience on which they’ve embarked.

“There’s tourism, and there’s travel,” Theroux says. “And I think they are two totally different things.”

Tourism is hoofing it from place to place, checking boxes. Seen that, did this. But travel, Theroux’s travel, is different. It’s about meeting people, making friends beside the worker at the front desk or the technician at the spa, and engaging with their lives, their realities, on a deeper level.

“Anytime you can have access to something unique or an area of interest—whether that’s craftsmanship, arts, music, dance, knives, enamel ware, things like that—you’re always going to get a little peek into a country or city that you didn’t know existed,” he says.

Like all his endeavors, Theroux’s Japan Expeditions aren’t perfect—if they were, there’d be no reason for visitors to come back—but they’re as close to perfect as it gets.

Both of Justin Theroux’s Tokyo and Kyoto Global Explorer Edits are available for booking on the Luxury Collection Expeditions website. The Tokyo Edit is a day-long experience that costs $9,505, while the Kyoto Edit, also a day-long experience, costs $13,145. That price includes a guest, a one-night hotel stay, a private guide, and transportation.

#6854. The 6 Smartphones to Buy in 2026

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

We don’t care if you’re on team Apple or Android. We’ve found you the phone to upgrade to.

You’ve landed here, so let me guess: You’re an iPhone person and you want a new iPhone. Or ... you’re an Android person, dedicated to your brand of choice already, be it Google or Samsung. Here’s the thing: Your choice of phone says a lot about you, but realistically you’ve already locked yourself into choosing between the same three phones all your friends are getting. We are humans, so our brains are finely tuned to love habits. The result is not too many people want to adjust to a new way of interfacing with daily life every other year; they just want the same thing. There’s a chance you are the rare third kind of person who wants a change. In all three cases, there’s good news. You kinda already know what you want (and don’t want) out of a smartphone.

My fellow editors and I have tried the latest in what Apple, Samsung, and Google have to offer, and have determined for ourselves which are the greatest. This year, the biggest shake-up to the race is foldable phones. Not entirely a new category, but the iPhone Duo is the capstone on a past 12 months that have legitimized the form across both Apple and Android devices. You’ll be paying a premium, and not all the software is properly optimized yet, but the tech is finally there. For everyone else, there’s still the new pro model iPhone or the latest A series Pixel for the value hunters. Regardless of whether you’re after a bold reinvention, or just an improved camera and a slightly shinier display, you’ll find something to love in these six smartphones.

I’ve made vocal arguments in favor of going against the grain and getting a Windows device instead of a MacBook, but at the end of the day it’s all too easy to stick with it if you’re on team iPhone already. The 18 Pro is the iPhone to get if you’re a daily power user, on your phone 24/7 for both work and personal reasons. It’s got a glorious 120-hertz-refresh-rate retina screen, exactly the smoothness and crispness you’ve come to expect from a pro level phone.

It’s not merely the one we’ve seen the most in the wild, it’s the best bet for an iPhone that will last you another four years. And while we can't say we love the new design, it’s been easy to get used to. And functionally, it’s the iPhone Pro we know and love. It is fast, it’s got a smooth 120 hertz screen and it has one of the best cameras you can get on a phone. If there’s one upgrade we truly feel each time we get a new phone, it’s the camera. This year’s is no exception.

Compared with the other Android offerings out there, the Pixel 11 Pro is in elite territory. It’s not technically as beefy as a Samsung Galaxy S26 Ultra or ZFold 8 Ultra, but it’s notably a lot cheaper than either of those phones.

It’s fast and it’s got that smooth refresh rate a high-end phone should. Best of all, holy shit is the camera something else? It can take one incredible photo after another and it’s probably equipped with the best selfie camera on a phone. Our editors who’ve tested this one also won’t shut up about how much better Gemini is than every other AI assistant. Finally, a “smart” feature that lives up to the moniker. And compared to the equivalent iPhones and Samsung phones, the battery life reigns supreme—even if, as a trade-off, it takes the longest to charge.

If you’re of the Android persuasion, it’s easy to see the iPhone 18 Pro as the other team’s Google Pixel 11 Pro, instead of the other way around. On a pure specs level it’s a better phone—just probably not better enough to get Apple diehards over the OS gap. Serious sarcasm aside, it’s not the iPhone users who need this phone. It’s everyone currently suffering on an older Pixel model or, worse, some no-name Android phone.

It was only a couple of years ago you could read me advising you not to buy a renewed iPhone. But with how expensive all technology has become recently, there is now a wide enough gulf between something like this and a new iPhone to make buying refurbished worth the cost savings. Instead of buying a 17e new for $700, pick up this refurbished 16e—it’s half the price, and you won’t be living too far in the past.

The 16e is pretty much everything a phone should be and nothing extraneous. This phone is light, yet durable. It’s got the Action Button from the 16 lineup but does lack the Camera Control.

The other omission of note is the lack of MagSafe functionality. The 16e supports wireless Qi charging, but it does not magnetize the way every iPhone since the 12 (barring the SE line) has. Once again, we don’t think this matters to the core audience for this phone, so it’s less of a flaw and more of a way Apple has honed in on exactly the audience it wants to hit for this one.

For $600, this is the most affordable big-name smartphone. There are plenty of Androids in the $200 to $300 range, but by and large they aren’t meant to last. They are poorly made stopgaps to get you to something like the Pixel 10a. Right now for only $400, this is the best value you can get on a brand-new smartphone.

It has a lot of what we love from the newest Pixel phones—including the latest OS features like Gemini and the invaluable Call Screening feature that automatically detects spam. And while the front-facing camera isn’t mind-blowing compared with the 10 series Pixels, it does still rock a very impressive wide-angle back camera that offers more variation than the e series of iPhones.

The iPhone Duo is the most innovative and groundbreaking phone release of the year. We said as much in our gadget awards and editor Luke Gullory has been giving his updates along the way. He’s tested and tested, and the verdict is in. This one is a keeper and, most importantly, it’s the best foldable phone to hit the market yet.

The genius touch here is the aspect ratio. For the Duo, Apple went with a √2:1 (or 1.414:1) aspect ratio. That is slightly different from the other foldable, but the subtle change makes a difference. On the Duo, the small outside screen exactly mirrors the size of half the large inner screen, but it also exactly mirrors the total aspect ratio of the entire large inner screen. The matte screen seems odd at first, but it’s the solution Apple has found to showing any creasing and, once again, this gives it that design edge. There’s a reason people start to care when Apple does it.

I’ll also note it has the same chipset and specs as the 18 Pro, including RAM. Normally, this would put it on par power-wise, but the real-world performance lags behind due to the amount of thermal throttling needed to pull off the design miracle of the form factor.

If you want a flip phone, you can shop vintage for pennies but when it comes to using those like daily phones, the truth is they don’t support modern cellular networks. They are as good as a toy. But if you still want that flip phone experience, Samsung’s most affordable new folding phone gives you something of the sort.

Don’t get me wrong, the Z Flip8 is still expensive, starting at $1,200. But compared to all the other foldables from Samsung and Google, it is technically the least expensive one. That’s largely because it’s got the least amount of screen space. Really it’s just a normal-sized Galaxy phone with a front-screen, not something that has a unique aspect ratio like the Duo or the Galaxy Z Fold 8 phones. On a specs level, it’s on par with these phones. Crisp 120 hertz screen, solid front and rear cameras, and all the Android app support you’d want. Really, though, if you’re committing it’s a commitment to having a wacky, folding flip phone that you can set up like a tent or show off all the different camera modes.
